  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The review finds that (1) punishment institutions, when adopted, reliably increase both cooperation and group efficiency/payoff in public goods and related games; (2) the effect is strongest when the institution eliminates free-riding incentives and is not too costly; (3) the popularity and effectiveness of punishment institutions increase with repeated choice and learning; (4) endogenously chosen punishment institutions often yield higher efficiency than exogenously imposed ones, especially for weaker institutions, but the aggregate benefit of endogenous choice is ambiguous because some groups fail to implement the institution; (5) institutional cost and incomplete elimination of free-riding are key barriers to adoption and effectiveness; (6) most studies report both behavioral and payoff outcomes, with efficiency and group payoff being central dependent variables.
  prediction_guidance: This review provides strong evidence that, in public goods game-like environments, enabling punishment mechanisms generally increases group efficiency and payoffs, especially when the punishment is effective (eliminates free-riding) and not too costly. The effect is robust across a variety of game designs, player counts, and institutional choice mechanisms. For prediction, the presence of a punishment institution should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, with the magnitude moderated by punishment cost, effectiveness, and whether the institution is adopted. The review also highlights that repeated exposure, information, and learning opportunities increase the likelihood of institution adoption and thus efficiency gains. However, if the institution is costly or does not fully deter free-riding, efficiency gains may be limited or absent. The review's synthesis of many studies with detailed design features makes it highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Kraft-Todd, G. T., Rand, D. G., Nowak, M. A., & Norton, M. (2021). Invisible inequality leads to punishing the poor and rewarding the rich. *Behavioural Public Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated public goods games with unequal endowments, allowing punishment and/or reward, the visibility of income distribution has a strong effect on sanctioning and contribution behavior. When incomes are hidden, participants tend to punish the poor (for low absolute contributions) and reward the rich (for high absolute contributions). When incomes are revealed, this pattern reverses: the poor are rewarded (for high percentage contributions) and the rich are punished (for low percentage contributions). Revealing incomes increases total contributions to the public good and reduces final payoff inequality (lower Gini coefficient). The effect is robust whether incomes are assigned randomly or earned via performance. The paper does not report group efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but the increase in total contributions and reduction in inequality suggest a more positive effect on efficiency when income is revealed and punishment/reward is available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with unequal endowments and punishment/reward options, making income information visible increases total contributions and reduces payoff inequality, especially by shifting sanctioning away from the poor and toward the rich. For prediction tasks, the presence of income transparency (showing others' endowments) is a key moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency-related outcomes. The game design dimensions most directly supported are player count (5), number of rounds (10), presence of punishment and reward, unequal endowments, and the manipulation of information about others' incomes. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment in a context where income is visible will have a more positive effect on efficiency and equality than when income is hidden. However, the paper does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, so the exact magnitude of the effect is not available.

- source: Chugunova, M., Luhan, W. J., & Nicklisch, A. (2020). When to leave carrots for sticks: On the evolution of sanctioning institutions in open communities. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Participants initially choose reward-based public goods communities, which provide higher payoffs and are preferred by most. Over time, as rewards decline and inequality increases in the reward community, some participants migrate to the punishment community. The punishment community achieves higher contribution rates, but lower average payoffs compared to the reward community. Importantly, when punishment and reward communities compete, the punishment community requires less actual punishment to sustain cooperation, reducing welfare losses typically associated with punishment. The threat of punishment, rather than its frequent use, is sufficient to maintain high contributions. However, the reward community still retains a majority of participants and maintains higher average payoffs, though the difference with the punishment community narrows over time.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that the efficiency impact of punishment depends on the institutional context. In isolation, punishment communities sustain high contributions but at a welfare cost due to punishment expenditures. When a reward community is available, migration dynamics mean that the punishment community can sustain cooperation with less actual punishment, reducing welfare losses. Thus, the negative efficiency impact of punishment is mitigated when there is institutional competition. For prediction, this implies that the efficiency of punishment-enabled games may be higher when players can choose between institutions, and that the mere threat of punishment can be effective if migration is possible. However, in direct comparison, reward communities yield higher payoffs than punishment communities, though the gap narrows over time.

- source: Hou, G. L., Wang, F., Shi, J. Y., Chen, W. J., & Yu, J. (2019). Which is the ideal sanction for cooperation? An experimental study on different types of third-party sanctions. *Psych Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Third-party punishment and the combination of third-party punishment and reward both significantly increase the average level of cooperation in a public goods game compared to a baseline with no sanctions. Third-party reward alone has little effect on cooperation. The combination of punishment and reward (TP/R) leads to the highest average cooperation, though the difference with punishment alone is not statistically significant. Both punishment and TP/R treatments maintain high cooperation throughout the game, while cooperation declines in the baseline and reward-only treatments. In terms of payoffs, third-party interventions (especially punishment and TP/R) change the earnings of PGG-players, generally improving the alignment between contribution and payoff. The paper provides evidence that third-party sanctions, especially those involving punishment, can increase group earnings and improve the outcome of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling third-party punishment in a standard PGG increases group earnings and cooperation compared to a baseline with no sanctions. The effect is even more pronounced when both punishment and reward are available, suggesting a complementary effect. The game design dimensions (e.g., player count, number of rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech) are clearly specified, making the results directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in similar lab PGGs. The findings support the prediction that, holding control efficiency constant, enabling punishment (especially with a 3:1 punishment tech and moderate cost) will increase efficiency. Reward alone is much less effective. The results are most applicable to lab PGGs with 4 players, 10 rounds, no chat, and full information about others' contributions and payoffs.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., & Guérin, C. (2021). Decoupling cooperation and punishment in humans shows that punishment is not an altruistic trait. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that in a standard repeated public goods game with punishment, contributions remain stable when all can punish and be punished. However, when some individuals are made immune to punishment, these immune individuals (even those classified as conditional cooperators) learn to contribute less over time, despite being able to punish others and being surrounded by high contributors. Punishment is frequent but often hypocritical or anti-social among immune individuals, and overall, the costs of punishment are greater than the benefits from increased contributions, making punishment destructive to group welfare. The results indicate that punishment does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff, and that the link between cooperation and punishment is not altruistic but may be driven by confusion and learning.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be destructive: the costs of punishment can outweigh any gains from increased cooperation, leading to lower overall efficiency. The design closely matches standard PGGs with linear returns, four players, repeated rounds, and costly punishment (1 MU cost to reduce 3 MU from another). The paper does not report efficiency directly, but states that punishment is 'ultimately destructive' in terms of group welfare. Therefore, when using game design dimensions similar to this study (small groups, linear PGG, costly punishment, no communication, anonymous punishment), one should expect that enabling punishment may not increase, and may even decrease, group efficiency relative to the control. The findings are especially relevant for settings where punishment is frequent and costly, and where the marginal per capita return is less than one.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Malaeb, B., Vlassopoulos, M., & Wahba, J. (2021). Cooperation in a fragmented society: Experimental evidence on Syrian refugees and natives in Lebanon. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that: (1) Homogeneous groups (same nationality) contribute more to the public good than mixed groups, and this is mainly due to Lebanese participants reducing contributions in mixed groups. (2) Punishment is used more in homogeneous groups, and there is substantial antisocial punishment, especially among Lebanese. (3) The opportunity to punish does not increase contributions overall; in fact, average contributions decrease in the punishment stage for Syrians and do not change significantly for Lebanese or mixed groups. (4) Average earnings after the contribution stage are higher in homogeneous groups, but total earnings (including punishment costs) are not significantly different between treatments, as mixed groups assign less punishment and thus incur lower punishment costs. (5) The presence of punishment does not remedy the lower cooperation in mixed groups. (6) Behavior in mixed groups is more conditionally cooperative, while homogeneous groups show more unconditional cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the effect of punishment in a two-player repeated linear public goods game with a 0.75 MPCR and a 1:3 punishment cost-to-impact ratio. The main finding is that the introduction of punishment does not increase efficiency or average earnings; in fact, contributions do not rise and may even fall, especially due to antisocial punishment. While punishment increases costs (especially in homogeneous groups), it does not lead to higher total payoffs. Thus, for similar game designs (small groups, moderate MPCR, punishment with 1:3 cost-impact, no communication), one should not expect punishment to increase efficiency, especially in settings with potential for antisocial punishment. The paper also highlights that group composition (in-group vs. out-group) can moderate both cooperation and punishment, but the overall effect of punishment on efficiency is neutral or negative in this context.

- source: DeAngelo, G., & Gee, L. K. (2020). Peers or police?: The effect of choice and type of monitoring in the provision of public goods. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This laboratory experiment uses a 2x2 design to compare exogenous vs. endogenous monitoring and peer vs. group monitoring in a linear public goods game with punishment. When monitoring is exogenously imposed, both peer and group monitoring yield similar public good provision and net earnings. When monitoring is endogenously chosen, group monitoring collapses (due to free riders refusing to pay for monitoring), leading to a 44% drop in public good provision, but net earnings are not significantly lower because less is spent on punishment. Peer monitoring, however, is robust to endogenization: public good provision and net earnings remain high, and willingness-to-pay for monitoring is not strongly correlated with contribution level. The results are robust to changes in the punishment mechanism and the voting rule for group monitoring. The key implication is that the effectiveness of punishment in improving efficiency depends on the monitoring regime and whether monitoring is exogenous or endogenous.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the impact depends on the monitoring regime and whether monitoring is exogenously imposed or endogenously chosen. If monitoring is exogenous (automatic), punishment increases or maintains efficiency (via higher public good provision) regardless of whether monitoring is peer-based or group-based. If monitoring is endogenous and requires group consensus, punishment may fail to improve efficiency because monitoring is not implemented (especially in the presence of free riders), leading to low public good provision but not necessarily lower net earnings due to reduced punishment costs. Peer monitoring is robust to endogenization: punishment maintains efficiency even when monitoring is endogenous. Thus, when using game design dimensions to predict treatment efficiency, it is crucial to account for whether monitoring is exogenous or endogenous and the type of monitoring (peer vs. group). Control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the paper provides sufficient data to estimate the relative effect of punishment under different monitoring regimes.

- source: Lefebvre, M., & Stenger, A. (2020). Short- & long-term effects of monetary and non-monetary incentives to cooperate in public good games: An experiment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Monetary and non-monetary punishments and rewards all significantly increase contributions to the public good compared to baseline. Monetary punishment and non-monetary reward are the most effective. After incentives are removed, contributions remain higher than baseline for several rounds, indicating some long-lasting effect, but eventually decline. The effect of punishment and reward on later contributions depends on whether individuals were high or low contributors and how many points they received. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the findings are limited to contribution behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (especially monetary punishment) increases contribution rates in a standard linear public goods game with four players and 30 rounds. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are highly informative for predicting contribution behavior, but less so for efficiency unless one assumes a direct mapping from contribution to efficiency (which is not always valid, especially when punishment is costly). The design dimensions are well specified, so the paper is useful for understanding the behavioral response to punishment and reward mechanisms in similar lab PGGs.

- source: Yang, X. Q., Zhang, F., Wang, W. X., Zhang, D., Shi, Z. H., & Zhou, S. W. (2020). Effect of habitat destruction on cooperation in public goods games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing a weak, automatic punishment institution (where the lowest contributor in a group loses 1 yuan per round) does not significantly increase contributions compared to a control PGG or to a reward institution. There is no significant effect of matching or mismatching subjects' institutional preferences (punishment vs reward) with the institution they are assigned to. Subjects who prefer punishment tend to be lower contributors and have social preferences associated with anti-social or efficiency-reducing profiles. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a main outcome, and the punishment institution is designed to be strictly efficiency-reducing at the group level.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a repeated linear PGG with weak, automatic, centralized punishment (punishment cost = 1, punishment tech = 1, imposed on lowest contributor), enabling punishment does not increase contributions or efficiency compared to a control. The punishment is non-deterrent and strictly efficiency-reducing by design. Therefore, in similar game designs (weak, automatic, centralized punishment), one should not expect punishment to improve efficiency over the control. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so the evidence is indirect and based on contribution rates and the institutional design. The findings are most relevant for settings with weak, automatic, non-peer punishment and may not generalize to peer punishment or stronger punishment regimes.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & van Dijk, E. (2019). The effect of decision timing on the willingness to costly reward cooperation and punish noncooperation: Sanctioning the past, the present, or the future. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main finding is that people are less willing to assign costly rewards or punishments when they must decide before observing others' (non)cooperation, compared to after. This effect is robust across both dichotomous and continuous public goods game variants, and for both reward and punishment, though the effect is stronger for punishment. The effect is not explained by differences in emotional reactions. The results are interpreted as evidence for nonconsequential reasoning: uncertainty about future behavior reduces decisiveness and willingness to sanction. The study does not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the willingness to use costly punishment (and reward) in public goods games is lower when sanctions must be decided before observing others' behavior, compared to after. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the timing of sanctioning opportunities (ex ante vs. ex post) is a critical moderator of the behavioral use of punishment, which could in turn affect efficiency if actual group payoffs were measured. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot directly inform predictions of efficiency changes due to punishment. The findings are most useful for understanding when punishment is likely to be used, not its payoff consequences.

- source: Chapkovski, P. (2021). Strike one hundred to educate one: Measuring the efficacy of collective sanctions experimentally. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Collective sanctions are not effective at increasing cooperation or contributions in a public good game, and may even reduce them compared to individual sanctions, especially when peer punishment is available. The presence of peer punishment increases contributions overall, but under collective sanctions, contributions are significantly lower than under individual sanctions with peer punishment. Average earnings are slightly lower under collective sanctions, but this difference is not statistically significant. There are notable gender differences: men contribute more under collective sanctions, women less. Perceptions of fairness mediate these effects, with collective sanctions seen as less fair, especially by women.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard PGG with 3 players, 15 rounds, continuous contributions, and a fixed threshold for external punishment, enabling collective sanctions (punishing the whole group for one member's under-contribution) does not improve efficiency or group payoff compared to individual sanctions, and may even reduce cooperation and contributions, especially when peer punishment is also available. The presence of peer punishment increases contributions, but collective sanctions reduce this effect. The paper does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average earnings are slightly lower under collective sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that switching from individual to collective sanctions is unlikely to increase efficiency, and may decrease it, particularly in small groups with peer punishment. Gender composition may moderate effects.

- source: Clark, J., & Dickinson, D. L. (2020). The effect of sleep on public good contributions and punishment: Experimental evidence. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, in a standard VCM with 3-person groups and 10 rounds per treatment, the introduction of peer punishment increases contributions for sleep-restricted subjects but not for well-rested subjects. Without punishment, contributions do not differ by sleep condition. There is no significant difference in punishment behavior between sleep-restricted and well-rested subjects. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are robust to various compliance and attrition corrections, but some results are sensitive to the sleep deprivation measure used.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the effect of punishment on contribution behavior in a standard VCM, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task, the paper confirms that, in a 3-player, 10-round VCM with standard punishment (linear cost, known identities), the introduction of punishment increases contributions, especially for sleep-restricted subjects. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed, the paper cannot directly inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment. The design dimensions are well specified, and the behavioral results are likely to correlate with efficiency, but the lack of direct payoff analysis is a limitation.

- source: Harrell, A. (2018). Competition for Leadership Promotes Contributions to Collective Action. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the opportunity to compete for an elected leadership position (with punishment power) increases contributions to the public good before the leader is chosen, but only when voters can observe and use prior contribution behavior in their decision. After the leader is installed, groups with elected leaders maintain higher contributions than those with appointed leaders. The leader's use of punishment does not differ significantly between elected and appointed conditions. The main outcome is contribution rate; no explicit group payoff or efficiency measures are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the process of leader selection (election vs. appointment) and the visibility of contribution history can significantly affect contribution rates in public goods games with punishment. However, because the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes due to punishment. Its findings are most useful for predicting how leader selection mechanisms and information about contributions affect cooperation, which may indirectly inform efficiency predictions if contribution rates are assumed to map closely to efficiency in similar designs.

- source: Langenbach, P., & Tausch, F. (2019). Inherited Institutions: Cooperation in the Light of Democratic Legitimacy. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment uses a one-shot, three-player linear public goods game with or without a centralized, non-deterrent punishment (automatic fine for less-than-full contribution). The institutional setting (punishment or not) is determined either by majority vote (Endo) or random draw (Exo) in the first generation, and this setting is inherited by a matched group in the second generation. In the first generation, contributions are higher when punishment is democratically adopted and lower when it is democratically rejected, compared to random assignment. In the second generation, the positive effect of democratic adoption disappears, but the negative effect of democratic rejection persists (marginally). The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff. The paper does not report efficiency or total earnings as a share of the social optimum, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible from this paper alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a centralized, non-deterrent punishment mechanism can increase contributions if it is democratically adopted, but only in the generation that participates in the decision. The effect does not persist to subsequent generations who inherit the institution. The negative effect of democratic rejection may persist. However, since the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, only contribution rates, its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. The game design dimensions are well specified, so the paper is informative for mapping design to expected contribution behavior, but not for direct efficiency prediction. If contribution is used as a proxy for efficiency (assuming linearity and no punishment cost to the group), the results suggest that democratic adoption of punishment increases efficiency in the first generation, but not in the second.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2020). Heterogeneous groups cooperate in public good problems despite normative disagreements about individual contribution levels. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, in a linear public goods game with peer punishment and heterogeneous returns, experimentally induced normative disagreement within groups does not reduce cooperation (measured by average contributions) or increase punishment. Instead, the average normative view within a group predicts the group's contribution level. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but notes that joint payoffs are maximized at full contribution, and that observed contribution levels are lower than this maximum, consistent with underprovision. The presence of punishment maintains cooperation at moderate levels, but does not eliminate the effect of group-mean norms. The study does not include a no-punishment control, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the effect of normative disagreement in a linear PGG with peer punishment, but does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, nor does it include a no-punishment control. For prediction tasks, it shows that, in a 3-player, 10-round, continuous-contribution PGG with punishment (cost 1, impact 3), with full information about others' contributions and payoffs but anonymous punishment, group-mean normative views are a strong predictor of contribution levels. The presence of punishment maintains moderate cooperation, but the paper does not allow direct estimation of the efficiency gain from enabling punishment versus disabling it. The results suggest that, under these parameters, punishment supports cooperation even in the presence of normative disagreement, but the efficiency effect relative to no-punishment is not measured.

- source: Bond, R. M. (2019). Low-cost, high-impact altruistic punishment promotes cooperation cascades in human social networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games, the presence and structure of punishment (especially low-cost, high-impact punishment) increases individual contributions and the persistence and spread of cooperative behavior through the network. The effect of one player's contribution on another's is stronger and endures longer in the low-cost, high-impact punishment condition. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral measures (contribution and its network effects).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that low-cost, high-impact punishment increases individual contributions and the spread of cooperation in public goods games, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that punishment (especially when low-cost and high-impact) is likely to increase efficiency if higher contributions translate to higher group payoffs, but this is an inference rather than a direct finding. The paper is most useful for understanding how punishment design affects contribution behavior and its propagation, rather than direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Molleman, L., Kölle, F., Starmer, C., & Gächter, S. (2019). People prefer coordinated punishment in cooperative interactions. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that in a one-shot, three-player binary public goods game with a costly punishment stage, a substantial fraction of participants prefer to punish free riders only if another group member also punishes (coordinated punishment). This preference is especially prevalent among those who do not punish unconditionally. The presence of coordinated punishment is robust across two large samples and is not correlated with conditional cooperation preferences. The study provides detailed behavioral evidence on how punishment decisions are conditioned on others' actions, but does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the behavioral mechanisms of punishment in public goods games, especially the prevalence and motivations for coordinated punishment. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, nor does it compare efficiency between punishment-enabled and punishment-disabled conditions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the structure of punishment (e.g., whether it is coordinated) may matter, but does not provide quantitative or comparative efficiency data. The game design dimensions are well specified, which may help in mapping to other studies that do report efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kanitsar, G. (2019). Solidarity through punishment: An experiment on the merits of centralized enforcement in generalized exchange. *Social Science Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Centralized punishment substantially increases the frequency of cooperation in both public good and generalized exchange games. However, the main focus is on how punishment affects solidarity, not efficiency. Solidarity (measured affectively, behaviorally, and via beliefs) increases with punishment in generalized exchange but not in the public good setting. The positive effect of punishment on solidarity is largely mediated by increased cooperation frequency. The experiment does not report group efficiency or total payoff, and the only payoff-related outcomes are cooperation rates and within-group payoff variation. Thus, the findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that centralized punishment increases cooperation rates in both public good and generalized exchange games with four players and 16 rounds. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, binary choice, etc.) are well specified, and the manipulation of punishment is clear (centralized, probabilistic, fixed fine). The main value for efficiency prediction is the robust increase in cooperation frequency with punishment, which is typically associated with higher efficiency in standard PGGs, but the absence of direct efficiency or payoff analysis limits the strength of inference.

- source: Mischkowski, D., Glöckner, A., & Lewisch, P. (2018). From spontaneous cooperation to spontaneous punishment - Distinguishing the underlying motives driving spontaneous behavior in first and second order public good games. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in both one-shot and iterated public goods games with a punishment option, individuals who decide to punish quickly invest more in punishment, especially if they contributed more than their peers and feel highly upset. The spontaneous punishment effect is not moderated by dispositional pro-sociality (SVO), but by situational factors (above-average contribution and negative affect). The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, so it does not provide evidence on whether punishment increases or decreases efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed evidence on the psychological and situational determinants of punishment behavior in public goods games, but does not report or analyze efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Therefore, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. However, it does confirm that punishment is used more by above-average contributors and those experiencing negative affect, and that the cost-to-impact ratio of punishment is 1:4. The game design is a standard PGG with continuous contributions, four players, two rounds (Study 1) or one round (Study 2), no chat, and full information about others' contributions. The absence of efficiency or payoff analysis limits the paper's direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Albrecht, F., Kube, S., & Traxler, C. (2018). Cooperation and norm enforcement - The individual-level perspective. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds substantial heterogeneity in both cooperation and punishment patterns. About half of subjects are pro-social punishers, and about 40% are non-punishers. There is only a moderate correlation between cooperation and punishment types: many conditional cooperators do not punish, and some free-riders do punish pro-socially. In repeated public goods games with punishment, groups with more pro-social punishers achieve higher and more stable contribution levels, even after controlling for the number of conditional cooperators. Regression analyses show that the number of pro-social punishers in a group is a stronger predictor of group contribution levels than the number of conditional cooperators. However, the paper does not report group payoffs, efficiency, or welfare, so the effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in linear public goods games with punishment, the presence of pro-social punishers in a group increases contribution levels. This suggests that enabling punishment can increase cooperation, but the paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoffs. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is informative about behavioral mechanisms (contributions and punishment) but not about the net payoff effects of punishment. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the experimental setup matches the canonical PGG with punishment, so the findings are highly relevant for understanding how group composition and punishment opportunities affect cooperation rates, but not directly efficiency.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2021). Cooperation between newcomers and incumbents: The role of normative disagreements. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Normative disagreement between newcomers and incumbents in a public goods game with peer punishment does not reduce average contributions to the public good. While group identification and the emergence of a shared social norm are negatively affected by normative disagreement, cooperation (as measured by contribution rate) is maintained. Punishment is used as a norm enforcement tool, especially targeting low-contributing newcomers, who subsequently increase their contributions to match incumbent standards. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a 3-player repeated public goods game with peer punishment, normative disagreement does not reduce average contributions, and punishment is effective at norm enforcement. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and findings are relevant for understanding behavioral mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. The game design dimensions are well specified, so the paper can inform the mapping from design to expected contribution rates, but not to efficiency.

- source: Engel, C., Kube, S., & Kurschilgen, M. (2021). Managing expectations: How selective information affects cooperation and punishment in social dilemma games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Selective pre-play information about others' cooperativeness can strongly affect initial beliefs, contribution behavior, and the effectiveness of punishment in repeated public goods games with punishment and counter-punishment. Unfavorable information (showing low cooperation in other groups) leads to lower initial and sustained contributions, and reduces the ability of punishment to increase cooperation. Favorable information has little effect in already cooperative environments (ceiling effect), but in less cooperative environments it can prevent further decline in cooperation and enhance the positive effect of pro-social punishment. The effect of information does not depend on whether it is public or private, but is strongest when the information is surprising to participants. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the strong effects on contribution rates imply likely effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing cooperation (and thus likely efficiency) is moderated by participants' expectations, which can be manipulated by selective pre-play information. In environments where baseline cooperation is high, punishment is effective unless unfavorable information is provided, which undermines both cooperation and the effectiveness of punishment. In less cooperative environments, favorable information can help maintain cooperation and enhance the effect of pro-social punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the baseline expectations and informational context (not just structural game parameters) are important moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, predictions must be inferred from contribution data.

- source: Gallier, C. (2020). Democracy and compliance in public goods games. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a non-deterrent contribution rule in a public goods game increases contributions, regardless of whether it is imposed exogenously or chosen democratically. The aggregate increase in contributions is primarily due to the presence of the rule itself, not the democratic process. Democratic participation (voting) does not have a statistically significant effect on contributions at the aggregate level after accounting for the direct effect of the rule, self-selection, and information effects. At the individual level, participants who are overruled in the group vote tend to reduce their contributions, but this negative effect is counteracted by a small democracy premium if the rule is democratically chosen. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a linear public goods game with a mild, non-deterrent automatic sanction, the introduction of the rule (punishment) increases contributions, but the effect is not due to democratic participation per se. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing a weak punishment mechanism on contribution rates, but not directly on efficiency or group payoff, as these are not reported. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, continuous contribution, etc.) are clearly specified and can inform parameterization of prediction models. However, since efficiency is not reported, the paper's quantitative results cannot be directly used for efficiency prediction, but the qualitative pattern (punishment increases cooperation even when non-deterrent) is robust.

- source: Schlösser, T., Berger, S., & Fetchenhauer, D. (2018). Justice Sensitivity and Cooperation Dynamics in Repeated Public Good Games. *Social Justice Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that allowing costly punishment in a repeated public good game leads to a substantial increase in average contributions (from 9.18 to 18.34 tokens per round out of 20). This replicates the classic result that punishment options strongly promote cooperation. The effect of individual justice sensitivity on cooperation is strong in the absence of punishment but disappears when punishment is available, as cooperation becomes strategic to avoid sanctions. Punishment is used infrequently, but its mere availability is sufficient to sustain high cooperation. Although efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the high contribution rates in the punishment condition imply much higher group efficiency compared to the no-punishment condition.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a standard repeated public good game with four players, ten rounds, and a punishment technology similar to Fehr & Gächter (2002) leads to a large increase in group efficiency, as measured by average contributions. The control (no-punishment) condition yields about 46% of maximum possible contributions, while the punishment condition yields about 92%. This suggests that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will approximately double efficiency relative to the control. The paper's design and results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated linear PGGs with similar parameters.

- source: Pleasant, A., & Barclay, P. (2018). Why Hate the Good Guy? Antisocial Punishment of High Cooperators Is Greater When People Compete To Be Chosen. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that when participants in a public goods game with punishment are competing to be chosen for a subsequent trust game, antisocial punishment (punishing high contributors) is much higher than in a standard PGG with punishment. This effect is present from the first round and persists across rounds. In the control condition, contributions increase over rounds (as is typical when punishment is available), but in the markets condition, contributions do not increase, suggesting that antisocial punishment suppresses the escalation of cooperation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral pattern implies that the efficiency gains typically associated with punishment may be undermined or reversed in contexts with high antisocial punishment driven by partner competition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the context of partner competition (biological markets) can substantially increase antisocial punishment in public goods games with punishment, which in turn suppresses the usual increase in cooperation seen with punishment. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the findings imply that in environments where partner choice or competitive altruism is salient, enabling punishment may not improve and may even harm group efficiency, as antisocial punishment undermines cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of partner competition or similar social comparison incentives is a critical moderator: in such settings, the efficiency benefit of punishment is likely reduced or reversed, even if control efficiency is known. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Engel, C. (2019). When Does Transparency Backfire? Putting Jeremy Bentham's Theory of General Prevention to the Experimental Test. *Journal of Empirical Legal Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment manipulates the level of feedback about punishment and contributions in a standard linear public goods game with centralized punishment. Three feedback treatments (low, medium, high) vary the precision of information about others' contributions and punishment. The main finding is that full transparency (high treatment: individual-level information about both contributions and punishment) leads to significantly lower contributions than aggregate or less precise feedback. Information about punishment of others does not increase cooperation; instead, it can reduce it. Participants are more responsive to descriptive norms (others' contributions) than to injunctive signals (punishment). The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff as a dependent variable, but reports total earnings descriptively.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a linear public goods game with centralized punishment, increasing transparency about individual punishment and contributions can reduce cooperation (contributions), contrary to standard deterrence theory. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment with high transparency may not increase efficiency and could even reduce it, especially if the control (no punishment) game already has moderate contributions. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed, the findings should be interpreted as applying primarily to contribution behavior. The design dimensions most relevant here are the feedback structure (show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id), centralized punishment, and the linear PGG structure.

- source: Weber, T. O., Weisel, O., & Gächter, S. (2018). Dispositional free riders do not free ride on punishment. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that the availability of punishment leads dispositional free riders (DFR) to increase their contributions to the public good to levels similar to dispositional conditional cooperators (DCC). Both DFR and DCC are equally likely to punish non-cooperators, and their punishment expenditures and motives are similar, regardless of whether punishment reduces payoff inequality (3:1 ratio) or not (1:1 ratio). The presence of punishment thus increases cooperation among DFR, but the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, focusing instead on behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a one-shot public goods game increases contributions among individuals who would otherwise free ride, and that both free riders and conditional cooperators are willing to punish non-cooperators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does, however, support the expectation that punishment increases cooperation even among those predisposed to free ride, which is likely to translate into higher efficiency in similar game designs. The lack of direct efficiency data is a limitation for quantitative prediction.

- source: Kirchkamp, O., & Mill, W. (2020). Conditional cooperation and the effect of punishment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three treatments: a baseline public goods game (no punishment), a treatment where the conditional decision maker (CDM) can be punished by an unconditional decision maker (UCPun), and a treatment where the CDM can punish an unconditional decision maker (CPun). The threat of punishment (UCPun) increases the slope of conditional cooperation but reduces the probability of being a conditional cooperator (i.e., more free riders). The power to punish (CPun) does not reduce conditional cooperation (no substitution effect on cooperation), and actually increases the slope of conditional cooperation due to a responsibility effect. There is evidence for substitution between punishment and cooperation in punishment behavior, but not in contribution behavior. The overall effect of punishment is to increase conditional cooperation, but with some increase in free riding. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a one-shot public goods game increases the slope of conditional cooperation, but also increases the number of free riders. The net effect on cooperation is positive, but the effect on efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, but may have ambiguous effects on efficiency if the increase in free riding offsets the gains from stronger conditional cooperation. The design features—such as costless punishment, no communication, and a one-shot game—should be considered when generalizing to other settings. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff analysis limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Liu, J., Riyanto, Y. E., & Zhang, R. K. (2020). Firing the right bullets: Exploring the effectiveness of the hired-gun mechanism in the provision of public goods. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The hired-gun mechanism, a centralized punishment system targeting the lowest contributor(s), is highly effective at increasing contributions in public goods games when both unilateral and tie punishments are positive. The effect is robust to increasing the choice set (larger endowment). However, if the unilateral punishment is removed (set to zero), contributions decay over time, and the mechanism fails to sustain high cooperation, even if the tie punishment remains. Theoretical predictions suggest a range of punishment parameters should work, but empirically, zero unilateral punishment is insufficient. The main outcome is contribution rate, not direct efficiency or group payoff, but high contributions imply high efficiency in this context. The study also finds that punishment can be softened (reduced in size) to a degree without loss of effectiveness, but not eliminated.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks relating to efficiency in public goods games with centralized punishment, this paper provides strong evidence that the hired-gun mechanism (centralized, automatic punishment of the lowest contributor) is highly effective at increasing contributions (and thus likely efficiency) when both unilateral and tie punishments are positive. The effect is robust to larger choice sets and group size of 4. However, if the unilateral punishment is zero, the mechanism is ineffective, and contributions decay. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the near-full-contribution rates in the treatment with punishment strongly suggest near-maximal efficiency. Thus, when mapping from game design to expected efficiency, the presence and size of both punishment components are critical moderators. The absence of unilateral punishment is a strong negative moderator. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with centralized, automatic punishment and group size 4, with full information about others' contributions and payoffs.

- source: Fraser, S., & Nettle, D. (2020). Hunger Affects Social Decisions in a Multi-Round Public Goods Game but Not a Single-Shot Ultimatum Game. *Adaptive Human Behavior and Physiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 20-round PGG (10 rounds without punishment, 10 with punishment), groups of four played with either all members having eaten breakfast or all having skipped breakfast. In the no-punishment phase, hungry participants contributed more initially and were more likely to match their contributions to those of their groupmates in subsequent rounds. In the punishment phase, hungry participants punished less, especially when group contributions were high, making punishment less effective at boosting contributions. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative behavioral evidence about how punishment and hunger interact to affect contribution and punishment behavior in a standard PGG. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment is less effective at increasing contributions when participants are hungry, which may imply a smaller efficiency gain from punishment in such contexts, but this is not quantified in payoff terms. The game design dimensions are well specified and match standard PGG with punishment, so the behavioral patterns may inform models that link contribution and punishment behavior to efficiency, but direct efficiency data are lacking.

- source: Krawczyk, M., & Szczygielski, K. (2019). Do professions curb free-riding? An experiment. *European Journal of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that allowing peer punishment in a standard linear PGG leads to typical patterns: punishment is directed at low contributors and can increase subsequent contributions. However, the main treatment—group homogeneity—does not significantly affect investment or punishment. Homogeneous groups (same profession) do not cooperate more; if anything, they may cooperate less. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that, in this design, punishment works similarly regardless of group composition, and that group homogeneity does not enhance cooperation when punishment is available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a clear example of a standard PGG with punishment, but does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it confirms that punishment is implemented in a typical way (costly to punisher, more costly to target, anonymous, with summary feedback), and that the presence of punishment supports cooperation (as in prior literature). The lack of efficiency data limits its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes, but the behavioral results are consistent with the literature showing that punishment can sustain cooperation. The design details (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, etc.) are well specified and can inform parameter mapping for prediction tasks.

- source: Wu, J. H., Balliet, D., Peperkoorn, L. S., Romano, A., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2020). Cooperation in Groups of Different Sizes: The Effects of Punishment and Reputation-Based Partner Choice. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both punishment and reputation-based partner choice significantly increase the rate of full cooperation in one-shot public goods games, regardless of group size (4, 20, or 40). The effect of punishment on cooperation is positive and robust, but there is no evidence that its effectiveness depends on group size. The main outcome is the binary cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not report on efficiency, welfare, or total group earnings, so its findings are adjacent to the prediction task, which requires payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment increases cooperation rates in one-shot public goods games across a range of group sizes (4, 20, 40), with a fixed marginal group return (MGR = 1.6). However, because the outcome is cooperation rate (all-or-nothing contribution), not efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency. The design dimensions are well specified (player count, all-or-nothing, no chat, fixed MGR, known group size, etc.), so the paper is useful for understanding how punishment affects cooperation rates under these conditions, but not for directly predicting efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum.

- source: Chang, H. I., Dawes, C. T., & Johnson, T. (2018). POLITICAL INEQUALITY, CENTRALIZED SANCTIONING INSTITUTIONS, AND THE MAINTENANCE OF PUBLIC GOODS. *Bulletin of Economic Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing a centralized sanctioning authority (who can punish low contributors at a cost) increases contributions to the public good compared to no authority. However, electing the authority (democratically) does not further increase contributions compared to random selection. When voting power is distributed unequally, contributions only decrease if the inequality is based on prior economic performance (earnings in the PGG), not if it is randomly assigned. The negative effect of wealth-based political inequality appears to operate through reduced perceived legitimacy of the institution. Average earnings are reported by condition, but efficiency is not directly analyzed as a main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a centralized punishment institution increases contributions (and likely efficiency) relative to no punishment, but does not support the claim that democratic selection of the punisher further increases efficiency. The effect of punishment on efficiency is likely positive in this design, but the paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff as the main outcome. The findings suggest that the way punishment institutions are selected (random vs. democratic) may not matter for efficiency, unless political inequality is tied to economic wealth, which can reduce contributions. For prediction, the presence of centralized punishment should be coded as increasing efficiency, but the selection mechanism (random vs. democratic) should not be expected to matter unless wealth-based voting is used.

- source: Morgan, S. N., Mason, N. M., & Shupp, R. S. (2019). The effects of voice with(out) punishment: Public goods provision and rule compliance. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that: (1) Comments alone do not significantly increase contributions or compliance with the MCR compared to control. (2) Enforcement (punishment) alone increases contributions and compliance, but the effect is significantly larger when comments are combined with enforcement. (3) The comment with enforcement treatment leads to the highest and most sustained contributions and compliance. (4) Rule-makers exposed to comments set higher MCRs, which in turn increases contributions. (5) The effect of comments on contributions is partly mediated by the higher MCRs set by rule-makers. (6) The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the increased contributions and compliance in the comment with enforcement treatment imply higher efficiency in that condition.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that in a linear public goods game with a probabilistic punishment mechanism, adding a comment-based participation mechanism (private, one-way comments to a rule-maker who sets a minimum contribution rule) increases contributions and compliance above punishment alone. While efficiency is not directly measured, the structure of the game (MPCR = 0.4, group size 5, continuous contributions, no chat, no rewards) means that higher contributions and compliance will translate into higher efficiency. The effect of punishment alone is positive but smaller than the effect of punishment plus comments. The paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency, so predictions should be based on the observed contribution and compliance patterns. The findings are most relevant for games with third-party rule-making, probabilistic exogenous punishment, and no peer-to-peer punishment or reward.

- source: Farrow, K., & Romaniuc, R. (2019). The stickiness of norms. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both peer disapproval and face-saving mechanisms increase contributions relative to baseline. When a temporary monetary sanction is introduced and then removed, peer disapproval groups experience a significant drop in cooperation (contributions fall below baseline), indicating a negative post-intervention spillover. In contrast, face-saving groups maintain or even increase cooperation after the sanction is removed, showing no negative spillover. The results suggest that the type of informal norm enforcement moderates the legacy of formal sanctions, with face-saving mechanisms supporting more robust cooperation after sanctions end.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) depends on the type of informal norm enforcement present. While the study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the contribution data imply that punishment can have negative, neutral, or positive legacy effects depending on whether peer disapproval or face-saving is salient. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and type of informal norm enforcement (especially observability/social image vs. anonymous peer disapproval) are important moderators of punishment's effect. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the findings should be interpreted as applying primarily to contribution rates.

- source: Lindström, B., Jangard, S., Selbing, I., & Olsson, A. (2018). The Role of a Common Is Moral Heuristic in the Stability and Change of Moral Norms. *Journal of Experimental Psychology-general*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across nine experiments, participants observing one-shot PGGs judged common behaviors (whether altruistic or selfish) as more moral and less deserving of punishment than rare behaviors. This effect was robust across lab and online samples, and held for both preexisting moral norms (cooperation/selfishness) and arbitrary behaviors. In one experiment, participants could spend real money to punish, and commonness reduced costly punishment of selfishness. The effect of commonness was explained by a formal model of social influence (Social Impact Theory). Agent-based simulations showed that the CIM heuristic can generate both stability and sudden change in group-level moral norms. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the perceived commonness of behaviors in PGGs shapes moral and punishment judgments, and that common selfishness is punished less than rare selfishness. It also shows that costly punishment is reduced when selfishness is common. However, because the experiments are one-shot and do not measure group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, the findings do not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. The results are most useful for understanding psychological and behavioral responses to norm violation and punishment, rather than for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions in repeated or strategic PGGs.

- source: Windmann, S., Binder, L., & Schultze, M. (2021). Constructing the Facets of Altruistic Behaviors (FAB) Scale. *Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The FAB scale reliably measures three distinct facets of altruistic behavior, including peer punishment. In a pilot study, the peer punishment subscale correlates with the amount participants spend on punishing free riders in a public goods game with punishment. The public goods game used is a standard design (groups of four, endowment, MPCR=0.75, punishment with 1:3 fee-to-fine ratio), but the only behavioral outcome reported is the amount spent on punishment, not group efficiency or total earnings. The study provides evidence that self-reported peer punishment trait predicts willingness to punish in a PGG, but does not analyze or report the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that self-reported peer punishment trait is associated with actual punishment behavior in a public goods game with punishment. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The game design dimensions (player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are described for the pilot PGG, but no efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported. Therefore, this paper does not directly inform prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. It may be useful for understanding individual differences in punishment behavior, but not for predicting efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., Kiyonari, T., Molenmaker, W. E., & van Dijk, E. (2019). Do people prefer leaders who enforce norms? Reputational effects of reward and punishment decisions in noisy social dilemmas. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across three experimental studies, the paper finds that both rewarding and punishing norm enforcement by leaders in public goods games (and an organizational scenario) generally improve third-party observers' perceptions of leader competence and morality, especially under conditions of perfect monitoring (no noise). Under behavioral noise (imperfect monitoring), the reputational benefits of punishment are reduced or eliminated, while rewarding continues to yield positive reputational effects. These effects extend to leader preference (i.e., observers are more likely to prefer leaders who reward or punish, especially reward, over those who do not enforce norms). However, the studies do not measure or report on actual group efficiency, payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public goods games. Its findings are limited to third-party observer perceptions of leader reputation and preference, not to actual behavioral or payoff outcomes among group members. While the design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, punishment cost) are described, the outcomes are not efficiency-related. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. It may, however, inform hypotheses about the reputational incentives for leaders to use punishment or reward, which could indirectly affect norm enforcement behavior in other studies.

- source: Dannenberg, A., Haita-Falah, C., & Zitzelsberger, S. (2020). Voting on the threat of exclusion in a public goods experiment. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
