  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with groups of 5, the option to exclude (ostracize) members is introduced either costlessly (B10) or with a 20% endowment cost to all (B8). Groups can vote to implement the exclusion institution (endogenous) or have it imposed (exogenous). The exclusion institution leads to significantly higher contributions in all treatments. When exclusion is costless (B10), average payoffs (efficiency) are higher with exclusion than without. When exclusion is costly (B8), average payoffs are lower with exclusion than without, despite higher contributions. The exclusion mechanism is used almost exclusively to remove the lowest contributors, and the threat of exclusion increases cooperation among those who remain. The effect of exclusion on efficiency is positive when costless, but negative or neutral when costly. Endogenous and exogenous implementation yield similar results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment (here, exclusion/ostracism) on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the costliness of the punishment institution. When exclusion is costless, enabling punishment increases both contributions and efficiency. When exclusion is costly (reducing endowments), enabling punishment increases contributions but does not improve efficiency and may reduce it. The marginal per-capita return (MPCR), group size, and the presence/absence of communication are all specified, allowing for direct mapping to prediction tasks. The results are robust to endogenous vs exogenous institution choice. For prediction, the key moderator is whether the punishment institution imposes a cost on all group members; if so, efficiency gains are not realized even if cooperation rises.

- source: Lippert, S., & Tremewan, J. (2021). Pledge-and-review in the laboratory. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares a standard two-player public goods game (PG) to three pledge-and-review (P&R) variants: fixed disagreement payoffs (F), heterogeneous/certainty (C), and uncertainty (U). In all P&R treatments, after pledges are made, both players must vote to accept the pledges for them to be implemented; otherwise, both receive a disagreement payoff. Efficiency (average payoffs as a fraction of the full cooperation benchmark) is significantly higher in all P&R treatments than in the control PG game. The efficiency gain is persistent in the fixed disagreement payoff treatment, with no decline over 20 rounds, while some decline is observed in the other P&R treatments. There is no significant difference in efficiency between the three P&R variants. The mechanism allows conditional cooperators to avoid the typical decline in cooperation seen in repeated public goods games. The P&R institution functions as a form of second-party punishment (via voting down low pledges), but with endogenous costs and without explicit costly punishment tokens.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that adding a pledge-and-review (P&R) mechanism—where players can veto low contributions after seeing pledges—substantially increases efficiency in a two-player, 20-round public goods game with continuous contributions and no communication. The effect is robust to variations in disagreement payoffs (fixed, heterogeneous, uncertain). For prediction, if the control (PG) efficiency is known, enabling a P&R-style review stage is likely to increase efficiency by about 10% of the full cooperation benchmark, with the effect persisting over repeated rounds (at least in the fixed disagreement payoff case). The P&R mechanism is not identical to standard costly punishment (no explicit punishment tokens), but functions similarly by allowing players to block low contributions at a cost. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, continuous-contribution games without chat, with full feedback, and a review/veto stage after pledges.

- source: Micheli, L., Stallen, M., & Sanfey, A. G. (2021). The Effect of Centralized Financial and Social Incentives on Cooperative Behavior and Its Underlying Neural Mechanisms. *Brain Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: Across two lab experiments (one with fMRI), the prospect of centralized, probabilistic financial or social incentives (either rewards or punishments) substantially increases the percentage of tokens contributed to the group pot in a public goods game, compared to a no-incentive baseline. Financial incentives have a stronger effect than social incentives, but both are effective. There is no significant difference between the effect of rewards and punishments on contribution rates. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, so the findings are most directly about contribution behavior rather than payoff-based efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing centralized, probabilistic punishment (and/or reward) mechanisms increases contribution rates in a standard public goods game, with financial incentives being more effective than social incentives. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings must be interpreted as indirect evidence for efficiency effects. In typical PGGs, higher contribution rates are associated with higher efficiency, so the results suggest that enabling punishment (or reward) would likely increase efficiency relative to a no-incentive baseline, especially when the incentives are financial and administered centrally. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, probabilistic centralized punishment, no chat, continuous contribution) are well specified, making the study informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar lab PGGs, but the lack of direct efficiency analysis is a limitation.

- source: Faillo, M., Grieco, D., & Zarri, L. (2020). The impact of peer ratings on cooperation: The role of information and cost of rating. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with four players and 20 rounds, adding a mandatory, costless peer rating stage (where players rate each other's contributions on a 0-10 scale, with no monetary consequences) increases group contributions and efficiency relative to a baseline with no rating. This effect is robust to whether rating information is private (only the recipient sees their ratings) or public (all see everyone's ratings). When a small cost (1 token per peer rated) is introduced for assigning ratings, most players stop rating, and the effect on contributions and efficiency vanishes. The study provides direct evidence that non-monetary, payoff-irrelevant peer approval can foster cooperation and efficiency in public goods games, but only if it is costless to provide.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a costless, non-monetary peer approval mechanism (rating) in a standard repeated public goods game increases efficiency, even when ratings have no direct payoff consequences and are anonymous. The effect is robust to whether rating information is private or public. However, even a small cost to rating eliminates the effect. For prediction, the presence of a costless, mandatory, non-monetary peer approval mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control. If rating is costly, no efficiency gain should be expected. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the only sanction/reward is social approval, not monetary punishment or reward.

- source: Gërxhani, K., Volker, B., & Van Breemen, J. (2021). Who Will Do It? Volunteering to Change Cooperation Rules in a Heterogeneous Population. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Changing the minimum contribution rule from low to high in a public goods game increases both individual and collective earnings for all group members, regardless of their MPCR. The effect is robust and statistically significant. The likelihood of attempting a rule change depends on both social value orientation and benefit level: high-earning proselfs are most likely to initiate a change, while prosocials' behavior depends on whether they are high or low earners. The rule change also increases inequality between high and low earners. The experiment does not include peer punishment or reward mechanisms; the only 'cost' is the fee to initiate a rule change vote.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that increasing the minimum required contribution in a public goods game (via a formal rule change) increases group efficiency and total earnings, even in the presence of heterogeneity in MPCR. The findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of institutional changes that increase minimum cooperation requirements, but less directly relevant for peer punishment interventions. The absence of peer punishment or reward means the results are most applicable to settings where institutional rules, rather than decentralized sanctions, are the main intervention. The efficiency gain from raising the minimum contribution is clear and robust, suggesting that, in similar designs, enabling a mechanism to increase minimum contributions can substantially improve efficiency.

- source: Yang, C. L., Zhang, B., Charness, G., Li, C., & Lien, J. W. (2018). Endogenous rewards promote cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The endogenous, budget-balanced reward mechanism in a repeated four-player PGG leads to a substantial and sustained increase in contributions compared to the standard PGG. The mechanism works by taxing each player's income and allowing players to allocate their share of the tax fund as rewards to others. Contributions remain high and even increase over time with rewards, while they decline in the control. The paper finds that most subjects are conditional cooperators, and that reward assignment is strongly correlated with contribution. Net profits (payoffs) are higher under the reward mechanism than in the control, and the mechanism is more efficient than punishment-based mechanisms reported in the literature, which often fail to increase net payoffs due to the cost of punishment. The study does not test punishment directly, but positions endogenous reward as a more efficient and socially desirable alternative.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a budget-balanced, endogenous reward mechanism can substantially increase both contributions and net payoffs in a repeated PGG compared to a standard control. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling such a reward mechanism (with a 20% tax, peer allocation, and 1:1 reward effectiveness) will lead to higher efficiency than the control, and likely higher than typical punishment treatments (based on literature comparisons). However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on punishment, so its value for predicting the effect of punishment per se is indirect: it shows that reward can outperform punishment in terms of efficiency, especially when punishment is costly. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, feedback, etc.) are well specified and can inform parameterization for similar prediction tasks involving reward mechanisms, but not for punishment.

- source: Koukoumelis, A., & Levati, M. V. (2019). An experiment investigating the spillover effects of communication opportunities. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that recipients of unfair offers in a mini dictator game reduce their contributions in a subsequent public goods game. However, if they are allowed to send a message to the dictator, this reduction is significantly smaller—only in treatments without belief elicitation. When beliefs about the partner's contribution are elicited, the messaging opportunity does not significantly affect contribution changes for the full sample, but does for the subsample of recipients who actually reduce their contributions. The mechanism appears to be emotional relief from having a 'voice,' not a change in beliefs about partner behavior. The study does not analyze group efficiency or total payoff, and there is no monetary punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the effects of non-monetary, symbolic communication (emotional expression) on cooperation, but not for monetary punishment or efficiency outcomes. The design and findings may be informative for adjacent questions about communication, emotion, and cooperation, but not for the core prediction task involving punishment and efficiency.

- source: Grund, C., Harbring, C., Thommes, K., & Tilkes, K. R. (2020). Decisions on Extending Group Membership-Evidence from a Public Good Experiment. *Games*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that temporary group members contribute marginally more when the possibility of group membership extension is decided endogenously by a group member, compared to an exogenous random draw. However, this effect is weak and does not translate into significantly higher overall group contributions. Permanent members' contributions are not significantly affected by the extension mechanism. When a temporary member is replaced (especially exogenously), the newcomer initially contributes more than other group members, but this effect diminishes quickly. The study discusses monetary incentives and payoffs, but the main focus is on contribution behavior rather than efficiency or group payoff. There is no direct analysis of how the extension or replacement mechanisms affect group efficiency relative to the social optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that mechanisms resembling social sanctions (replacement or exclusion) can have incentive effects on contribution behavior, but these effects are modest and short-lived. The design does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms, and efficiency is not directly measured. For prediction, the results suggest that changing the group membership mechanism (endogenous vs. exogenous extension) has limited impact on overall group efficiency, and that replacement (as a form of exclusion) may temporarily boost newcomer contributions. However, these effects are primarily on contribution rates, not on efficiency or total group payoff. The study is most informative for understanding how exclusion or replacement (as a weak form of sanction) affects cooperation, rather than for predicting the efficiency impact of explicit punishment mechanisms.

- source: Li, J. B., Liu, X. L., Yin, X. L., Li, S. Q., Wang, G. R., Niu, X. F., & Zhu, C. K. (2018). Transcranial Direct Current Stimulation Altered Voluntary Cooperative Norms Compliance Under Equal Decision-Making Power. *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that modulating rDLPFC activity via tDCS causally affects voluntary cooperative norm compliance (measured as contribution) in a linear public goods game with four players, asymmetric endowments, and no punishment or sanction options. Anodal stimulation increases contributions, cathodal decreases them, and sham is intermediate. The effect is observed for both high and low endowment players. The study also finds that participants' own norm perceptions (norm.own) are more predictive of compliance than their perceptions of others' norms (norm.other), and that anodal stimulation changes the relative influence of these norm perceptions. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported, and the game does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are limited to the effect of neural stimulation on voluntary cooperation (contribution) in a standard linear PGG. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.) are reported and may be useful for context, but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot inform the downstream prediction task regarding the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful as a control or baseline for voluntary cooperation in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Martinsson, P., & Persson, E. (2019). Public Goods and Minimum Provision Levels: Does the Institutional Formation Affect Cooperation?. *Scandinavian Journal of Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Imposing a minimum contribution level (5 tokens out of 20) in a linear public goods game increases average contributions compared to a standard voluntary contribution mechanism. This effect is robust to whether the minimum is imposed exogenously or chosen endogenously by group vote. The increase in contributions is mainly due to free riders being forced to contribute at least the minimum; there is little evidence of crowding out among those who previously contributed more than the minimum. The mode of institutional formation (democracy vs. authority) does not significantly affect the outcome. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, but the results suggest that minimum contribution rules can increase public good provision in settings where punishment is not feasible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of minimum contribution rules in public goods games, especially in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that institutional interventions (minimum levels) can increase contributions, mainly by raising the floor for low contributors. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that minimum contribution rules can be effective in increasing contributions, but does not inform about the effect of punishment. The game design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, no chat, continuous contributions, no punishment/reward) are clearly specified, which helps in mapping to other studies. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured, the findings should be used with caution for efficiency prediction.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., & D'Amico, V. (2021). A preference to learn from successful rather than common behaviours in human social dilemmas. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Participants in a repeated linear public goods game (MPCR=0.4, groups of 4, 6 rounds) were more motivated to learn from and copy successful (high-earning) behaviors than to conform to common behaviors. When shown both the average (common) contribution and the average contribution of the highest earners from a previous experiment, participants contributed significantly less than when shown only the common behavior. This decline in cooperation was not explained by simply knowing about free riders, but specifically by the desire to learn from success. Most participants preferred to see information about successful behaviors over common behaviors. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but the lower contributions in the 'success' information condition imply lower efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated public goods games without punishment or reward, providing information about successful (high-earning) behaviors leads to lower cooperation and thus likely lower efficiency, compared to providing only information about common behaviors. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of success-based social information (even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward) can undermine efficiency. However, the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment directly, and so its direct relevance to predicting the effect of enabling punishment is limited. The findings are most relevant for understanding the role of social information and learning in public goods games, and suggest that efficiency may be lower when participants can observe and learn from successful free-riding.

- source: Boulu-Reshef, B., Holt, C. A., Rodgers, M. S., & Thomas-Hunt, M. C. (2020). The impact of leader communication on free-riding: An incentivized experiment with empowering and directive styles. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that empowering leader messages reduce free-riding (increase contributions) more than directive messages when there is no two-way leader-follower communication. When two-way communication is allowed, both leadership styles lead to higher contributions, and the difference between styles disappears. The presence of two-way communication is especially beneficial for directive leadership. The main dependent variable is contribution rate; earnings are reported but not the focus. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present, and efficiency is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides evidence that communication (especially two-way leader-follower communication) can increase contributions, which may be relevant for understanding the context in which punishment is or is not necessary. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, communication, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of punishment means the findings are only indirectly relevant for the prediction task.

- source: Wei, C. (2020). Can job rotation eliminate the Ratchet effect: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that ethnic dominance (caste hierarchy within groups) has a large negative effect on contributions in a public goods game, while ethnic diversity alone does not. Individuals display strong in-group bias, increasing their contributions in response to co-ethnic peers but not to others. The experiment did not include punishment or reward options, and all quantitative results are about contribution levels, not payoffs or efficiency. The authors discuss antisocial punishment and discrimination as real-world mechanisms but these are not present in the experimental design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or reward mechanisms were present in the experiment. It does, however, provide informative context about how group composition (especially ethnic dominance) can affect baseline cooperation rates, which may be relevant for interpreting control group efficiency in similar environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative about the effect of enabling punishment, but it does highlight that group structure (e.g., ethnic dominance) can strongly affect baseline cooperation, which could interact with punishment effects in other studies.

- source: Shirado, H., & Christakis, N. A. (2020). Network Engineering Using Autonomous Agents Increases Cooperation in Human Groups. *Iscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing bots that facilitate the breaking of ties with defectors ('disengaged' intervention) in dynamic network public goods games leads to a substantial increase in cooperation rates among human participants, compared to controls (no bots, bots that always cooperate, or tit-for-tat bots). The effect is robust even when only a single bot is present and when humans are aware of the bots' presence. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperative players per round. There is some supplementary evidence that, under the disengaged intervention, cooperators earn more than defectors, but group-level efficiency or total group payoff is not the main focus. The study does not involve punishment or sanctions in the standard sense, but the network exclusion of defectors is interpreted as a form of decentralized ostracism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Instead, it provides evidence about the effect of network engineering (ostracism via tie-breaking) on cooperation rates. The findings suggest that enabling players to break ties with defectors (or having bots facilitate this) can increase cooperation and, to some extent, the earnings of cooperators. However, because efficiency (group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) is not directly measured or reported, and because punishment is not present, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency under punishment is limited. It may be useful as adjacent evidence for the effects of exclusion or ostracism mechanisms.

- source: Liu, X. L., Li, J. B., Wang, G. R., Yin, X. L., Li, S. Q., & Fu, X. G. (2020). Transcranial direct current stimulation of the rLPFC shifts normative judgments in voluntary cooperation. *Neuroscience Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study demonstrates that modulating rLPFC activity via tDCS causally affects both participants' normative judgments about appropriate contributions and their actual contributions in a one-shot, linear, asymmetric public goods game. Anodal stimulation (increasing rLPFC excitability) leads to higher normative judgments and higher contributions, while cathodal stimulation (decreasing rLPFC excitability) leads to lower judgments and contributions, relative to sham. The effect is robust across both 'rich' and 'poor' player types. No punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms are present in the game, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant to understanding the neural basis of normative judgment and voluntary cooperation, but not to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions reported (e.g., player count, MPCR, number of rounds) may be useful for context, but the absence of punishment means this study should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Sun, X. P., Li, Y. B., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., Peng, J., Wang, H. Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2020). Co-Evolution of Complex Network Public Goods Game under the Edges Rules. *Entropy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that allowing players to break unsatisfactory connections and form new ones (edge rules) in a public goods game on a network leads to higher and more stable cooperation, especially at low gain factors (r=2). Both simulation and a 30-person lab experiment show that the fraction of cooperators increases and stabilizes under these rules, compared to a static network. The mechanism is interpreted as self-organized punishment (breaking ties with defectors) and reward (forming ties with cooperators), but there is no explicit costly punishment. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that endogenous network adaptation (breaking and forming ties based on satisfaction) can promote cooperation in public goods games, even at low MPCR. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of explicit punishment on efficiency. The 'punishment' here is not a costly sanction but the breaking of ties, which is structurally different from standard punishment mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the effects of network fluidity or endogenous partner choice, but not for predicting efficiency changes from adding explicit punishment to a fixed-group PGG.

- source: Jolly, E., & Chang, L. J. (2021). Gossip drives vicarious learning and facilitates social connection. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Allowing private communication (gossip) in a repeated public goods game increases average group contributions and slows the decline of cooperation over rounds, compared to no-communication conditions. Gossip is especially important when direct observation of others' behavior is limited, as it enables vicarious learning and helps participants adjust their own contributions based on information received from others. Gossip also increases social bonding and alignment of impressions between conversation partners. The study does not include any formal punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication (gossip) in public goods games increases contribution rates and sustains cooperation, especially under incomplete information. However, it does not address the effects of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that social information transmission (gossip) can substitute for formal sanctions in sustaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates for efficiency outcomes or for games with punishment enabled. The design dimensions most directly addressed are player count, number of rounds, chat/communication, and information visibility. The absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes limits its direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Stoop, J., van Soest, D., & Vyrastekova, J. (2018). Rewards and cooperation in social dilemma games. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that net-positive rewards (where the recipient gains more than the sender loses) dramatically increase cooperation and efficiency in standard public goods games (VCM), with most groups reaching high contribution levels when rewards are available. In contrast, in the Claim game—where players can undo others' contributions by taking from the public pool—rewards have only a marginal effect on cooperation, and average net contributions are not significantly different from zero. In the Claim game, rewards are often exchanged bilaterally and are not strongly linked to cooperative behavior in the social dilemma. The results suggest that the effectiveness of rewards in promoting efficiency depends critically on the structure of the social dilemma, particularly whether others can undo cooperative actions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of reward mechanisms in increasing efficiency in public goods games depends on whether the game allows for negative actions (i.e., taking from the public pool) and whether the MPCR is certain. In standard VCMs (linear PGGs), rewards can substantially increase efficiency, but in environments where contributions can be undone (as in the Claim game or CPR games), rewards are much less effective. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a 'take' option or uncertain MPCR is a critical moderator: control efficiency is not a sufficient predictor of treatment efficiency with rewards if the game allows for undoing others' contributions. The findings do not inform about punishment effects, as punishment is not studied.

- source: Qiu, H. Q., Zhang, Y. L., Hou, G. L., & Wang, Z. M. (2018). The Integrative Effects of Leading by Example and Follower Traits in Public Goods Game: A Multilevel Study. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated PGG with no punishment or reward, the presence and quality of leading by example (leader's average contribution) significantly affect both group and individual payoffs. High leader contributions (good leadership) increase team and follower payoffs above the control (no leader) condition, while low leader contributions (bad leadership) decrease payoffs below control. The effect is robust across rounds, though contributions decline over time (end-of-game effect). Follower traits also moderate cooperation, with risk attitude positively and trust attitude negatively associated with follower contributions. No evidence is provided on punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in PGG-like environments. It is directly relevant for understanding the impact of leadership structure (sequential vs. simultaneous moves) and leader behavior on efficiency, but not for predicting the effect of enabling punishment. The control group (simultaneous move, no leader) and treatment groups (sequential move with leader) are compared on efficiency, but all conditions lack punishment. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be informative for understanding baseline efficiency in repeated PGGs with and without leadership.

- source: Kocher, M. G., Tan, F. F., & Yu, J. (2018). PROVIDING GLOBAL PUBLIC GOODS: ELECTORAL DELEGATION AND COOPERATION. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares a standard linear public goods game (Baseline) to a Delegation treatment where each group elects a delegate who determines the contributions of all group members. Delegation leads to higher average contributions and efficiency than the Baseline, but both fall short of the social optimum. Over time, contributions and efficiency decline in both treatments, but less so in Delegation. Delegates rarely exploit their group members, typically assigning equal contributions. However, intergroup conditionality and lack of coordination across groups lead to a downward spiral in contributions and efficiency, a phenomenon the authors term 'P-inefficiency.' The study provides direct evidence on how delegation affects efficiency in public goods provision, but does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the effect of electoral delegation (but not punishment) on efficiency in a linear public goods game. It is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments with and without delegation, but not for environments with punishment or sanctioning. The design dimensions (player count, group size, rounds, MPCR, information, etc.) are well specified, but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is useful as a control or baseline for understanding efficiency in the absence of punishment.

- source: Schmidt, K. M., & Ockenfels, A. (2021). Focusing climate negotiations on a uniform common commitment can promote cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that negotiation design is crucial for promoting cooperation and efficiency in public goods games. Uniform common commitments (UCC) result in group investments averaging 83% of the social optimum with enforcement, compared to 45% (individual commitments, IC) and 48% (complex common commitments, CCC). Even without enforcement, UCC achieves 52% of the optimum, while IC and CCC achieve only 34%. The UCC design is robust to asymmetries and to the absence of enforcement. The paper does not study punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that negotiation design—specifically, requiring a uniform minimum contribution—can dramatically increase group efficiency in public goods games, even in the absence of enforcement. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It is highly relevant for understanding the impact of commitment and negotiation structure, but not for the prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Ren, T. Y., & Zheng, J. J. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics in the spatial public goods game with tolerance-based expulsion and cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in a standard linear public goods game (VCM) with four players and 20 rounds per part, groups that start with unequal endowments (two rich, two poor) and then experience redistribution to equality do not increase their group contributions or efficiency after redistribution. Group contributions in the post-redistribution phase are indistinguishable from those in groups that remain unequal, and both are significantly lower than in always-equal groups. The experience of initial inequality creates a persistent negative spillover, locking groups into low cooperation and efficiency even after equality is restored. The mechanism is traced to the former poor mimicking the low-contribution behavior of the former rich after redistribution. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, simply reducing inequality via redistribution does not increase efficiency in public goods games if groups have previously experienced inequality. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, for games with similar design (no punishment, no reward, linear PGG, 4 players, 20+20 rounds, MPCR=0.4, no chat, no identification, no summary of individual contributions), the efficiency in the treatment with redistribution will not exceed that of the control with persistent inequality, and both will be lower than in always-equal groups. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in PGGs without punishment or reward, but do not inform about the effect of punishment.

- source: Nax, H. H., Balietti, S., Murphy, R. O., & Helbing, D. (2018). Adding noise to the institution: an experimental welfare investigation of the contribution-based grouping mechanism. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that, in a repeated public goods game with 16 players (4 groups of 4), introducing a contribution-based grouping mechanism (meritocratic matching) with low or moderate noise leads to much higher efficiency (average total payoff) than random matching (no meritocracy). The efficiency gains are robust across different levels of noise, with the highest efficiency observed under perfect meritocracy (no noise), but even substantial gains at moderate noise levels. The control (no meritocracy, i.e., random matching) produces the lowest efficiency, consistent with standard PGG decay. The study also finds that increased meritocracy reduces payoff inequality, contrary to theoretical expectations. The results are robust to different measures of inequality and are observed both in lab and online settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with grouping based on contributions, increasing the precision of the grouping mechanism (i.e., reducing noise in meritocratic matching) leads to large increases in efficiency relative to random matching. For prediction tasks, the presence of a contribution-based grouping mechanism (even if noisy) should be expected to substantially increase efficiency compared to standard PGG controls. The effect is robust across a range of noise levels, and the efficiency gains are not offset by increased inequality. However, the study does not involve punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so its direct applicability to punishment-enabled games is limited. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of sorting/grouping mechanisms, not punishment.

- source: Bartos, V. (2021). Seasonal scarcity and sharing norms. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that neither making ethnic identity salient nor priming participants with memories of past ethnic violence significantly affects cooperation or generosity in the public goods or dictator games. There is a modest negative effect of violence priming on coordination with in-group partners in the stag hunt game, but this does not generalize to the public goods game. No significant differences are found in trust or trustworthiness by treatment. The results suggest that, in this context, ethnic divisions and reminders of conflict do not meaningfully alter pro-social economic behavior in standard experimental games. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it provides no evidence on the effects of punishment on efficiency or cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any of the games. It does, however, provide a well-powered null result for the effect of ethnic identity salience and conflict priming on cooperation in a two-player public goods game. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not relevant. For baseline efficiency in two-player PGGs without punishment, the results suggest that neither ethnic matching nor conflict salience meaningfully affect average contributions or pro-social behavior.

- source: Isler, O., Maule, J., & Starmer, C. (2018). Is intuition really cooperative? Improved tests support the social heuristics hypothesis. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that time-pressure (intuitive decision-making) increases contributions to the public good compared to forced-delay (reflective decision-making), especially under certain instructional conditions. The effect is robust to compliance and understanding confounds. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment condition or analyze payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on contribution rates under different cognitive conditions may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with or without punishment. The design dimensions reported (e.g., player count, MPCR, no punishment, no reward, no chat) may help contextualize the baseline, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes limits its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Chakraborti, R., Maloney, M., Roberts, G., & Shogren, J. F. (2018). Social capital and the voluntary provision of public goods. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: Both structured (team-building) and unstructured (free-form) communication interventions increase contributions in a linear public goods game. Free-form communication leads to higher and more persistent contributions than team-building when group composition is stable. When groups are reallocated after the intervention, team-building's effect on contributions persists, but the effect of free-form communication is reduced, though still positive. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome. Its findings are relevant for understanding the impact of communication and social capital interventions on contribution behavior, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency.

- source: Martinsson, P., Medhin, H., & Persson, E. (2019). MINIMUM LEVELS AND FRAMING IN PUBLIC GOOD PROVISION. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that, in a standard linear public goods game, contributions are higher in the give frame than in the take frame. When a minimum contribution level (20% of endowment) is introduced, contributions decrease in the give frame (crowding out) but increase in the take frame (crowding in). The effect is driven by more low contributors being forced to increase in the take frame, while in the give frame, some higher contributors reduce their contributions to the minimum. The distribution of contributor types is stable across frames and minimum level conditions. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it provides informative evidence about the effects of minimum contribution requirements and framing on contribution behavior. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly relevant. For tasks involving minimum contribution levels or framing, the results suggest that minimum levels can have opposite effects depending on the frame: crowding out in give, crowding in in take. Since efficiency is not reported, the findings should not be used to predict efficiency outcomes of punishment-enabled games.

- source: van den Berg, P., Dewitte, P., Aertgeerts, I., & Wenseleers, T. (2020). How the incentive to contribute affects contributions in the one-shot public goods game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that average contributions in a one-shot public goods game increase with the marginal per capita return (MPCR), with most of the increase occurring at lower MPCR values (below 0.58–0.64). At higher MPCR, contributions plateau well below full cooperation. The experiment uses groups of three, no punishment or reward, and no communication. The findings provide a high-resolution mapping of how MPCR shapes contribution behavior, but do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting baseline (control) efficiency in public goods games as a function of MPCR, group size, and the absence of punishment or reward. It does not provide any evidence about the effect of punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for estimating control efficiency (without punishment) given MPCR and group size, but does not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Brañas-Garza, P., Molis, E., & Neyse, L. (2021). Exposure to inequality may cause under-provision of public goods: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that making subjects aware of inequality in returns between groups (by informing them about other groups' mpcr) leads to a significant reduction in contributions among low-mpcr groups and a non-significant increase among high-mpcr groups, but only when the inequality is large (mpcr difference of 0.5). When the inequality is small (mpcr difference of 0.25), the effect of social information is negligible. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcome is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that transparency about inequality can undermine cooperation in public goods provision, especially for those at a disadvantage.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in any treatment. However, it does provide informative evidence about how information about inequality (social comparison) can affect contribution rates, which may indirectly affect efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight that contextual factors like perceived inequality and information can strongly moderate contribution behavior. The game design dimensions are well specified (player count, mpcr, number of rounds, information structure), but the absence of punishment means the findings cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Kok, L., Oosterbaan, V., Stoker, H., & Vyrastekova, J. (2020). In-group favouritism and social norms: Public goods experiments in Tanzania. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that making sub-village identity salient (In-group treatment) does not increase public goods contributions compared to mixed sub-village groups (Out-group treatment). In fact, contributions are significantly higher in the Out-group treatment. This is explained by the activation of a village-level social norm of cooperation in the Out-group context, while the In-group context triggers more selfish, conditional cooperation. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide evidence that social context and group identity salience can affect contribution behavior, which may be relevant for understanding baseline cooperation in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as not providing direct evidence on punishment or efficiency effects.

- source: Fellner-Röhling, G., Kröger, S., & Seki, E. (2020). Public good production in heterogeneous groups: An experimental analysis on the relation between external return and information. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in public goods games with heterogeneous external returns, the level and structure of information about group heterogeneity and contributor type significantly affect contribution behavior. When no information about heterogeneity is provided, both high- and low-ability types contribute similarly. When heterogeneity is known but contributions cannot be linked to type, low-ability types contribute more than high-ability types (a 'poisoning-of-the-well' effect). When contributions can be linked to type, high-ability types contribute more. Overall, more information about heterogeneity and contributor identity increases average contributions, but the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff with or without punishment, as no punishment mechanism is present and efficiency is not measured. However, it provides informative evidence about how information structure and heterogeneity in marginal returns affect contribution rates in public goods games. For prediction tasks involving efficiency, the results suggest that information about heterogeneity and contributor identity can increase contributions, but the translation to efficiency or payoff outcomes would require additional assumptions or data. The absence of punishment means the findings are not directly transferable to settings where punishment is enabled or disabled.

- source: Martinangeli, A. F. M. (2021). Do what (you think) the rich will do: Inequality and belief heterogeneity in public good provision. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games with unequal endowments, both rich and poor participants expect the rich to contribute more. Individual contributions in the repeated game are more strongly correlated with beliefs about the rich than about the poor. Group cohesion interventions (allowing chat in a team-building task) do not significantly increase overall contributions, though there is some evidence of increased contributions among poor participants. There is no significant difference in the proportion of endowment contributed between equal and unequal endowment conditions. The paper does not analyze efficiency or total group payoff, and there are no punishment or reward mechanisms in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or reward mechanisms are present. However, it provides informative context about how endowment heterogeneity and belief formation affect contribution behavior. For prediction tasks involving efficiency, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of beliefs about specific group members (especially the rich) in driving cooperation, and in showing that group cohesion interventions may have limited impact in the absence of feedback or punishment.

- source: Kölle, F., & Quercia, S. (2021). The influence of empirical and normative expectations on cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a standard linear public goods game, most people are conditional cooperators, increasing their contributions as others do. When there is strategic uncertainty (simultaneous play), normative expectations (what is socially appropriate) and empirical expectations (what others will do) diverge, and actual contributions are lower than the social optimum. When players can condition on others' contributions (sequential/strategy method), empirical and normative expectations are more closely aligned, and contributions are higher and more predictable. The paper provides direct evidence for the existence of a social norm of conditional cooperation, but does not study punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation and the role of expectations in the absence of punishment, but cannot inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The game design dimensions are well specified for a standard PGG, but the absence of any punishment or sanctioning mechanism means this paper cannot be used to predict treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Kim, D. G. (2018). Population uncertainty in voluntary contributions of public goods. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that population uncertainty can increase voluntary contributions in public goods games when the production function is nonlinear and concave, but has no significant effect when the production function is linear. The main outcome measured is the average (percentage) contribution to the group account. No punishment or sanction mechanisms are present in the design. The results suggest that the effect of population uncertainty depends on the shape of the production function and the nature of warm-glow utility, but do not provide evidence about punishment or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency under punishment, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in any treatment. It does provide evidence about how population uncertainty and production function shape affect contribution rates, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes under punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, except as a control for understanding baseline contribution behavior in the absence of punishment.

- source: Gianotti, L. R. R., Dahinden, F. M., Baumgartner, T., & Knoch, D. (2019). Understanding Individual Differences in Domain-General Prosociality: A Resting EEG Study. *Brain Topography*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that higher resting beta2 and beta3 current density in the right temporo-parietal junction (TPJ) is positively associated with higher domain-general prosociality, as measured by contributions in three one-shot PGGs (with multipliers 1.2, 2, and 3.2) and giving in a dictator game. The neural trait is stable and specific to the TPJ and beta bands. No relationship is found between prosociality and the honesty-humility personality scale. The study does not include any punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings are limited to individual differences in prosociality (contribution/giving) and their neural correlates. Thus, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment or related game design dimensions.

- source: Yam, P. P. C., Ng, G. T. T., Au, W. T., Tao, L., Lu, S., Leung, H., & Fung, J. M. Y. (2018). The effect of subgroup homogeneity of efficacy on contribution in public good dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that arranging participants into homogeneous or heterogeneous subgroups of efficacy does not significantly affect the number or proportion of contributions to the public good. This is because two mediating mechanisms—perceived efficacy and expectation of subgroup contribution—operate in opposite directions and cancel each other out. The experiment does not include any punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are limited to contribution behavior and psychological mediators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or discussed. The design dimensions studied (subgroup homogeneity, efficacy) are not among the core prediction dimensions for punishment effects. The outcome measures are contribution rates and psychological variables, not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Chen, Y. Y., Lu, X. B., Li, Y. Z., Zeng, L. L., Yu, P., Luo, J., Ye, H., & Zheng, W. J. (2021). The Role of the Ventromedial Prefrontal Cortex in Public Good and Public Bad Games: Evidence From a tDCS Study. *Frontiers in Behavioral Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that modulating VMPFC activity via tDCS can causally affect cooperative behavior in public bad games (loss domain), specifically decreasing cooperation at the start of the game, but has no effect in public good games (gain domain). The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution/loss contribution, beliefs, attitudes), not efficiency or group payoff. There is no punishment or sanction mechanism in the design. The findings are relevant for understanding neural mechanisms of cooperation, but do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as there is no punishment or sanction mechanism in the experimental design, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported. The findings are limited to neural modulation of cooperation and beliefs in standard and loss-framed public goods games. It may provide some contextual background on behavioral outcomes in PGGs, but does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Alventosa, A., Antonioni, A., & Hernández, P. (2021). Pool punishment in public goods games: How do sanctioners? incentives affect us?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The ratchet-up mechanism (contributions cannot decrease) reduces cooperation rates compared to a standard voluntary contribution mechanism. Adding a binding collective minimum contribution requirement (lowest common denominator) increases cooperation rates by about 25 percentage points compared to the ratchet-up mechanism alone, but does not reach the social optimum. A non-binding minimum has no significant effect. The difference is explained by conditional cooperation: non-compliance with the non-binding minimum leads to negative reciprocity and declining cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome, so findings are about contribution/cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a public goods game with a ratchet-up mechanism, adding a binding collective minimum contribution requirement increases cooperation rates, while a non-binding minimum does not. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency. The absence of punishment or sanctioning means the results do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and results are highly relevant for understanding how minimum contribution rules (binding vs. non-binding) affect cooperation, but not for the effect of punishment per se.

- source: Zhang, Z., Zhang, X., & Putterman, L. (2019). Trust and cooperation at a confluence of worlds: An experiment in Xinjiang, China. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that both Uyghur and Han students contribute more in public goods games when grouped with coethnics than with outgroup members, and least with anonymous group members. However, allowing face-to-face communication in mixed-ethnicity groups leads to the highest cooperation levels, even exceeding those in homogeneous groups. The study does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution, trust, trustworthiness), and efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed by condition. The results suggest that communication can be a stronger facilitator of cooperation than ethnic homogeneity, and that social identifiability (even of outgroup members) increases cooperation relative to anonymity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it provides informative evidence about the effects of group composition and communication on cooperation rates. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation levels in the absence of punishment, and for considering the effects of communication and social context as moderators. The absence of punishment means it cannot inform the treatment-control efficiency difference for punishment-enabled games.

- source: Frey, U. J. (2019). Long-term evidence on cooperation and cultural differences in public goods dilemmas. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a real-world, online public goods game, average cooperation rates are low and relatively stable over time, with only minor differences between countries. Individual differences are more important than cultural, economic, or religious factors. The results suggest that cooperation in this context is lower than in typical laboratory public goods games, especially those with punishment. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the game, and the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a useful baseline for public goods games without punishment: average contribution rates are much lower than in laboratory experiments, and cultural background has little effect. However, it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff, as no punishment mechanism is present or analyzed. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment, this paper is only relevant as a control or baseline for no-punishment environments, especially in more naturalistic, long-term, or online settings.

- source: Li, J. B., Liu, X. L., Yin, X. L., Li, S. Q., Wang, P. C., Niu, X. F., & Zhu, C. K. (2018). Transcranial Direct Current Stimulation of the Right Lateral Prefrontal Cortex Changes a priori Normative Beliefs in Voluntary Cooperation. *Frontiers in Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that modulating rLPFC activity via tDCS causally shifts participants' a priori normative beliefs about cooperation in a public goods game. Anodal stimulation increases, and cathodal stimulation decreases, both the perceived normative standard (norm.belief) and the expected contributions of others (pg.belief). These effects are robust across player types (high and low endowment) and are statistically significant. However, the study does not measure or report actual contribution behavior, group payoffs, or efficiency, nor does it include any punishment or sanction mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It uses a standard linear PGG design but does not include punishment, nor does it report any efficiency, payoff, or contribution outcomes. The only outcomes are beliefs about norms and others' contributions. Therefore, it does not inform the downstream prediction task of how punishment affects efficiency, given game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Heine, F., & Strobel, M. (2020). Reward and punishment in a team contest. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares reward and punishment mechanisms in a team contest game (with and without between-group competition). Groups of four play 15 rounds, deciding how much to contribute to a group lottery (contest) and then can reward or punish teammates at a cost. The main findings are: (1) Both punishment and reward mechanisms lead to over-contribution relative to Nash equilibrium, causing significant efficiency losses (rent dissipation). (2) Punishment does not increase efficiency; it reduces it, as punishing is costly and does not significantly increase contributions. (3) Rewarding increases contributions more than punishment, but is welfare-neutral (cost to giver equals benefit to receiver). (4) The contest environment (with an opponent group) leads to even greater inefficiency and lower average payoffs than the non-contest environment. (5) The R&P treatment (both reward and punishment available) in the contest environment is the least efficient, with the highest overspending and lowest mean income. (6) Receiving reward increases subsequent contributions, but punishment does not. (7) Overall, the presence of punishment does not solve the efficiency problem in this environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a team contest environment with public-good-like features, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency and may worsen it, especially when both reward and punishment are available. The effect of punishment is less positive (more negative) on efficiency compared to reward or baseline. The contest structure (between-group competition) amplifies inefficiency. For prediction, if the control game (no punishment) is already inefficient due to over-contribution, enabling punishment is likely to further reduce efficiency, not improve it. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, known MPCR, punishment cost and tech, no identification of punishers, no communication) are all specified and match standard lab contest/PGG designs.
