The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled in similar environments.

- source: Abbink, K., Ryvkin, D., & Serra, D. (2020). Corrupt police. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three main conditions: no police, honest police (can only fine), and corrupt police (can fine or demand bribes). With no police, crime rates are very high and citizen payoffs are low. Honest police reduce crime and increase citizen payoffs. Corrupt police also reduce crime compared to no police, but citizen payoffs are much lower than with honest police due to high bribe payments to officers, resulting in strong inequality. Bribes are used as a substitute for fines, and are often targeted at law-breakers, but imperfectly so—many law-abiding citizens are also extorted. When the maximum bribe is capped at the fine level, crime rates rise to near the no-police level, showing that the deterrent effect of bribes depends on their size. Reward mechanisms for officers based on low bribery rates (but not low crime rates) moderately reduce both bribery and crime, and increase citizen payoffs, but do not eliminate the efficiency loss from corruption.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling punishment (via police, even if corrupt) increases group efficiency compared to no-punishment baselines, but the efficiency gain is much smaller when punishment is corrupt and redistributive (bribes) rather than legal (fines). The efficiency effect of punishment depends on both the targeting and the cost/impact of punishment: when bribes are large and targeted at law-breakers, crime is deterred, but citizen payoffs are much lower due to wealth transfer to officers. If bribes are capped at the fine level, the deterrent effect is lost. Reward mechanisms for officers based on low bribery rates can partially mitigate the efficiency loss from corruption. For prediction, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, but the structure (honest vs. corrupt, bribe size, targeting) and the presence of anti-corruption incentives are critical moderators.

- source: Wegmann, J., & Musshoff, O. (2019). Groundwater management institutions in the face of rapid urbanization - Results of a framed field experiment in Bengaluru, India. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both externally imposed punishment and reward rules, implemented via random monitoring and sanctions/rewards for exceeding/following optimal extraction, significantly reduce over-extraction of groundwater in a framed field experiment with 600 real-world users. Social efficiency (group payoff relative to the social optimum) increased from 86% in the control to 97% under punishment and 95% under reward, with welfare gains of 8% and 6% over control, respectively (both statistically significant at 1%). Communication alone had a much smaller effect (90% efficiency, 1% gain, not significant). The effect of punishment and reward was not significantly different between rural and urban areas. User types (myopic, rational, social optimal) persisted across treatments, but all treatments crowded out the most pro-social (social optimal) users to some extent. The main outcome is that externally imposed monitoring and sanctioning (punishment) is highly effective at increasing efficiency in this CPR context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that, in a dynamic CPR game with real-world users, adding an externally imposed punishment mechanism (random monitoring, monetary penalty for over-extraction) increases group efficiency (payoff/welfare) by a substantial and statistically significant margin compared to a control with no punishment. The effect is robust to user type and urbanization. The game is not a standard linear PGG but is a close CPR variant with continuous extraction, group interdependence, and profit-based payoffs. The punishment cost and technology are specified, and the efficiency gains are quantified. This evidence should increase predicted efficiency for similar games when punishment is enabled, especially when the control efficiency is below the social optimum.

- source: Lohse, J., & Waichman, I. (2020). The effects of contemporaneous peer punishment on cooperation with the future. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a multigenerational public goods game, voluntary cooperation is difficult to sustain across generations. When peer punishment is available within a generation, the likelihood that the group meets the contribution threshold and sustains future generations more than doubles compared to the baseline. However, the increase in group earnings from more sustained generations is offset by the costs incurred through punishment, resulting in no significant difference in average aggregate earnings (efficiency) between the punishment and no-punishment conditions. The effect of punishment on individual contribution levels is small, but it is sufficient to increase the rate at which groups meet the threshold. Punishment is mainly directed at those who contribute less than their peers, especially when the group fails to reach the threshold.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling peer punishment in a threshold-based, multigenerational public goods game increases the probability of group success (i.e., reaching future generations and thus group-level payoff), but the efficiency gains are offset by the costs of punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of peer punishment can increase the likelihood of group success, but unless punishment is low-cost or highly effective, the net effect on efficiency may be limited or null. The design dimensions—such as group size, threshold, punishment cost and effectiveness, and the absence of communication—are all well-specified and should be used to inform predictions. The results are most relevant for settings with threshold public goods, no communication, and contemporaneous peer punishment.

- source: De Geest, L. R., & Stranlund, J. K. (2019). Defending public goods and common-pool resources. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 2x2 lab experiment (PG/CPR x Theft/No Theft), groups of four insiders played 15 rounds, with or without two outsiders who could steal surplus. Insiders could use costly sanctions (1:3 cost:impact) against outsiders. The main findings are: (a) In the CPR setting, the threat of theft increased cooperation and surplus creation, leading to higher group profits and surplus net of theft compared to the no-theft control. In the PG setting, the threat of theft did not significantly affect cooperation or surplus creation, and group profits decreased. (b) Sanctions were used more often in the PG setting, but were not effective at deterring outsider theft in either setting. (c) The presence of punishment did not restore efficiency to the fully cooperative benchmark, as insiders could not coordinate sanctions well enough to fully deter outsiders. (d) The introduction of theft increased variation in group outcomes, especially in the PG setting. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is mixed: it does not reliably increase efficiency, and its effectiveness depends on the game structure and the ability of insiders to coordinate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency in public-goods-like environments with an external threat (outsider theft). The design allows for direct comparison of efficiency (group profit, surplus net of theft) with and without punishment enabled, under known game parameters. The results show that punishment does not reliably increase efficiency: in the CPR setting, the threat of theft (with punishment available) increases surplus creation and efficiency, but in the PG setting, efficiency decreases. In both cases, punishment is not effective at fully deterring theft, and group variation increases. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is contingent on the type of social dilemma (PG vs CPR), the presence of an external threat, and the group's ability to coordinate sanctions. Control efficiency (no-theft, no-punishment) is higher than treatment efficiency (theft, punishment) in the PG setting, but lower in the CPR setting. The paper is highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency, especially in environments with external threats and endogenous punishment.

- source: Kanitsar, G. (2021). Self-Governance in Generalized Exchange. A Laboratory Experiment on the Structural Embeddedness of Peer Punishment. *Games*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Peer punishment increases cooperation and group earnings (efficiency) in public good structures only when punishment is costless; costly punishment does not improve efficiency. In generalized exchange, peer punishment (both costly and costless) does not increase cooperation or group earnings. The effectiveness of punishment is thus highly contingent on the network structure: dense sanctioning networks (public good) allow punishment to deter defection and improve efficiency, while sparse networks (generalized exchange) do not. The cost-impact ratio of punishment is important, but its effect is moderated by the structural environment. The study provides direct evidence that the efficiency gains from peer punishment in public goods do not transfer to generalized exchange settings.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on the network structure. In standard public good games, costless punishment can substantially increase efficiency, but costly punishment may not. In generalized exchange (circular, sparse punishment network), enabling punishment—even if costless—does not improve efficiency. Thus, when predicting the efficiency effect of punishment, it is crucial to account for the density of the punishment network and whether the game is a standard public good or a generalized exchange. Control efficiency is not a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency when moving from public good to generalized exchange structures.

- source: Vollan, B., Pröpper, M., Landmann, A., & Balafoutas, L. (2019). Self-Governance and Punishment: An Experimental Study among Namibian Forest Users. *Economic Development and Cultural Change*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study implemented a framed field/lab-in-the-field experiment with Namibian forest users, using a threshold public goods/extraction game with two punishment treatments: internal (peer) and external (random monitoring). Both punishment regimes reduced extraction compared to the baseline, but neither increased group or individual earnings (efficiency). Internal punishment was more effective at disciplining high extractors, especially those of low status, due to more targeted and severe peer punishment. However, internal punishment also led to high levels of antisocial punishment, which, together with the direct costs of punishment, resulted in lower overall earnings than both the external punishment and the no-punishment baseline. Ethnic heterogeneity increased antisocial punishment but did not reduce cooperation or prosocial punishment. The study concludes that while self-governance with peer punishment can discipline free riders, it may also generate substantial welfare losses due to costly and vengeful punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a threshold public goods/extraction game with both internal (peer) and external (centralized) punishment, the introduction of punishment mechanisms does not necessarily increase efficiency (group or individual earnings) and may even reduce it, especially when peer punishment is available. The main reason is that the costs of punishment (both direct and indirect, including antisocial punishment) can outweigh the benefits from increased cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar environments (5 players, 20 rounds, MPCR 0.4, threshold/tipping point, no chat, anonymous punishment, no rewards), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when peer punishment is allowed. The effect may be less negative or neutral with centralized punishment, but still not positive. The findings also highlight the importance of group composition (status, ethnicity) and the potential for antisocial punishment to undermine efficiency gains.

- source: Shreedhar, G., Tavoni, A., & Marchiori, C. (2020). Monitoring and punishment networks in an experimental common pool resource dilemma. *Environment and Development Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a nonlinear CPR lab experiment with three punishment network structures (complete, undirected circle, directed circle), the authors find that average appropriation (resource extraction) is similar across all networks. However, the amount and type of punishment, as well as efficiency (net payoffs), differ. The complete network, with perfect monitoring and punishment, leads to the highest punishment expenditures and the lowest efficiency. Both imperfect networks (undirected and directed circles) have lower punishment expenditures and higher efficiency. The undirected circle network elicits more severe and more prosocial punishment than the other two, but overall, both imperfect networks are more efficient than the complete network. The results suggest that reducing the number of potential punishers (i.e., making monitoring and punishment less than perfect) can increase efficiency by reducing costly punishment, even if cooperation (appropriation) does not change.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in nonlinear CPR games with punishment enabled, the structure of the punishment network (i.e., the number of potential punishers and the network's density/directedness) can significantly affect efficiency. Specifically, reducing punishment opportunities (imperfect networks) increases efficiency compared to perfect monitoring and punishment, because less punishment is used and thus less is wasted on costly sanctions. Appropriation (cooperation) rates remain similar, so the efficiency gain is due to lower punishment costs. For prediction, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment in a perfect monitoring/punishment environment may reduce efficiency relative to imperfect punishment environments, and that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not always positive—it depends on the punishment network's structure. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially the structure of punishment opportunities.

- source: De Geest, L. R., & Kingsley, D. C. (2021). Inequality and Peer Punishment in a Common-Pool Resource Experiment. *Strategic Behavior and the Environment*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Peer punishment in a nonlinear CPR game reduces extractions in both equal and unequal endowment settings, but its effect on efficiency (group earnings relative to the social optimum) depends on the distribution of endowments. In the equal endowment treatment, punishment leads to lower earnings due to high punishment costs and lack of coordination. In the unequal endowment treatment, punishment does not reduce average earnings and increases earnings for low-endowment participants, as groups coordinate around the Equal Earnings norm. Punishment is more targeted and less frequent in the unequal treatment, leading to higher efficiency. The effect of punishment is thus moderated by the presence of endowment inequality, which creates a focal point for coordination and more effective norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in CPR/public goods-like games depends on the distribution of endowments (inequality). When endowments are equal, punishment can reduce efficiency due to high punishment costs and poor coordination. When endowments are unequal, punishment is more effective, leading to higher efficiency, especially for low-endowment participants, as groups coordinate around a salient norm. For prediction, the presence of endowment heterogeneity should be considered a key moderator: punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when inequality is present and salient. Other design features (e.g., punishment technology, group size, number of rounds) are held constant in this study, so the main actionable insight is about the interaction between punishment and endowment inequality.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., Otaka, T., Shimao, H., Shimomura, K. I., & Yamato, T. (2018). Reciprocity and exclusion in informal financial institutions: An experimental study of rotating savings and credit associations. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compared four treatments in a fixed-order ROSCA game: baseline (no sanctions), punishment rule (forfeiture), voting (exclusion), and both punishment and voting. Exclusion by voting led to significantly higher contribution rates and mean payoffs than the other treatments. The punishment rule, which prevented non-contributors from receiving payouts, did not increase contributions or payoffs relative to the baseline. The combination of voting and punishment was less effective than voting alone. The results suggest that social exclusion based on reputation is a more effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation and improving group efficiency than the explicit punishment rule tested here.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong evidence that a costless, rule-based punishment (forfeiture of payout for non-contributors) does not improve efficiency or mean payoff compared to a baseline with no punishment. In contrast, exclusion by peer voting (reputation-based exclusion) substantially increases both contributions and efficiency. The game structure is a close variant of a public goods game (ROSCA), with fixed group size, known rounds, and full information about others' actions. The findings suggest that not all punishment mechanisms are equally effective: exclusion based on group voting is effective, but a non-costly, rule-based punishment is not. This distinction is important for predicting the effect of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Robbett, A. (2019). Just ask? Preference revelation and lying in a public goods experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares four conditions in a repeated public goods game with private, randomly assigned types: (1) baseline voluntary contribution (VCM), (2) Mechanism (report type to a mechanism that enforces the socially optimal contribution for the reported type), (3) Revelation (report type to group members, then contribute voluntarily), and (4) Punishment (as Revelation, but with a costly peer punishment stage). The main findings are: (a) Mechanism and Revelation both increase cooperation and group efficiency relative to VCM, but Mechanism's effect fades over time; (b) Punishment does not increase cooperation or efficiency compared to VCM, and group profits are actually lower in the Punishment condition; (c) Punishment is mainly used to sanction obviously dishonest reports, not under-contribution, and is too weak to incentivize higher contributions; (d) The presence of punishment undermines the positive effect of truthful reporting on cooperation seen in the Revelation condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with private, heterogeneous incentives and observable reports/contributions, adding a peer punishment mechanism (with a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio) does not increase efficiency or group payoffs compared to a baseline without punishment. In fact, efficiency is lower with punishment, likely due to underutilization and mis-targeting of sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar environments (3 players, 10 rounds, no chat, private types, punishment available after observing reports/contributions, punishment cost 1:3), enabling punishment is likely to have a neutral or negative effect on efficiency relative to control. The results also highlight that the effectiveness of punishment depends on the clarity of norms and the ability to identify free-riders; when these are ambiguous due to private information, punishment may backfire or be ineffective.

- source: Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2019). Individual solutions to shared problems create a modern tragedy of the commons. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can choose between solving a shared problem collectively (public good) or individually (private solution), many opt for the individual solution even when collective action would be more efficient. This leads to inefficient resource allocation and coordination failure, especially at intermediate cost-benefit ratios. When peer punishment is introduced, it increases the rate of collective solutions but also triggers mutual punishment between individualists and collectivists, which reduces overall group welfare. Punishment does not reduce free riding but instead is used in feuds between incompatible strategies, amplifying inefficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in public-goods-like games where individual solutions are available, enabling peer punishment can reduce efficiency (group welfare), even if it increases collective action. The effect is driven by wasteful punishment exchanges rather than improved cooperation. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling punishment is likely to decrease efficiency in similar settings, especially when individual solutions are a viable alternative to collective action. The findings are most relevant when the game allows both individual and collective solutions, and punishment is peer-based, costly, and has a strong impact.

- source: Nikias, A., & Sy, A. (2021). Mutual monitoring in multi-period teams: an experimental investigation. *Team Performance Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that lowering the cost of punishment in a two-period team production game (with payoffs analogous to a prisoner's dilemma) increases both the frequency of punishment and the rate of working (cooperation) in both periods. When punishment is costless, participants are more likely to punish non-cooperators, and this threat leads to higher cooperation. The design ensures that higher working rates directly translate to higher group output and expected utility, so efficiency is higher when punishment is less costly. The effect is statistically significant for both periods. The study isolates the effect of punishment cost by holding other factors constant and using a one-shot horizon to rule out future gains from cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player, two-period team production game with mutual monitoring and contingent punishment, reducing the cost of punishment increases cooperation and thus group efficiency. The design is closely related to public goods and prisoner's dilemma games, and the mapping from working to group payoff is explicit. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that lower punishment cost (punishment_cost dimension) will lead to higher efficiency when punishment is enabled, holding other design features constant. The findings are most directly applicable to small, short-horizon, mutual monitoring settings without communication or rewards.

- source: Nair, A., Blome, C., Choi, T. Y., & Lee, G. (2018). Re-visiting collaborative behavior in supply networks structural embeddedness and the influence of contextual changes and sanctions. *Journal of Purchasing and Supply Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated triadic supply network game with real managers, the introduction of sanctions (punishment) reduces both group allocation and average payoffs when the incentive structure favors cooperation (i.e., low temptation to defect). When the incentive structure is changed to increase the temptation to defect, sanctions increase group allocation compared to the no-sanction condition, but average payoffs remain lower than in the cooperative baseline. Sanctions are only effective at increasing group allocation when the underlying incentive structure is unfavorable to cooperation. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average payoffs are provided for each condition. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus negative in cooperative contexts and ambiguous (possibly less negative) in defect-prone contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like network games depends critically on the underlying incentive structure. When the baseline (control) efficiency is high (cooperative context), enabling punishment reduces efficiency. When the baseline efficiency is low (defection is tempting), enabling punishment can increase group allocation but does not fully restore efficiency/payoff to cooperative levels. For prediction, this suggests that the marginal effect of punishment on efficiency is negative when control efficiency is high, and less negative or possibly weakly positive when control efficiency is low, but the overall efficiency with punishment rarely exceeds the cooperative baseline. The game design dimensions most relevant here are the payoff structure (temptation to defect), the presence and cost/effectiveness of punishment, and the information structure.

- source: Chen, S., Shi, J. C., Yang, X. L., Ye, H., & Luo, J. (2019). Modulating Activity in the Dorsolateral Prefrontal Cortex Changes Punishment in the 3-Player Prisoner's Dilemma: A Transcranial Direct Current Stimulation Study. *Frontiers in Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that modulating DLPFC activity via tDCS can significantly decrease the amount of punishment assigned by cooperators to defectors (both in second-party and third-party punishment), depending on the stimulation mode. One bilateral stimulation (left anodal/right cathodal) increases cooperation rates. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding neural mechanisms of punishment and cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or prisoner's dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, as it does not report these outcomes. It does, however, provide detailed evidence on how punishment behavior and cooperation rates are affected by neural modulation in a 3-player prisoner's dilemma with punishment. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that punishment behavior can be modulated by factors unrelated to game design (e.g., neural stimulation), and that cooperation can be increased by certain interventions. However, without efficiency or payoff data, the paper's direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is limited.

- source: Liao, Y. L., Zhang, L., Lei, S. Y., Song, M. Z., Deng, W. K., & Hu, D. F. (2021). RETRACTED: Third-Party Punishment Mechanism and Corporate Cooperation in Environmental Investment: Experiments on Public Goods Game (Retracted Article). *Discrete Dynamics in Nature and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally compares a threshold public goods game with and without third-party punishment (TPP), using groups of three participants (framed as enterprises) making resource investment decisions toward a group threshold. The TPP mechanism automatically punishes group members whose investment rate is below the group mean. The presence of TPP leads to higher mean resource investment amounts, higher individual and group investment rates, and a substantially higher success rate of cooperation (from 55.57% to 76.85%). Regression analysis confirms that TPP has a significant positive effect on investment behavior, even after controlling for individual characteristics and measured preferences for interaction and equity (IEP).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a third-party punishment mechanism in a threshold public goods game increases group investment and the probability of achieving the public good. For prediction tasks, the presence of TPP (punishment enabled) should be expected to yield higher efficiency or group payoff than the control (punishment disabled), especially in small groups (n=3) with a threshold structure and automatic, impersonal punishment. The results are most directly applicable to threshold PGGs with automatic third-party punishment and no communication. The findings support a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency-related outcomes under these design conditions.

- source: Volk, S., Nguyen, H., & Thöni, C. (2019). Punishment under threat: The role of personality in costly punishment. *Journal of Research in Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that individuals high in Honesty-Humility engage in costly punishment for prosocial reasons, independent of threat, while those high in Emotionality engage in costly punishment in response to perceived punishment threat. Agreeableness does not predict punishment behavior. The experiment uses a two-person PGG with a punishment stage where each participant can punish a member of another team and can be punished by a different member of a third team, ruling out direct reciprocity. The main outcomes are assigned and expected punishment, both measured as deduction points. The paper does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, but discusses that punishment under threat is likely inefficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and personality drivers of costly punishment in a PGG-like environment with punishment threat, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. The design is a two-person PGG with continuous contributions, no communication, and a punishment stage where punishment is costly (1 token cost to punisher, 3 token loss to punished). The findings suggest that punishment can be driven by threat rather than prosocial motives, and the authors argue that such punishment is likely inefficient, but this is not empirically demonstrated. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that punishment under threat may not improve efficiency and may even reduce it, especially when driven by non-prosocial motives, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Selles, J., Bonhommeau, S., Guillotreau, P., & Vallee, T. (2020). Can the Threat of Economic Sanctions Ensure the Sustainability of International Fisheries? An Experiment of a Dynamic Non-cooperative CPR Game with Uncertain Tipping Point. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Introducing an endogenous economic sanction (a fixed cost triggered by resource depletion below a threshold) in a dynamic CPR game leads to more cooperative behavior, less overexploitation, and higher average resource stocks compared to a baseline with no sanction. The effect is even stronger when the location of the threshold is uncertain. However, while sanctions reduce the frequency of myopic (selfish) strategies, many groups still fail to avoid the threshold, and only a few achieve near-optimal resource management. Average group profit is lower and more variable in sanction treatments due to the high penalty for crossing the threshold, but overall, the threat of sanctions shifts behavior toward greater cooperation and less rent dissipation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (here, an economic sanction triggered by overexploitation) in a dynamic, non-cooperative CPR game increases cooperative behavior and reduces overexploitation, which should translate into higher efficiency relative to the baseline. However, the effect on group profit is nuanced: while sanctions reduce overexploitation, groups that cross the threshold incur large losses, and only a minority achieve near-optimal outcomes. For prediction, the presence of a punishment/sanction mechanism is likely to increase efficiency on average, but the effect may be heterogeneous depending on group behavior and the risk of triggering the sanction. The game design here is a dynamic CPR with 3 players, no chat, continuous contribution (harvest), and a punishment that is endogenous and collective (not peer-to-peer). The findings are most informative for similar dynamic CPR or threshold public goods settings.

- source: Kornhauser, L., Lu, Y. J., & Tontrup, S. (2020). Testing a fine is a price in the lab. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment shows that introducing a fine for non-compliance (either small or proportional) reduces compliance (performance) among pro-social subjects (crowding-out effect) and increases it among pro-self subjects (price effect). When the fine is framed as a penalty (collected by the experimenter, not the partner), the crowding-out effect disappears and pro-socials' compliance is restored. The overall mean performance across all subjects does not differ significantly between treatments with and without fines, due to the offsetting effects. The study provides strong evidence for the fine-is-a-price mechanism as the cause of crowding out, but does not measure or report group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment/fines on compliance in public-goods-like environments depends on the social value orientation of participants: fines crowd out compliance among pro-socials but increase it among pro-selfs. However, because the study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that the net effect of punishment on efficiency may be neutral in mixed populations, but could be negative in highly pro-social groups and positive in highly pro-self groups. The design and manipulation of punishment (including framing as price vs. penalty) are critical moderators. For prediction tasks, the absence of explicit efficiency data limits direct applicability, but the behavioral patterns are informative for understanding potential efficiency effects in similar designs.

- source: Dave, C., Hamre, S., Kephart, C., & Reuben, A. (2020). Subjects in the lab, activists in the field: public goods and punishment. *Eurasian Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that both lab and field (activist) subjects respond to incentives in a public goods context with punishment. Activists are more likely to punish non-cooperative behavior, even when it is costly and probabilistic, and are more likely to settle at the Nash equilibrium when firms increase their public good contributions. Higher MPCR leads to higher contributions and more frequent punishment. The main behavioral drivers are inequity aversion and negative reciprocity. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome, so the effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how punishment is used in a public-goods-like environment, especially with a costly, probabilistic punishment mechanism. It shows that punishment is frequently used and is responsive to MPCR and fairness concerns. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is most useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and the likelihood of punishment under different design parameters (e.g., MPCR, subject pool), but not for quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Selterman, D. (2019). Altruistic Punishment in the Classroom: An Update on the Tragedy of the Commons Extra Credit Question. *Teaching of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that adding an altruistic punishment option (students can sacrifice their own points to remove points from a random over-consumer) increases the rate at which groups successfully avoid the 'tragedy of the commons' (i.e., more classes stay under the 10% threshold and receive points) compared to prior years without punishment. However, the study does not directly measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The main findings are that the punishment option increases cooperation (fewer students choose the selfish option), and that students report positive attitudes and increased understanding of social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism in a threshold public goods dilemma can increase group cooperation and the likelihood of group success (i.e., receiving the reward). However, because the outcome is binary (group gets points or not) and not a continuous efficiency or payoff measure, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The evidence suggests that punishment can improve group outcomes in similar all-or-nothing, threshold-based social dilemmas, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency change. The design is also classroom-based and not a standard repeated PGG, so caution is warranted in generalizing to other settings.

- source: Chierchia, G., Lesemann, F. H. P., Snower, D., & Singer, T. (2021). Cooperation across multiple game theoretical paradigms is increased by fear more than anger in selfish individuals. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing
  findings: Induced fear, compared to anger, increases cooperation (as measured by a latent factor across multiple games, including the public good game) in individuals with selfish value orientations, bringing their cooperation levels up to those of prosocial individuals. No significant effect of emotion induction is found on punishment behavior. The effect is robust to controls for mood, risk attitudes, and other confounds. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that emotional context (fear vs. anger) can moderate cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially among selfish individuals. However, because the outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, punishment) rather than efficiency or payoff, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a treatment (i.e., enabling/disabling punishment), but rather includes punishment games as part of a behavioral factor. Thus, while the paper supports the idea that context and individual differences can moderate cooperation and punishment, it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Tsvetkova, M., Wagner, C., & Mao, A. (2018). The emergence of inequality in social groups: Network structure and institutions affect the distribution of earnings in cooperation games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that enabling punishment in networked cooperation games (including PGG-like games) increases inequality in the distribution of final wealth, as measured by the Gini coefficient. This is because the cost of punishment is disproportionately borne by poorer players, who become even poorer as a result. However, the paper does not find a systematic relationship between the presence of punishment and group average wealth (i.e., efficiency or total payoff). Thus, while punishment increases cooperation and inequality, it does not reliably increase or decrease group efficiency. The main focus is on the distributional consequences (inequality), not on efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in PGG-like networked cooperation games increases inequality in final wealth, but does not provide evidence that punishment systematically increases or decreases group efficiency (total payoff relative to full cooperation). For the downstream prediction task, this means that the presence of punishment should not be assumed to improve or worsen efficiency based on this evidence alone. The main effect is on the distribution of payoffs, not their sum. If the prediction task is about efficiency, this paper suggests that punishment's effect is likely neutral or context-dependent, and that other design dimensions (e.g., network structure, fluidity) may matter more for efficiency than for inequality.

- source: Chávez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2018). Managing and defending the commons: Experimental evidence from TURFs in Chile. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common-pool resource game with insiders (who can communicate) and outsiders (who cannot), the introduction of poaching without monitoring or sanctions dramatically reduces insiders' earnings and resource sustainability, nearly eliminating the efficiency gains from cooperation. Adding weak external enforcement (low-probability monitoring and fines) or allowing insiders to invest in monitoring (with the same fine) both significantly reduce poaching and increase insiders' earnings and harvests, but do not fully restore efficiency to baseline (no-poaching) levels. Combining external and local monitoring does not further reduce poaching or increase efficiency. The main constraint is insiders' inability to coordinate sufficient monitoring investment. The results are robust across field (TURF members) and student samples.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a dynamic CPR game with communication among insiders, the introduction of punishment (via monitoring and fines for poaching) increases group efficiency relative to a poaching-only control, but the effect is partial and depends on the ability to coordinate monitoring. The effect is similar whether monitoring is provided externally or by insiders, and combining both does not yield additional efficiency. For prediction, the presence of punishment (even if weak) should be expected to increase efficiency above the poaching-only baseline, but not to the level of the no-poaching or full-cooperation baseline. The magnitude of the effect is limited by the monitoring probability and the coordination of monitoring investment.

- source: Makowsky, M. D., & Wang, S. Y. (2018). Embezzlement, whistleblowing, and organizational architecture: An experimental investigation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a laboratory experiment with 6-player groups, participants played a common pool resource game with a whistleblowing option: after each withdrawal, any non-first-mover could choose to 'blow the whistle', setting all group payoffs to zero. The game was played under six different organizational structures, varying the number of sequential levels (from 1 to 6) and the arrangement of players (pyramid, inverted pyramid, flat, etc.). The main findings are: (1) Embezzlement rates are high in all structures, but increase with the number of sequential levels; (2) The probability of group-level punishment (whistleblowing) increases with the number of levels; (3) Average group income (efficiency) decreases as the number of levels increases; (4) Flatter (simultaneous) structures have higher efficiency than taller (sequential) ones; (5) Pyramid structures have lower embezzlement than inverted pyramids, but this does not translate into significant efficiency differences due to similar rates of group-level punishment. The punishment mechanism is highly costly and does not improve efficiency; rather, it reduces it further when triggered.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with a costly, group-level punishment mechanism (whistleblowing that sets all payoffs to zero), enabling punishment does not improve efficiency and may reduce it, especially as the game becomes more sequential (taller). The efficiency loss is driven by both high embezzlement and the destructive nature of the punishment. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, when the control (no-punishment) game already has low efficiency due to high embezzlement, adding a highly destructive punishment option (like whistleblowing) is likely to further reduce efficiency, particularly in games with more sequential moves or more levels. The design dimensions most relevant are the number of players, number of rounds, the structure of play (sequential vs. simultaneous), and the nature/cost of punishment. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment is costly and group-level (not targeted), and less so to standard peer-to-peer punishment in classic PGGs.

- source: Littman, R., Estrada, S., Stagnaro, M. N., Dunham, Y., Rand, D., & Baskin-Sommers, A. (2020). Community Violence and Prosociality: Experiencing and Committing Violence Predicts Norm-Enforcing Punishment but Not Cooperation. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in a high-violence urban US sample, exposure to and commission of violence are not associated with increased cooperation in economic games (including a Public Goods Game), but are positively associated with norm-enforcing punishment (i.e., willingness to pay to punish selfish others in economic games). The association is specific to norm-enforcing punishment and not to antisocial punishment or general willingness to punish. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. However, it does provide indirect evidence that, in a US urban context, individuals with higher exposure to or commission of violence are more likely to engage in norm-enforcing punishment, but not more likely to cooperate. This suggests that the presence of punishment may not increase cooperation or efficiency in similar populations, but the study does not test the causal effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The design and results are most relevant for understanding individual differences in punishment and cooperation, not for predicting treatment effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Bonroy, O., Garapin, A., Hamilton, S. F., & Monteiro, D. M. S. (2019). FREE-RIDING ON PRODUCT QUALITY IN COOPERATIVES: LESSONS FROM AN EXPERIMENT. *American Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing the probability of exclusion (punishment) significantly reduces free-riding (shirking) in larger groups, and that increasing the base payoff (independent of collective returns) also reduces free-riding. In small groups, free-riding is low regardless of punishment. The main outcome is the frequency of free-riding, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that punishment (exclusion) and higher base payoffs can facilitate cooperation, but the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency must be inferred.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment (in the form of exclusion) reduces free-riding in public-goods-like environments, especially in larger groups. However, because the main outcome is behavioral (frequency of free-riding) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings must be mapped to efficiency outcomes indirectly. The experimental design and manipulation of group size, punishment probability, and base payoff are informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data is a limitation. The results support the prediction that enabling punishment will increase efficiency in larger groups, especially when the probability of punishment is substantial.

- source: Bastian, B., Brewer, M., Duffy, J., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2019). From cash to crickets: The non-monetary value of a resource can promote human cooperation. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that highlighting the non-monetary value of a resource (e.g., the moral significance of not harming crickets) leads to reduced personal consumption in a resource dilemma game, indicating increased cooperation. This effect is robust across two studies and is not moderated by individual differences in empathy or moral expansiveness. When a punishment mechanism is introduced (participants can pay to fine a greedy player), there is no significant difference in punishment behavior between the moral-value and control conditions. The main outcome is behavioral (points taken), not direct group efficiency or payoff, but the results suggest that moral framing can promote cooperation even in the presence of defection and punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that moral or non-monetary framing of the resource can increase cooperation (reduce consumption) in resource dilemma games, which may translate to higher group efficiency if mapped to payoff outcomes. However, the study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, and the punishment mechanism (costly punishment of defectors) did not significantly affect behavior. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that game design features that increase the perceived moral value of the resource may enhance cooperation, but the effect of punishment per se on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper. The design is close to a public goods/common pool resource game, but not a standard PGG, and the main outcomes are not efficiency or payoff-based.

- source: Solda, A., & Villeval, M. C. (2020). EXCLUSION AND REINTEGRATION IN A SOCIAL DILEMMA. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment investigates endogenous and exogenous exclusion (ostracism) in a club-good setting where group members can vote to exclude others who over-extract from the group resource. Exclusion is primarily targeted at those who withdraw more than the group average, supporting the idea that exclusion enforces group norms. Upon reintegration, previously excluded members tend to reduce their extraction, indicating some deterrent or corrective effect of exclusion. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total group payoff, or welfare, so the effect of exclusion on efficiency cannot be directly assessed. The findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral responses to exclusion and the use of ostracism as a sanction, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the behavioral effects of exclusion (ostracism) as a sanctioning mechanism in public-good-like environments. It shows that exclusion can deter norm violations (over-extraction) and that reintegrated members may adjust their behavior. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment (exclusion) on efficiency. The design dimensions (e.g., group size, exclusion rules) are described, but their effects are only analyzed in terms of behavioral outcomes, not efficiency. Thus, this paper is useful for understanding the mechanisms and behavioral pathways by which exclusion might affect efficiency, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Billinger, S., & Rosenbaum, S. M. (2019). Discretionary mechanisms and cooperation in hierarchies: An experimental study. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment compares a baseline PGG (no hierarchy) to four HPGG treatments varying in whether managers can contribute (discretionary contribution: present/absent) and whether managers can allocate returns (discretionary return: static/dynamic). All HPGG treatments yield higher average contributions and accumulated earnings than the baseline, indicating that hierarchy and managerial discretion can sustain cooperation and improve efficiency. However, when managers have full discretion over returns but cannot contribute (AD treatment), both contributions and worker earnings are lowest among HPGG treatments, showing that unchecked managerial appropriation undermines efficiency. When managers can contribute, both group earnings and cooperation are higher and more stable over time. Managers consistently earn more than workers, especially in dynamic return treatments, due to 'strategic luring'—managers initially reward workers to elicit contributions, then appropriate more in later rounds. The design of discretionary mechanisms thus crucially shapes both efficiency and the distribution of surplus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing hierarchical mechanisms (managerial discretion over inputs and/or outputs) in a PGG-like environment increases group efficiency relative to a standard PGG baseline. However, the efficiency gains depend on the specific design: allowing managers to contribute and/or restricting their ability to appropriate returns leads to higher and more sustained efficiency. If managers can appropriate returns without contributing, efficiency and worker payoffs suffer. For prediction, the presence of hierarchy and the structure of managerial discretion are key moderators: enabling managerial contribution and limiting appropriation both support higher efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially in settings with hierarchical or leader-follower structures.

- source: Deffains, B., Espinosa, R., & Fluet, C. (2019). Laws and norms: Experimental evidence with liability rules. *International Review of Law and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally studies a 'Liability Game' that is structurally similar to a public goods game but with a binary choice (harmful vs harmless action) and state-dependent payoffs, so that sometimes the harmful action is efficient and sometimes not. The main treatments compare no law (no liability), strict liability, and negligence rule, each with perfect (Severe) or imperfect (Mild, 50% probability) enforcement. The key findings are: (1) Both strict liability and negligence rules increase efficiency relative to no law, even when enforcement is imperfect and sanctions are not fully deterrent. (2) Strict liability is more effective than the negligence rule at deterring welfare-decreasing actions under imperfect enforcement, but also deters some welfare-increasing actions (over-deterrence). (3) The presence of liability rules increases the weight individuals place on group welfare in their decision-making, beyond the direct material incentives. (4) Social disapproval (punishment) is mainly targeted at those who cause harm, regardless of whether the action was efficient or inefficient. (5) Informal sanctions (disapproval points) further reduce harmful actions, but the main efficiency gains come from the liability rules themselves.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment-like liability rule in a public-goods-like environment increases efficiency, even when enforcement is imperfect and the expected sanction is not fully deterrent. The effect is robust across two types of liability rules (strict and negligence), but strict liability is more effective at deterring welfare-decreasing actions (at the cost of some over-deterrence). The game design is a close variant of a PGG: 4 players, 10 rounds per treatment, binary choice, no chat, and state-dependent payoffs. The efficiency gains are not solely due to material deterrence but also to increased salience of group welfare (norm activation). For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment (liability) will increase efficiency relative to control, even if punishment is not fully effective or is only probabilistically applied. The magnitude of the effect may depend on whether the punishment is strict (always for harm) or negligence-based (only for inefficient harm), and on the enforcement probability.

- source: Kusterer, D. J., & Schmitz, P. W. (2020). Public goods, property rights, and investment incentives: An experimental investigation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that ownership structure affects investment incentives and efficiency in public-good provision, but not always as predicted by theory. When the investor values the public good more, giving them ownership increases investment and group surplus. When the non-investor values the public good more, giving them ownership does not increase investment or surplus, because the investor-owner sometimes withholds the public good to punish the other party, reducing efficiency. When the possibility of such punishment is removed (by exogenously enforcing provision), investment and efficiency fall in line with theoretical predictions. Thus, the efficiency effect of ownership and the potential for punishment are intertwined, and the ability to punish by withholding provision can reduce efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency effect of institutional design (ownership allocation) in public-good games depends on whether players can punish by withholding provision. In environments where the owner can withhold the public good as punishment, efficiency may be lower than predicted by standard theory, even if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is high. If the design disables such punishment (e.g., by enforcing provision), efficiency aligns with theoretical predictions. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of punishment opportunities (even if not explicit costly punishment) is a key moderator. The findings are most relevant for two-player, sequential, bargaining-based public-good games with binary investment and provision decisions.

- source: Brent, D. A., Gangadharan, L., Mihut, A., & Villeval, M. C. (2019). Taxation, redistribution, and observability in social dilemmas. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Both Pigovian tax and increasing block rate mechanisms, when combined with redistribution, lead to significantly higher group efficiency (mean payoff as a ratio to the social optimum increases from 0.86 in baseline to 0.97–0.98 with tax, p<0.01) and lower inequality, regardless of whether individual decisions are observable. Observability alone (without tax) increases extraction and reduces efficiency (mean payoff ratio drops to 0.82), contrary to the expectation that it would promote cooperation. When tax mechanisms are present, observability has no significant effect on efficiency or extraction. There is no significant difference in efficiency between the two tax mechanisms, but the Pigovian tax reduces inequality more. The results are robust across group types and over time.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a CPR environment with heterogeneous extraction capacities, introducing a monetary sanction mechanism (taxation with redistribution) substantially increases efficiency compared to a baseline with no sanctions, regardless of whether decisions are observable. The effect is robust to the presence or absence of observability (i.e., information about others' actions). The design is not a standard linear PGG, but the CPR game is closely related and the efficiency outcome is directly comparable. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling a monetary sanction mechanism (taxation) will predictably increase efficiency, and the effect is not moderated by observability. The results do not speak directly to peer-to-peer punishment, but do inform the effect of centralized monetary sanctions.

- source: Cagala, T., Glogowsky, U., Grimm, V., & Rincke, J. (2019). PUBLIC GOODS PROVISION WITH RENT-EXTRACTING ADMINISTRATORS. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study introduces a 'public trust game' (PTG), a repeated public goods game with an administrator who can extract rents from the contribution pool. The main finding is that the presence of a rent-extracting administrator reduces contributions and efficiency, but this is entirely explained by the reduction in MPCR (i.e., the efficiency of the public good). When comparing the PTG to a standard PGG with the same (lowered) MPCR, there is no significant difference in contributions or efficiency. The dynamics show that rent-extraction shocks and cooperation shocks only have short-run effects; the system returns to its previous level of cooperation and efficiency. The volatility of contributions is mainly driven by contributors' own behavior, not by the administrator. Groups with higher trust and cooperative attitudes experience less short-run volatility in cooperation after shocks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments where an administrator can extract rents, the negative effect on efficiency is fully mediated by the reduction in MPCR. If the control game (standard PGG) and the treatment game (with administrator) have the same MPCR, the presence of the administrator does not further reduce efficiency. Thus, for prediction, the key moderator is the effective MPCR: punishment or rent extraction that reduces MPCR will reduce efficiency accordingly, but the mere presence of an administrator or rent-extraction opportunity does not further depress efficiency beyond this. The paper does not study punishment in the standard sense (peer or centralized costly punishment), but its findings are highly informative for cases where punishment or corruption reduces the effective return from cooperation.

- source: Gross, J., & Böhm, R. (2020). Voluntary restrictions on self-reliance increase cooperation and mitigate wealth inequality. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can solve a collective action problem either individually (self-reliance) or collectively (public good), the availability of self-reliance crowds out cooperation and increases inequality, especially when self-reliance is relatively cheap. However, when groups are given the opportunity to vote to abolish the individual solution, they overwhelmingly do so under medium and high interdependence, which leads to higher rates of public goods provision, lower resource waste, higher group earnings, and reduced inequality. The effect is particularly strong when there is a risk of miscoordination (medium interdependence) or when group members differ in their ability to be self-reliant (wealth heterogeneity). The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in the standard sense, but self-reliance by richer group members can function as a coercive device, pressuring poorer members to cooperate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional design features that restrict individual opt-out/self-reliance options can substantially increase efficiency and group earnings in public-goods-like environments, especially when the efficiency gains from cooperation are significant (i.e., when self-reliance is costly relative to the public good). While the study does not manipulate punishment, it shows that endogenous institutional restrictions (analogous to mandatory participation or exclusion of exit options) can have large positive effects on efficiency, similar to or greater than those observed for punishment in standard PGGs. For prediction, the presence or absence of self-reliance/exit options, and the ability to restrict them, should be considered as key moderators of efficiency, alongside standard game design dimensions.

- source: Leibbrandt, A., Puerto, S., & Velez, M. A. (2021). Regrouping to Reduce Overfishing: Evidence from a Series of Lab-in-the-Field Experiments in Mexico. *Marine Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a framed CPR lab-in-the-field experiment with real fishermen, regrouping participants by their past extraction levels (publicly ranking and then reassigning groups) leads to a significant reduction in individual and group extraction, both in the observation period (after announcement) and after regrouping. The effect is strongest for the most sustainable group but is present across all groups. Compliance with the quota increases, especially in the most sustainable group. Other interventions (social disapproval, venting, moral priming) do not reduce extraction. The control group shows no reduction in extraction. The results suggest that symbolic recognition and group reassignment can reduce overexploitation and improve compliance, indirectly improving efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that regrouping (a form of symbolic reward and group reassignment based on past behavior) can reduce overexploitation in CPR games, even in field-relevant settings. However, it does not test costly punishment or direct sanctioning mechanisms. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of non-costly, symbolic, or reputational interventions rather than monetary punishment. The game design is close to a public goods game but is a CPR extraction game. Efficiency is not directly measured, but reductions in extraction and increased compliance imply improved group welfare. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that interventions leveraging social recognition and group identity can have positive effects on efficiency, especially when punishment is weak or absent.

- source: Stoddard, B. V., Cox, C. A., & Walker, J. M. (2021). Incentivizing provision of collective goods: Allocation rules. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: In a lab experiment with repeated collective good provision games, the introduction of a third-party allocator who can flexibly assign shares of the group fund (based on observed contributions) leads to higher group provision than a baseline with equal sharing. The two most flexible allocator mechanisms (PA-Flex and PA-Rules) both result in significantly higher provision than the baseline (PE-PE), and also outperform a less flexible allocator mechanism (PA-SWW). The highest levels of provision are observed in the most flexible mechanism (PA-Flex), though a small number of groups with ineffective allocators show low provision. The study does not directly report efficiency as a normalized payoff ratio, but group provision is closely related and higher provision implies higher group payoffs. The findings indicate that flexible allocation rules that allow for contribution-contingent rewards and punishments can strongly increase collective good provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments where a third-party allocator can flexibly assign shares of the collective good based on contributions, group provision (and thus efficiency) increases substantially compared to equal sharing. The effect is robust across two flexible allocator mechanisms and is strongest when the allocator has maximal flexibility. The results suggest that, when predicting the effect of introducing a flexible, contribution-contingent allocation mechanism (a form of endogenous reward/punishment), one should expect a large positive effect on group efficiency, provided the allocator uses the mechanism effectively. However, the effect may be heterogeneous if some allocators do not use the mechanism to incentivize contributions. The study does not use classic peer punishment, but the allocation mechanism serves a similar incentive function. The findings are most directly relevant for settings where a third party can allocate payoffs after observing contributions, rather than direct peer-to-peer punishment.

- source: Angelovski, A., Neugebauer, T., & Servátka, M. (2019). RANK-ORDER COMPETITION IN THE VOLUNTARY PROVISION OF IMPURE PUBLIC GOODS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Rank-Order-VCM, which rewards higher contributors with a larger share of the public good (via a higher personal multiplier), leads to significantly higher and more sustained contributions than a control treatment with random assignment of multipliers. In Rank-Order-VCM, median contributions are at the maximum in almost all rounds, while in the control, contributions decline to near zero. The mechanism avoids the efficiency loss of fixed-prize contests by not subtracting a prize from the pool.
