The results suggest that introducing rank-based competition for access to an impure public good can mitigate free-riding and move outcomes closer to the social optimum, increasing group welfare and implied efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a rank-based competition mechanism (where higher contributors receive a larger share of the public good) can dramatically increase contributions and implied efficiency compared to a control with random assignment of benefits. While the main reported outcomes are contributions, the payoff structure ensures that higher contributions translate into higher group payoffs and efficiency, especially since no costly prize is subtracted from the pool. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that mechanisms which create endogenous, contribution-dependent rewards (even without explicit punishment or costly rewards) can outperform standard VCMs and fixed-prize contests in terms of efficiency. However, the results are most directly applicable to impure public goods with preferential access, not to standard PGGs with explicit punishment or peer-sanctioning. The control efficiency (with random assignment) is much lower than in the treatment, so the predicted treatment efficiency should be substantially higher under similar design parameters.

- source: Chen, J., Fonseca, M. A., & Grimshaw, S. B. (2021). When a nudge is (not) enough: Experiments on social information and incentives. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In the large-N binary prisoners' dilemma, both financial incentives (reward or fine) and positive social information (majority invest) increase cooperation rates among unmotivated types. Negative social information (majority keep) reduces cooperation among motivated types. The combination of incentives and positive social information does not produce additional gains for unmotivated types, and may even crowd out intrinsic motivation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results imply that punishment/reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates, especially among those not intrinsically motivated to cooperate. The effect of social information is context-dependent: it is more effective when the externality of cooperation is salient and financially quantifiable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about the effect of punishment/reward mechanisms and social information on cooperation rates in a large-N binary prisoners' dilemma with MPCR = 0.05. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the increase in cooperation rates from punishment/reward treatments among unmotivated types suggests that enabling punishment/reward would likely increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is stronger when the externality is salient. The design dimensions (large group, binary choice, no chat, no explicit payoff feedback beyond own outcome, known group size, no identification of punishers) are all specified. The absence of direct efficiency data limits quantitative prediction, but the qualitative direction is clear: punishment/reward increases cooperation and likely efficiency, especially among defectors.

- source: Ramalingam, A., Stoddard, B. V., & Walker, J. M. (2019). The market for talent: Competition for resources and self-governance in teams. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Competition for a common-member's contributions creates 'winners' and 'losers': groups that start with higher contributions retain the common-member's loyalty and sustain higher contributions, while low-contribution groups decline further. Providing dedicated-members with more information about the common-member's contributions increases the common-member's total contributions and reduces discrimination between groups, but does not significantly raise dedicated-members' contributions in low-performing groups. Allowing ostracism (costless exclusion by majority vote) leads to higher and more equal contributions across groups, as both common- and dedicated-members increase contributions and the threat of exclusion disciplines low contributors. Ostracism is targeted at low contributors and rarely used against high contributors. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment-like mechanisms (ostracism) and information on contribution behavior in public goods games with divided loyalties. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the findings suggest that ostracism (a form of endogenous, costless group-level punishment) can increase and equalize contributions across groups, especially in settings where initial disparities would otherwise persist. The results also show that information about others' contributions can increase contributions by pivotal members. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling ostracism or increasing transparency may improve group outcomes in public goods games, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Becchetti, L., Pelligra, V., & Salustri, F. (2018). The impact of redistribution mechanisms in the vote with the wallet game: experimental results. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of an ex post redistribution mechanism (taxing defectors and subsidizing cooperators) in a repeated, all-or-nothing public good game significantly increases the share of cooperative choices compared to the baseline. In the baseline, cooperation decays over time, but with redistribution, cooperation is stabilized at a higher level. Framing the responsible product as socially beneficial also increases cooperation, but the effect of redistribution is robust across frames. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect on efficiency cannot be directly determined, but the increase in cooperation rates suggests likely higher group payoffs under redistribution.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a centralized, ex post redistribution mechanism (akin to a tax/subsidy rather than peer punishment) can substantially increase cooperation rates in a repeated, all-or-nothing public good game with 10 players and no communication. While efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the structure of the payoffs and the increase in cooperation rates imply that group efficiency would also increase under the redistribution mechanism. The findings are most directly relevant for predicting the effect of centralized, automatic sanction/reward mechanisms (not peer punishment) in all-or-nothing, no-communication, 10-player repeated games with known rounds and MPCR of 0.3. The results are less directly applicable to standard peer punishment settings, but suggest that institutionalized redistribution can be highly effective at sustaining cooperation.

- source: Secchi, S., & Banerjee, S. (2019). A dynamic semester-long social dilemma game for economic and interdisciplinary courses. *Journal of Economic Education*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that students, when given the opportunity, may devise and implement their own institutional solutions to the common-pool resource dilemma, including costly peer punishment. The game is effective for teaching about the dynamics of cooperation, cheating, and institutional innovation in resource management. However, the paper does not provide quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically compare outcomes with and without punishment. The main findings are qualitative and pedagogical, emphasizing the diversity of student-devised solutions and the context-dependence of group behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. While it confirms that peer punishment can emerge and is sometimes used by participants to address over-extraction, it does not provide quantitative or systematic evidence on its impact on efficiency or group payoff. The game design dimensions are described in detail, but the effect of punishment is only discussed anecdotally. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that allowing for endogenous punishment mechanisms can lead to creative solutions and potentially improved group outcomes, but does not provide data to calibrate the size or direction of the effect on efficiency.

- source: Gomez-Ruiz, L., & Sánchez-Expósito, M. J. (2020). The Impact of Team Identity and Gender on Free-Riding Responses to Fear and Cooperation Sustainability. *Sustainability*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that strong team identity significantly reduces free-riding responses to fear and increases cooperation rates among men, but not among women. The effect of team identity on cooperation sustainability (maintenance of cooperation over time) is marginally significant for men and not significant for women. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution rates, free-riding responses), not efficiency or group payoff. No formal punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present; the study focuses on informal control (team identity) and gender differences. The findings suggest that team identity can be an effective informal mechanism to sustain cooperation among men in repeated social dilemmas with binary contribution choices and feedback, but not among women.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It studies a repeated binary-choice social dilemma (close to a public goods game) without any punishment or reward mechanism. The main manipulations are team identity and gender. The outcomes are behavioral (contribution rates, free-riding responses), not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does provide contextual evidence about the role of informal mechanisms (team identity) and gender in sustaining cooperation. It may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation rates in binary-choice, feedback-rich, no-punishment settings, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: De Luca, G., Sekeris, P. G., & Spengler, D. E. (2018). Can violence harm cooperation? Experimental evidence. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares two treatments: a 'conflict' treatment where violent appropriation (chance) is a viable option when resources are low, and a 'control' treatment where the cost of appropriation is so high that it is never optimal. In the conflict treatment, participants are significantly less likely to choose the cooperative extraction rate ('low') and more likely to choose the non-cooperative rate ('high') in early rounds, anticipating future conflict. The expectation of future conflict thus undermines cooperation even before conflict occurs. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral shift from cooperation to non-cooperation implies lower efficiency in the conflict treatment. Additional analyses show that experiencing conflict in previous games further reduces cooperation in subsequent games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a credible, destructive appropriation/conflict option in a dynamic CPR game reduces cooperative behavior and likely reduces efficiency, even before conflict is actually chosen. For prediction tasks, the presence of a credible destructive sanction (even if not a standard punishment mechanism) should be expected to reduce efficiency relative to a control with no such option. However, the paper does not study standard punishment (costly peer punishment for norm violations) but rather a destructive appropriation/conflict option, so the results are most relevant for settings where punishment is destructive and ends the game, not for standard public goods games with ongoing punishment. The findings suggest that the threat of destructive sanctions can unravel cooperation and efficiency, especially when the sanction is credible and not prohibitively costly.

- source: Gürdal, M. Y., Torul, O., & Vostroknutov, A. (2020). Norm compliance, enforcement, and the survival of redistributive institutions. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in the absence of enforcement, contributions to redistribution decay rapidly due to free riding, even among individuals with a high propensity to follow rules. Exclusion (random checks with exclusion for non-compliance) slows the decay but does not prevent it. Only full enforcement (computer-enforced contributions) sustains high contributions and reduces post-tax income inequality. Rule-following individuals initially try to follow the rules but eventually reduce contributions in response to free riding. Rule-followers prefer institutions with strong rules when enforcement is present, but switch to unregulated institutions when enforcement is absent and free riding is prevalent. The paper reports post-tax income and Gini coefficients, but does not directly analyze efficiency as group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum. The findings suggest that some form of enforcement is necessary to sustain redistribution and prevent the collapse of cooperative institutions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of enforcement (a form of exogenous punishment) on group outcomes in public-goods-like environments. While it does not implement a classic costly punishment mechanism, it shows that enforcement (computer-enforced contributions) is necessary to sustain high contributions and reduce inequality; exclusion as a sanction slows but does not prevent decay. In the absence of enforcement, contributions and redistribution collapse. The results suggest that, in similar designs, enabling effective enforcement or punishment is likely to increase group welfare and reduce inequality, but the paper does not report efficiency in the sense of group payoff relative to the full cooperation benchmark. The findings are most informative for settings where enforcement is exogenous and not costly to the enforcer. The evidence is less directly applicable to classic peer punishment with explicit costs. Control efficiency (without enforcement) is low due to free riding; treatment efficiency (with enforcement) is likely much higher, but the exact ratio is not reported.

- source: Cavalcanti, C. (2020). On the Determinants of Denouncing Illegal Fishing: A Field Study in Artisanal Fishing Communities. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that fishermen who are more impatient (prefer immediate rewards in a lab task) and those who are more pro-social (contribute more in a public goods game) are more likely to report a willingness to denounce others for illegal fishing. The willingness to denounce is interpreted as a form of adaptive altruistic punishment, but is measured via self-report rather than observed behavior. No direct measures of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare are reported. The public goods game is used only as a measure of pro-sociality, not as a treatment or outcome. The study provides correlational evidence on individual differences in punishment propensity, not on the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate punishment as a game feature, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency outcomes. Instead, it provides correlational evidence that individual differences in impatience and pro-sociality predict willingness to denounce norm violations in a real-world resource dilemma. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, suggesting that individual differences may moderate punishment behavior, but not providing evidence on the efficiency consequences of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Tambunlertchai, K., & Pongkijvorasin, S. (2020). The impacts of collective threshold requirements for rewards in a CPR experiment. *Environmental Economics and Policy Studies*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Introducing collective-threshold-based rewards in a CPR game with villagers in Thailand leads to lower resource extraction and higher payoffs compared to the control (no reward) condition. The strict and moderate threshold treatments are most effective at reducing extraction and increasing payoffs, but differ in cost efficiency and equity: strict thresholds are most cost-efficient but induce free-riding and high payoff variance, while moderate thresholds are nearly as effective but yield more equitable payoff distributions. Relaxed thresholds are less effective. Increasing the reward size does not affect long-run extraction or payoffs, but accelerates the achievement of group targets. The study provides direct evidence on how reward design (threshold and size) affects efficiency and payoff outcomes in CPR settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in CPR games with collective-threshold-based rewards (but no punishment), efficiency and group payoffs increase relative to control. The effect is moderated by the threshold level: strict and moderate thresholds are most effective, with strict thresholds being most cost-efficient but less equitable. Reward size mainly affects the speed of cooperation, not long-run efficiency. For prediction, the absence of punishment and the presence of collective-threshold rewards should be expected to yield higher efficiency than control, especially under strict or moderate thresholds. The findings are directly relevant for settings with group-based positive incentives but do not inform about punishment effects.

- source: Zhang, Q. H., Chen, Y. Y., Tao, Y., Farid, T., & Ma, J. H. (2019). How Consistent Contributors Inspire Individuals to Cooperate: The Role of Moral Elevation and Social Value Orientation. *Sustainability*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This study demonstrates that introducing a consistent contributor (CC) into a step-level public-goods-dilemma game increases both cooperation and group/individual earnings. The effect is robust across both pro-social and pro-self participants, though the underlying psychological mechanisms differ: pro-selfs are influenced via increased moral elevation, while pro-socials respond directly to the CC's behavior. The design does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms, but provides strong evidence that motivational (role model) interventions can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanction mechanisms, introducing a consistent contributor (role model) can increase group efficiency (payoff) in step-level public-goods games. The findings are most relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar motivational interventions, but do not inform the effect of punishment. The design and results can serve as a control or baseline for understanding efficiency in the absence of punishment, and highlight the potential for non-punitive mechanisms to improve outcomes.

- source: Gross, J., Veistola, S., De Dreu, C. K. W., & Van Dijk, E. (2020). Self-reliance crowds out group cooperation and increases wealth inequality. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper experimentally demonstrates that when group members can solve a shared problem either through a public good (cooperation) or by investing in a private solution (self-reliance), the availability and affordability of private solutions lead to a decline in cooperation and group efficiency. The effect is stronger when there is asymmetry in endowments, such that some group members can more easily afford self-reliance. In these cases, less dependent members often choose private solutions, leaving more dependent members at risk of losing resources, which increases wealth inequality. When private solutions are unavailable, groups are able to efficiently coordinate on the public good, maximizing group welfare. The study also finds that third-party delegation would increase group welfare and reduce inequality, but is not often chosen by those who benefit from self-reliance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing the option for self-reliance (private solutions) in public goods games reduces group efficiency and increases inequality, especially when there is heterogeneity in members' ability to be self-reliant. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling private solutions (a form of exit or outside option) will lower the efficiency of the group compared to a standard public goods game without such options. The results are directly relevant for settings where the design dimension is the presence or absence of private alternatives, but do not address punishment or sanction mechanisms. The findings are most informative for predicting efficiency in games with and without self-reliance options, and for understanding the impact of resource asymmetry.

- source: Espinosa, M. P., Fatás, E., & Ubeda, P. (2019). Linguistic diversity and out-group discrimination in bilingual societies. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that the effect of ethno-linguistic diversity on efficiency in public goods provision is context-dependent. In Bilbao, diversity (mixed-language groups) leads to significantly lower contributions to the global public good and lower earnings, indicating reduced efficiency. In Valencia, diversity has a positive or neutral effect on global public good contributions and earnings, especially among Catalan speakers. The results are robust to controls for individualism/collectivism and are supported by belief elicitation in a follow-up experiment, which shows that negative effects in Bilbao are associated with normative tolerance for out-group discrimination, while positive effects in Valencia are linked to optimistic expectations about out-group cooperation. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment, the effect of diversity on efficiency in public goods games depends on the level of underlying group conflict. The nested public goods game is structurally similar to a standard PGG but with a local/global allocation choice. The findings suggest that, when predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) games, group composition and social context (e.g., group conflict) are important moderators. However, because there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design, this paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the role of diversity, but not for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Zhang, Y., & He, L. F. (2021). Theory and experiments on network games of public goods: inequality aversion and welfare preference. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that in network public goods games with a dominant strategy, subjects systematically contribute more than the self-interest level, resulting in higher group payoffs and welfare. This over-contribution is best explained by a welfare preference model, where individuals care about the payoffs of their neighbors. Inequality aversion explains some behavioral patterns but is less predictive overall. The effect of social preferences is moderated by network structure (star, circle, complete) and by the level of economic return: welfare preference dominates in star networks and at low returns, while self-interest becomes more important in circle networks and at high returns. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present or studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning is present in the design. However, it provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency can be higher than predicted by self-interest due to social preferences, especially welfare preference. The findings suggest that network structure and the level of economic return moderate the extent of over-contribution and efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper is informative for estimating control (no-punishment) efficiency in networked public goods games, but not for the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Nockur, L., Arndt, L., Keller, J., & Pfattheicher, S. (2020). Collective choice fosters sustainable resource management in the presence of asymmetric opportunities. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: In a lab common resource game with groups of four, participants played under three systems: individual choice, median choice, and majority voting. In both symmetric and asymmetric opportunity conditions, collective choice systems (median and majority voting) led to lower average extraction rates and higher profits than individual choice. The effect was especially pronounced for disadvantaged group members in asymmetric groups, whose profits increased substantially under collective choice. Advantaged group members' extraction was curtailed under collective choice, but their profits did not increase. Satisfaction and fairness ratings were also higher under collective choice. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms were present in any condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in common resource games with or without asymmetric extraction opportunities, implementing collective choice mechanisms (median or majority voting) increases efficiency and group payoff compared to individual choice. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of collective decision rules (not punishment) on efficiency, and less relevant for the downstream task of predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game structure is close to a public goods dilemma, but the absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled settings.

- source: Caria, A. S., & Fafchamps, M. (2019). Expectations, network centrality, and public good contributions: Experimental evidence from India. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that central players do not contribute more than others, despite being much more efficient at generating public good. This results in only about half of the potential efficiency being realized. Disclosing group expectations (elicited from participants) increases the likelihood that central players match those expectations, but does not increase average contributions or efficiency overall, except in groups with weak social ties, where a significant positive effect is observed. The dominant behavioral pattern is conditional cooperation, even when efficiency and equality would require higher contributions from the centre. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, even highly efficient central players do not contribute more than others, resulting in substantial efficiency losses. The findings suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is limited by conditional cooperation norms, not by the potential for high-impact contributions. The effect of expectation disclosure is context-dependent and does not generally increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper provides a clear control case for games with no punishment: efficiency is about 50% of the potential maximum, even with strong efficiency incentives for some players. The absence of punishment is critical—adding punishment would likely change these outcomes, but this paper does not provide direct evidence on that.

- source: Hackel, J., Yamamoto, H., Okada, I., Goto, A., & Taudes, A. (2021). Asymmetric effects of social and economic incentives on cooperation in real effort based public goods games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that social incentives (charity donations) significantly increase the proportion of participants who fully cooperate (complete 90+ tasks), while economic incentives (personal monetary reward) significantly reduce the proportion of full defectors (complete 10 or fewer tasks). The effects are asymmetric: social incentives promote high cooperation, economic incentives reduce non-cooperation. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, focusing instead on positive incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. The design is a close variant of a PGG, using real effort instead of resource allocation, and focuses on the effects of positive (economic and social) incentives on cooperation and defection rates. For prediction tasks about punishment and efficiency, this paper is not directly informative, but it does suggest that incentive structures (other than punishment) can have asymmetric effects on cooperation and defection. The absence of punishment and the focus on non-payoff behavioral outcomes limit its direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency under punishment.

- source: Chambers, C. R., & Baker, W. E. (2020). Robust Systems of Cooperation in the Presence of Rankings: How Displaying Prosocial Contributions Can Offset the Disruptive Effects of Performance Rankings. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing performance rankings tied to differential rewards causes a sharp and persistent decline in cooperation (measured as transfer rates) in a generalized reciprocity game. This effect is especially pronounced in groups that were highly cooperative before the intervention. When reputation information (about others' past giving) is also provided, the initial drop in cooperation is similar, but cooperation rates recover over time to pre-intervention levels. The mechanism appears to be that actors use reputation information to reward those who have been cooperative, especially when they perceive inequity. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, as there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design. The findings are relevant for understanding how performance rankings and reputation information affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game is a close variant of a PGG, but the outcome of interest is cooperation/transfer rate, not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be informative for understanding the impact of competitive incentives and reputation mechanisms on cooperation.

- source: Xu, Z. J., Li, R. Y., & Zhang, L. Z. (2019). The role of memory in human strategy updating in optional public goods game. *Chaos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in a spatial optional public goods game, human players update their strategies primarily based on their own past experiences (memory), not by imitating neighbors. The probability of switching to a strategy is higher if it performed well for the individual in the past, regardless of its performance among neighbors. Imitation and mutation dynamics do not adequately explain observed behavior. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome. Its main contribution is to the understanding of human strategy updating (memory vs. imitation) in repeated spatial PGGs with voluntary participation. It is not informative for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Weng, Q. F., He, N. R., Hu, L. W., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Heterogeneous investment with dynamical feedback promotes public cooperation and group success in spatial public goods games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that communication mechanisms—especially chain (one-on-one) communication and the combination of information display with chain communication—substantially increase the frequency and stability of successful group provision of the public good (EPGs via GECs) compared to a baseline with no intervention. Star network (information display only) and global communication also improve outcomes relative to baseline, but less so than chain communication. The most effective intervention is the combination of information display and chain communication. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, and does not report group efficiency or total payoffs. The findings are thus about behavioral contributions and threshold success, not efficiency per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants. It is useful for understanding the effects of communication and information display on contribution behavior and threshold success in a TPGG, but cannot inform predictions about punishment-enabled efficiency. None of the experimental treatments include punishment, sanctions, or peer enforcement. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be informative for the effects of communication and information structures in threshold public goods settings.

- source: Barclay, P., & Barker, J. L. (2020). Greener Than Thou: People who protect the environment are more cooperative, compete to be environmental, and benefit from reputation. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that (1) people who engage in more pro-environmental behaviors are more likely to cooperate in a continuous Prisoner's Dilemma; (2) people donate more to an environmental charity when their donations are observed, and even more when competing to be chosen as a partner; (3) observers preferentially choose and cooperate more with those who donated more to the environment; (4) the correlation between environmentalism and cooperation is partially mediated by concern for others. The study does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the effects of observability and partner choice (reputation/competition) on cooperative and pro-environmental behavior in a continuous Prisoner's Dilemma. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant. For prediction tasks involving the effects of observability or partner choice on cooperation, the findings suggest that such mechanisms can increase cooperative behaviors, but no efficiency or payoff data are available.

- source: Gallier, C., Langbein, J., & Vance, C. (2018). Non-binding Restrictions, Cooperation, and Coral Reef Protection: Experimental Evidence from Indonesian Fishing Communities. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that non-binding recommendations, especially those from an external source (e.g., NGO or scientist), can reduce extraction effort in a CPR game, but this effect is highly context-dependent and only significant in the most diverse community (Bunaken). Democratic recommendations have a weaker effect, and leader-based recommendations have no effect. There is no punishment or sanctioning in any treatment. The main outcome is extraction effort, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that, in the absence of enforcement, non-binding recommendations alone are often insufficient to promote cooperation, except in certain socio-cultural contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or CPR games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the limits of non-binding recommendations in the absence of enforcement, but do not inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The game design dimensions are well described, but the absence of punishment means this study is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Cardella, E., Kugler, T., Anderson, J., & Connolly, T. (2019). Is greed contagious? Four experimental studies. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that in a two-person CPR dilemma, participants increase their own withdrawals in response to high withdrawals by their partner, both when interacting with the same partner and with a new partner. However, this behavioral contagion is not mediated by perceptions of greed: participants' ratings of their partner's greediness do not predict their own subsequent behavior after controlling for observed withdrawals. Information about an opponent's greedy reputation (as rated by a previous partner) does not affect new participants' behavior. Attempts to manage one's own reputation for greed (when warned it will be shared) are weak and not statistically significant. The mechanism for behavioral contagion appears to be norm learning or direct response to observed behavior, not to perceptions or reputational information. No punishment, sanction, or reward options are present, and no group efficiency or payoff-based outcomes are analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in any condition. The design is a two-person CPR dilemma with continuous withdrawal choices, no communication, and no explicit efficiency or group payoff analysis. The findings are relevant for understanding behavioral contagion and norm learning in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. The results may be useful as a control or baseline for environments without punishment, showing that behavioral contagion (increased defection in response to observed defection) can occur, but do not address the effect of introducing punishment.

- source: Kamei, K. (2020). Group size effect and over-punishment in the case of third party enforcement of social norms. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Third party punishment in a prisoner's dilemma is effective at deterring defection as group size increases, because aggregate punishment increases even though individual punishment per punisher decreases only mildly. However, with large groups (e.g., 10 third party punishers), over-punishment leads to severe efficiency losses: defectors' payoffs become negative, and cooperators' payoffs are also reduced due to perverse punishment. When third parties are allowed to coordinate punishment levels democratically, over-punishment is mitigated, and efficiency is restored. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is positive at moderate group sizes but negative at large group sizes without coordination.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on group size and the presence of coordination mechanisms. For small to moderate group sizes, enabling third party punishment increases efficiency by deterring defection. For large group sizes, punishment without coordination leads to over-punishment and large efficiency losses, reducing group payoffs below the social optimum. If coordination mechanisms (e.g., voting) are present, over-punishment is mitigated and efficiency is restored. Thus, when predicting treatment efficiency from game design, group size and the presence/absence of coordination mechanisms are key moderators. The control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the paper provides expected payoffs for both control and punishment conditions, allowing for estimation of efficiency changes.

- source: Hajikhameneh, A., & Rubin, J. (2019). Exchange in the Absence of Legal Enforcement: Reputation and Multilateral Punishment under Uncertainty. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated trust game with 12 principals and 12 agents per session, a multilateral punishment institution allows principals to assign a permanent bad reputation to agents at a cost (C=0 or C=5). The experiment varies the probability that the principal's return is destroyed by nature (θ=0, 0.05, 0.10, 0.30). Total welfare (sum of principal and agent earnings) is the main outcome. When punishment is costless, welfare decreases monotonically with uncertainty. When punishment is costly, welfare is not significantly different between no uncertainty and modest uncertainty (θ=0.05), but falls at higher uncertainty. The cost of punishment reduces the frequency of punishment and can lead to more 'benefit of the doubt' given to agents with bad reputations, which can offset welfare losses from uncertainty at low levels. However, at high uncertainty, welfare declines regardless of punishment cost. The design does not allow for endogenous institution formation or communication between players.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on how the cost of punishment and the presence of outcome uncertainty moderate the effect of multilateral punishment on efficiency in a trust-game-like environment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that punishment can sustain high efficiency when uncertainty is low and punishment is not too costly, but that the effect is attenuated or reversed as uncertainty increases or punishment becomes more costly. The results are most relevant for trust games with binary reputation and costly, permanent punishment, and less directly transferable to standard PGGs. The findings support the importance of including punishment cost and environmental uncertainty as moderators when predicting the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Li, X. L., Jusup, M., Wang, Z., Li, H. J., Shi, L., Podobnik, B., Stanley, H. E., Havlin, S., & Boccaletti, S. (2018). Punishment diminishes the benefits of network reciprocity in social dilemma experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compared three treatments: (1) a well-mixed repeated PD (control), (2) a static network PD with two neighbors (network reciprocity), and (3) a static network PD with punishment available. Network reciprocity increased both cooperation and payoff relative to the well-mixed control. However, adding punishment did not further increase cooperation and actually reduced the median payoff per round back to the control level. Punishment also reduced assortment (clustering of cooperators) and, in larger neighborhoods, erased the positive correlation between cooperation and payoff. Thus, punishment in this setting is ineffective or even counterproductive for group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma games on static networks (with two or four neighbors), enabling costly punishment (with a 1:4 cost-to-impact ratio) does not improve and may even reduce group efficiency compared to the same game without punishment. The negative effect is especially clear in payoff-based outcomes: median payoff per round is lower with punishment than without, and the benefit of network reciprocity is erased. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar networked PD or adjacent PGG-like environments, adding punishment may not increase efficiency and could reduce it, especially as neighborhood size increases. The findings are most directly applicable to static network PDs with small groups, no communication, and fixed payoff matrices.

- source: Ahmad, M. G., & Loch, C. (2020). What do the followers want? The core functions of leadership. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in repeated two-player strategic games, allowing players to choose a punishment option (where one can assign a low-value payoff to the other after decisions are made) does not increase group efficiency. Instead, punishment is associated with lower group payoffs due to retaliation cycles and reduced cooperation. In all games, the average group benefit under punishment is lower than under individual decision-making or leadership. Leadership (especially recommending leadership) increases efficiency, while punishment governance leads to the lowest efficiency. The negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust across games with and without monetary incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated games with public-goods-like social dilemmas, enabling punishment as a governance option (without central leadership) reduces group efficiency compared to control conditions without punishment. The effect is robust across different game structures and incentive types. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment option (with moderate cost and impact, as in this design) should be expected to decrease efficiency relative to control. The findings are directly relevant for settings where punishment is decentralized and not embedded in a leadership structure.

- source: Mitzkewitz, M., & Neugebauer, T. (2020). Can intermediaries assure contracts? Experimental evidence. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment compares five treatments: a baseline (no intermediary), two ENFO treatments (punishment fines of 10 or 20 tokens), and two INFO treatments (reputation-based, with or without a small service cost). In the baseline, average payoffs are low (mean 0.64 tokens/round). INFO treatments increase payoffs modestly (mean 1.75 and 1.19 tokens/round for INFO(0) and INFO(.5)), while ENFO treatments yield much higher payoffs (mean 2.38 and 5.18 tokens/round for ENFO(10) and ENFO(20)). The ENFO institution, which enforces monetary fines for cheating, is much more effective at increasing efficiency than the INFO institution, which only provides reputation information. Increasing the fine in ENFO from 10 to 20 tokens further increases efficiency. The INFO institution's effect is limited, in part because reputation can be quickly repaired and there is no centralized punishment. The study also finds that the cost to use INFO reduces its adoption and thus its effectiveness. Over time, ENFO treatments move toward higher efficiency, while INFO treatments tend to regress toward less efficient outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated two-player prisoner's dilemma games with optional participation, introducing a punishment mechanism (ENFO) that imposes a monetary fine for cheating substantially increases efficiency compared to a control with no punishment. The effect is stronger with higher fines. An information-based reputation system (INFO) yields only modest efficiency gains, especially if reputation can be easily repaired and there is no centralized enforcement. For prediction, the presence and strength of punishment (punishment_cost, punishment_tech), as well as the absence of reward, are key moderators. The control efficiency (baseline payoff) is low, and the treatment efficiency (with ENFO) is much higher, especially with higher fines. The design features of the punishment mechanism (cost, effectiveness, centralization) are critical for predicting efficiency gains.

- source: Pansini, R., Campenni, M., & Shi, L. (2020). Segregating socioeconomic classes leads to an unequal redistribution of wealth. *Palgrave Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a costly punishment option, the ability to punish was restricted to one class (rich or poor) in segregated treatments, and randomly assigned in the integrated treatment. In segregated conditions, the class with punishment power earned nearly double the other, increasing inequality, but total group earnings were similar across all treatments. In the integrated condition, earnings were more equal and the marginal increase in total earnings was small. Thus, punishment's effect on efficiency (total earnings) is neutral, but it strongly affects the distribution of payoffs depending on who can punish.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (total group earnings) in a repeated dyadic social dilemma is minimal: enabling punishment does not substantially increase or decrease total earnings compared to control. However, the distribution of earnings is highly sensitive to who can punish: if only one class can punish, that class captures a much larger share of the total payoff. For prediction, this suggests that in similar dyadic games, enabling punishment may not improve efficiency, but can increase inequality if punishment power is asymmetric. The design dimensions most relevant are the symmetry of punishment access and the matching structure (segregated vs integrated).

- source: Safarzynska, K. (2020). Collective punishment promotes resource conservation if it is not enforced. *Forest Policy and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when participants can voluntarily contribute to a centralized sanctioning institution (which probabilistically punishes untrustworthy behavior in a dyadic trust game), they frequently do so, but the resulting institutions are typically inefficient. The cost of forming the institution is not offset by the gains in trust and trustworthiness, so overall group earnings (efficiency) are lower than in the control condition where no sanctioning institution can be formed. This result holds for both partner and stranger matching protocols. The paper also finds that higher sanction probabilities increase trust and trustworthiness, but the aggregate welfare is still lower due to the cost of institution formation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a trust-game environment with voluntary centralized sanctioning, enabling punishment can reduce efficiency compared to a no-punishment baseline, even though trust and trustworthiness increase. For prediction, this suggests that the presence of costly, voluntarily funded centralized punishment may decrease efficiency unless the punishment mechanism is highly cost-effective. The findings are most relevant for trust-game-like settings with endogenous institution formation, and less directly for standard PGGs, but the negative efficiency effect of costly punishment is a cautionary signal for similar environments.

- source: Chávez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2021). Co-enforcement of Common Pool Resources to Deter Encroachment: Evidence from a Field Experiment in Chile. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The field experiment with Chilean fishers shows that co-enforcement (insider monitoring plus government sanctions) reduces poaching and increases insiders' harvests and earnings relative to a no-enforcement baseline. However, the costs of monitoring largely offset these gains, so net efficiency improvements are modest. Voting on monitoring investments and higher fines both help insiders coordinate to invest enough in monitoring to eliminate average poaching gains, but poaching persists and is not highly sensitive to the level of monitoring or fines. The results suggest that while co-enforcement can improve outcomes, the ongoing cost of monitoring is a major constraint on efficiency gains.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing punishment (in the form of exogenous government sanctions for poaching, combined with group monitoring) increases group efficiency/payoff relative to a no-enforcement baseline, but the net gains are limited by the costliness of monitoring. The design is not a standard public goods game but is a dynamic common pool resource game with poaching by outsiders. The findings suggest that, in similar CPR settings, enabling punishment/sanctions will likely increase efficiency, but the magnitude of the effect depends on the cost structure of monitoring and the ability of insiders to coordinate. Voting and higher fines can help achieve deterrence, but unless monitoring costs are low, efficiency gains may be small. For prediction, the control (no-punishment) efficiency is lower than with punishment enabled, but the treatment effect is muted by monitoring costs.

- source: Szekely, A., Andrighetto, G., Payette, N., & Tummolini, L. (2020). Aggression, Conflict, and the Formation of Intimidating Group Reputation. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that when group members can use peer punishment to enforce an intimidating group reputation, they increase their punishment of outsiders (A), but this leads to higher conflict and lower earnings for both group members (B) and outsiders (A) in the short run. Without peer punishment, group reputation is exploited and aggression is low, with higher earnings. Individual reputation (with individual history) leads to more aggression than group reputation without peer punishment, but still results in higher earnings than the group reputation with peer punishment condition. Agent-based simulations suggest that in the long run, peer punishment can eventually increase group earnings as reputation deters conflict, but this is not realized in the 11-round experiment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling peer punishment to enforce group reputation in a conflict/aggression game increases punishment and conflict, but reduces efficiency and group earnings in the short run. For prediction tasks, this suggests that adding a peer punishment mechanism (with moderate cost and effectiveness) to a group setting where reputation is at stake will likely decrease efficiency compared to a control without punishment, at least over a moderate number of rounds. The effect may reverse in much longer games, but this is only supported by simulation, not experimental data. The design is not a standard public goods game, but is a social dilemma with conflict and punishment, so results are most transferable to adjacent settings.

- source: Zhao, Y. K., Zhao, X. B., & Shen, Z. J. M. (2018). The hot-versus-cold effect in a punishment game: a multi-round experimental study. *Annals of Operations Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compares two elicitation methods (strategy vs. direct-response) in a multi-round, two-player punishment game. The strategy method leads to significantly higher punishment rates (84% vs. 62%) and more frequent cooperative sender actions (S1: 68% vs. 44%). Combined payoffs (group welfare) are higher for S1 actions, and the higher frequency of S1 under the strategy method results in higher average group payoffs. Regression and learning model analyses show that the difference in punishment rates is due to mental accounting: the strategy method allows receivers to integrate the cost and expected benefit of punishment, increasing its perceived utility. Over repeated rounds, senders learn to avoid punishment by choosing S1 more often, further increasing group welfare. Reward rates are low and do not differ between methods. The findings suggest that, in repeated games, the strategic value of punishment (deterrence) is more important than the immediate emotional satisfaction of punishing, and that the design of the punishment mechanism (including decision timing) can significantly affect cooperation and group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated two-player punishment games, higher punishment rates (enabled by the strategy method) lead to more cooperative sender behavior and higher group payoffs. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the structure is adjacent: one player can take a selfish or cooperative action, and the other can punish. The results suggest that, in repeated settings, enabling effective punishment (with high perceived strategic value) increases efficiency by deterring selfish behavior. For prediction tasks, this implies that, all else equal, punishment mechanisms that increase the perceived strategic value of punishment (e.g., via decision timing or repeated interaction) will lead to higher efficiency, especially when the baseline (control) efficiency is low due to frequent selfish actions. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, punishment-enabled games, but the mechanism may generalize to other repeated social dilemmas with punishment.

- source: Kamei, K. (2018). The role of visibility on third party punishment actions for the enforcement of social norms. *Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when third party punishment is visible to another punisher, punishment of defectors becomes much stronger and punishment of cooperators is nearly eliminated. This shifts the incentive structure from a prisoner's dilemma to a coordination game, where mutual cooperation is a Nash equilibrium and cooperating yields a higher expected payoff than defecting. In the standard (low visibility) treatment, punishment is weaker and less well-targeted, and the incentive structure remains a prisoner's dilemma, with defection being more profitable. The findings suggest that increasing the visibility of punishment actions can enhance the effectiveness of third party punishment in promoting cooperation and improving group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the visibility of punishment actions (i.e., making punishment observable to other punishers) can substantially increase the effectiveness of third party punishment in promoting cooperation and improving expected payoffs. While the game is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the mechanism is closely related. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment visibility should be predicted to increase efficiency (group payoff) in similar environments. The paper also provides quantitative data on expected payoffs under different punishment regimes, which can inform predictions about the magnitude of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled and made visible.

- source: Zhang, M., Ma, Y. J., Tao, Y., Wang, Z., Shi, L., & Wang, R. W. (2020). Awareness of wealth inequalities breeds animosity. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in repeated PD games with costly punishment, the visibility of wealth information interacts with initial wealth inequality to affect both cooperation rates and average final wealth. When initial wealth is unequal, making wealth visible reduces cooperation and leads to lower average final wealth compared to when wealth is hidden. When initial wealth is equal, visibility has little effect on cooperation or final wealth. Punishment is available but rarely used, and its frequency does not significantly differ across treatments. Thus, in environments with inequality, hiding wealth information improves both cooperation and group payoff (final wealth), while visibility exacerbates inequality and reduces efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving efficiency in repeated PD or PGG-like games with punishment, this paper provides evidence that the visibility of wealth information is a strong moderator when initial endowments are unequal: making wealth visible reduces efficiency (average final wealth), while hiding it increases efficiency. The effect is not present when initial endowments are equal. The presence of punishment does not appear to moderate this effect, as punishment is infrequent and not significantly different across treatments. Thus, when modeling efficiency outcomes, consider both initial wealth inequality and information visibility as key moderators, especially in games with available but rarely used punishment.

- source: Friebel, G., Kosfeld, M., & Thielmann, G. (2019). Trust the Police? Self-Selection of Motivated Agents into the German Police Force. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Police applicants in Germany are more trustworthy (return more in the trust game) and invest more in costly third-party punishment and reward than comparable high school students. The difference in trust is explained by risk preferences, but the difference in trustworthiness and norm enforcement remains significant after controlling for covariates. The willingness to punish and reward is measured as the average amount invested in these actions as a third party in a trust-game-based scenario. The study provides evidence for positive self-selection of intrinsically motivated, norm-enforcing individuals into the police force. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, and thus does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that individuals who self-select into enforcement roles (police applicants) are more willing to engage in costly punishment and reward to enforce cooperation norms. However, the experimental design is not a public goods game or a repeated social dilemma, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the motivation to punish and enforce norms, but do not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoffs in public-goods-game-like environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as indirect evidence about the types of individuals who may be more likely to use punishment, rather than as evidence about the efficiency consequences of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2021). Cognitive load decreases cooperation and moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment option. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that cognitive load reduces both cooperation and moral punishment in a one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment. Hypocritical and antisocial punishment are not significantly affected by cognitive load, but a general punishment bias (punishing regardless of partner behavior) increases under cognitive load. The amount spent on punishment, when punishment occurs, does not differ between conditions. The results suggest that both cooperation and moral punishment require cognitive resources and are not purely intuitive. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a two-player one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, the presence of punishment (and its purposeful use) depends on cognitive resources. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding how cognitive load moderates the behavioral use of punishment and cooperation, not their impact on group efficiency. The game is a Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, so transfer to PGG settings should be cautious.

- source: Nese, A., O'Higgins, N., Sbriglia, P., & Scudiero, M. (2018). Cooperation, punishment and organized crime: a lab-in-the-field experiment in southern Italy. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that Camorra inmates are highly cooperative and strongly punish defectors, consistent with their internal codes of conduct. However, when external punishment (third-party punishment) is introduced, their cooperation decreases, indicating a rejection of external authority. Ordinary criminals, in contrast, increase cooperation when external punishment is possible, suggesting opportunism. Students' cooperation also decreases with TPP. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation and punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The findings highlight the importance of group-specific social norms and attitudes in shaping responses to punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on how the introduction of punishment affects cooperation rates in a Prisoner's Dilemma setting, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player PD and a three-player PD with third-party punishment, rather than a standard multi-player PGG. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, possibly on efficiency) is highly dependent on group norms and attitudes: punishment can reduce cooperation in some groups (students, Camorra) and increase it in others (ordinary criminals). However, because efficiency is not measured, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The evidence is most useful for understanding behavioral responses to punishment under different social norms, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Dorrough, A. R., Olsson, M. I. T., Froehlich, L., Glöckner, A., & Martiny, S. E. (2021). Does she compensate the victim while he punishes the perpetrator? No gender differences in anonymous economic games across 11 nations. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two large studies (one multinational, one German student sample), there is no evidence for systematic gender differences in third-party punishment or compensation behavior in response to unfairness. Both men and women are more likely to compensate the victim than to punish the perpetrator. Increasing the effectiveness of punishment (punishment impact per unit cost) does not substantially change this pattern. The study does not analyze or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, and the game is not a repeated public goods game but a one-shot third-party punishment/compensation scenario.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral use of punishment and compensation options in a third-party context, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves norm enforcement and costly punishment, but is not a repeated or group-based contribution game. The main decision-relevant finding is that, even when punishment is made more effective, participants prefer compensation over punishment, suggesting that punishment may be underutilized in similar designs. However, since efficiency or group payoff is not measured, the paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The evidence is most relevant for understanding behavioral preferences for punishment versus compensation, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Ferguson, E. (2021). Inequality averse and compassionate blood donor: implication for interventions. *Vox Sanguinis*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
