  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated ultimatum game, disclosing responders' decisions to proposers (enabling implicit communication of disapproval/punishment) increases rejection rates for low offers. Proposers anticipate this and make higher offers, but not enough to fully offset the increased rejections. As a result, total group earnings (the sum of proposer and responder payoffs) are slightly lower with disclosure, though the difference is not statistically significant. The effect is most pronounced for moderately unfair offers. The study also finds that when offers are random (not chosen by proposers), disclosure reduces rejection rates, as responders avoid signaling envy. Overall, the main efficiency-relevant result is that punishment via rejection, when made salient through disclosure, does not increase group efficiency and may slightly reduce it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that enabling punishment-like behavior (rejection of unfair offers) through increased transparency (disclosure) does not improve efficiency in a bargaining game adjacent to public goods games. Instead, it may slightly reduce efficiency, as the cost of increased rejections is not fully offset by higher offers. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in similar environments where punishment is enabled or made more salient, efficiency may not increase and could decrease slightly, especially if the punishment is costly and not perfectly anticipated by those being punished. The findings are most directly relevant to bargaining/ultimatum games, but the mechanism—costly punishment reducing group surplus—may generalize to PGG-like settings.

- source: Fabbri, M., Barbieri, P. N., & Bigoni, M. (2019). Ride Your Luck! A Field Experiment on Lottery-Based Incentives for Compliance. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: A field experiment on city buses in Italy tested a lottery-based reward for buying tickets, layered on top of existing probabilistic sanctions for fare-dodging. Treated buses saw a significant increase in on-board ticket sales (18–30% increase per hour), with no overall reduction in off-board ticket sales, indicating a net increase in compliance and revenue. The increased revenue covered the cost of the prizes actually claimed, but not the total value of all prizes offered, so cost-effectiveness depended on unclaimed prizes. The effect was not persistent after the lottery ended. Suburban routes showed a substitution from off-board to on-board ticket purchases. The study provides evidence that combining probabilistic sanctions with lottery-based rewards can increase compliance and revenue in a real-world public good context, but the net efficiency gain is limited by the cost of rewards and the sustainability of the effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides field evidence that adding a lottery-based reward to an environment with probabilistic sanctions increases compliance and revenue (a proxy for efficiency), but the net efficiency gain depends on the cost of the reward and whether all prizes are claimed. The effect is not persistent after removal. For prediction, the presence of both probabilistic sanctions and a reward mechanism (lottery) increases efficiency relative to sanctions alone, but the magnitude of the gain may be modest and sensitive to the cost structure and salience of the reward. The design is not a standard PGG, but the compliance/public good context and outcome measures are closely related. The findings are most informative for settings where compliance is individually observable and can be incentivized with both sanctions and probabilistic rewards.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Liu, X. S., Bao, H. L., Su, Y., & He, M. F. (2018). Evolution of cooperation through adaptive interaction in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing and increasing the probability of disconnection (β) after defection leads to a significant increase in the fraction of cooperators in both simulation and human lab experiments. The effect is nonlinear: at low β, the effect is modest, but at higher β, cooperation increases sharply. The mechanism is interpreted as a punishment for defection, as defectors are excluded from interactions and thus lose opportunities for payoff. The system can transition between states of all-defector, all-cooperator, or coexistence depending on the parameters (temptation to defect, cost, and β). While payoffs are discussed and collected, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that a punishment-like mechanism (disconnection after defection) can substantially increase cooperation rates in a spatial prisoner's dilemma environment. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, so its value for predicting efficiency is indirect. The findings suggest that enabling a punishment mechanism that reduces defectors' ability to interact (and thus earn payoff) can increase cooperation, which is likely to improve efficiency in similar environments, especially when the punishment is strong (high β). The design is adjacent to public goods games, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious. The main predictive signal is that punishment mechanisms that exclude defectors can be effective, especially when the exclusion probability is high.

- source: Truskanov, N., Emery, Y., & Bshary, R. (2020). Juvenile cleaner fish can socially learn the consequences of cheating. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Juvenile cleaner fish, after observing adults interacting with model clients (plates) that respond negatively to cheating (either by fleeing or punishing), subsequently behave more cooperatively (feed more against their preference) and prefer partners that are tolerant to cheating. Social learning about the consequences of cheating shapes cooperative strategies, but juveniles do not simply copy arbitrary preferences of adults. The study provides evidence that social learning about sanctions can promote cooperation in a non-human animal, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff as in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that observation of punishment or negative partner responses can increase cooperative behavior in a dyadic animal model. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that the presence of observable sanctions can bias individuals toward more cooperative choices, which in analogous human or multi-player games could translate to higher efficiency if similar mechanisms operate. However, the lack of direct payoff or efficiency measurement, and the dyadic (not group) structure, limit direct transfer to the prediction of efficiency in public goods games with punishment. The main value is mechanistic: social learning about sanctions can promote cooperation.

- source: Julle-Danière, E., Whitehouse, J., Vrij, A., Gustafsson, E., & Waller, B. M. (2020). The social function of the feeling and expression of guilt. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that individuals who feel more guilt after being blamed for a cooperative failure allocate more of a shared reward to their partner, regardless of friendship strength. When the victim of a transgression (Player 2) perceives their partner as guilty, they are more likely to 'punish' (i.e., take more for themselves) if the partner is a close friend, though this effect is sensitive to outliers. The expression of guilt thus has a complex social function, sometimes leading to harsher punishment from close friends. The main behavioral outcome is the reward split in a dictator game, not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not directly address the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, using a one-shot dictator game after a cooperative task with induced guilt, but does not involve repeated contribution decisions, group efficiency, or explicit punishment mechanisms as in standard PGGs. The main outcome is the allocation of a fixed reward, interpreted as relationship repair or punishment, but not as efficiency. The findings suggest that social context (friendship, perceived guilt) can modulate punitive or reparative behavior, but do not provide quantitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency in PGG-like games. Thus, the paper is more informative about the social and emotional moderators of punishment-like behavior than about efficiency outcomes relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Enke, B. (2019). KINSHIP, COOPERATION, AND THE EVOLUTION OF MORAL SYSTEMS. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that kinship structure predicts the type of moral system a society develops: tight kinship societies rely on communal values, shame, revenge, and local institutions, while loose kinship societies develop universal values, guilt, impersonal (altruistic/third-party) punishment, and large-scale institutions. These systems are internally consistent and mediate cooperation patterns, such as in-group favoritism and trust. Theoretical modeling suggests that universalizing moral systems in loose kinship societies enable more efficient cooperation with strangers, which becomes advantageous with economic development. However, empirical analyses focus on psychological, cultural, and institutional variables, not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the role of punishment and moral systems in supporting cooperation, especially in relation to kinship structure and the scope of cooperation. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games or similar environments. However, it suggests that impersonal (altruistic/third-party) punishment is more prevalent in societies with loose kinship ties and universal values, which are theoretically linked to higher efficiency in broad cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding how cultural and institutional context may moderate the effectiveness of punishment, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency effects in controlled game settings.

- source: Baumann, F., Benndorf, V., & Friese, M. (2019). Loss-induced emotions and criminal behavior: An experimental analysis. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that loss framing (compared to gain framing) increases negative emotions (anger, frustration) and decreases positive emotions (satisfaction, happiness) for both men and women. However, only men are more likely to take money from a charity (commit a norm violation) in the loss frame, and this behavior is positively correlated with their reported negative emotions. The punishment for norm violation is probabilistic: if caught, the subject pays a fine equal to the amount taken. The study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on individual norm-violating behavior and emotional states.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it includes a punishment mechanism (probabilistic fine for norm violation), the design is not a public goods game and does not report group payoff or efficiency outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding how emotional states and loss framing affect norm violations under the threat of punishment, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The punishment mechanism is similar to those in economic crime experiments, so the paper may provide indirect context for how punishment and emotional states interact, especially with respect to gender differences, but it does not support direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Eriksson, K., Strimling, P., Gelfand, M., Wu, J. H., Abernathy, J., Akotia, C. S., Aldashev, A., Andersson, P. A., Andrighetto, G., Anum, A., Arikan, G., Aycan, Z., Bagherian, F., Barrera, D., Basnight-Brown, D., Batkeyev, B., Belaus, A., Berezina, E., Björnstjerna, M., Blumen, S., Boski, P., Zeineddine, F. B., Bovina, I., Huyen, B. T. T., Cardenas, J. C., Cekrlija, D., Choi, H. S., Contreras-Ibáñez, C. C., Costa-Lopes, R., de Barra, M., de Zoysa, P., Dorrough, A., Dvoryanchikov, N., Eller, A., Engelmann, J. B., Euh, H., Fang, X., Fiedler, S., Foster-Gimbel, O. A., Fülöp, M., Gardarsdottir, R. B., Gill, C. M. H. D., Glöckner, A., Graf, S., Grigoryan, A., Gritskov, V., Growiec, K., Halama, P., Hartanto, A., Hopthrow, T., Hrebícková, M., Ilisko, D., Imada, H., Kapoor, H., Kawakami, K., Khachatryan, N., Kharchenko, N., Khoury, N., Kiyonari, T., Kohút, M., Linh, L. T., Leslie, L. M., Li, Y., Li, N. P., Li, Z., Liik, K., Maitner, A. T., Manhique, B., Manley, H., Medhioub, I., Mentser, S., Mohammed, L., Nejat, P., Nipassa, O., Nussinson, R., Onyedire, N. G., Onyishi, I. E., Özden, S., Panagiotopoulou, P., Perez-Floriano, L. R., Persson, M. S., Pheko, M., Pirttilä-Backman, A. M., Pogosyan, M., Raver, J., Reyna, C., Rodrigues, R. B., Romanò, S., Romero, P. P., Sakki, I., San Martin, A., Sherbaji, S., Shimizu, H., Simpson, B., Szabo, E., Takemura, K., Tieffi, H., Teixeira, M. L. M., Thanomkul, N., Tiliouine, H., Travaglino, G. A., Tsirbas, Y., Wan, R., Widodo, S., Zein, R., Zhang, Q. P., Zirganou-Kazolea, L., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2021). Perceptions of the appropriate response to norm violation in 57 societies. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that across cultures, the perceived appropriateness of informal sanctions (confrontation, gossip, ostracism) increases as the perceived inappropriateness of a norm violation increases. There is substantial cross-country variation in which sanctions are viewed as most appropriate, and this variation is consistent across norm violation domains (including a common resource depletion scenario). Metanorms for different sanctions are not interchangeable: for example, societies that view physical confrontation as less appropriate tend to view gossip as more appropriate, and vice versa. The perceived appropriateness of sanctions correlates with cultural, economic, and ecological variables (e.g., higher income countries view confrontation as less appropriate and gossip as more appropriate). However, the study does not measure actual cooperation, punishment behavior, or efficiency in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not report on actual game play, contributions, or payoffs, nor does it manipulate punishment availability or cost. Its main relevance is contextual: it shows that beliefs about the appropriateness of punishment and other sanctions vary widely across societies, even for cooperation-related norm violations. This may inform expectations about the acceptability or uptake of punishment in different cultural contexts, but does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Di Guida, S., Han, T. A., Kirchsteiger, G., Lenaerts, T., & Zisis, I. (2021). Repeated Interaction and Its Impact on Cooperation and Surplus Allocation-An Experimental Analysis. *Games*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three treatments: (1) Baseline (forced cooperation, exogenous re-matching), (2) Re-match (voluntary cooperation, exogenous re-matching), and (3) Endogenous-match (voluntary cooperation, endogenous group formation). Allowing refusal to cooperate (punishment) increases average contributions and receiver earnings, and decreases distributor earnings, relative to forced cooperation. However, allowing repeated interaction with the same group (endogenous matching) does not further increase average efficiency or payoffs compared to exogenous re-matching. Instead, repeated interaction leads to self-selection: some groups are highly cooperative and efficient, while others are unstable and inefficient. The most efficient groups are composed of 'greedy' distributors (who contribute more to avoid rejection) and 'modest' receivers (who accept lower offers). Overall, the possibility of punishment (refusal to cooperate) increases efficiency, but repeated interaction does not further increase average efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment via refusal to cooperate (i.e., making cooperation voluntary) increases efficiency and average payoffs compared to forced cooperation in a surplus allocation game with asymmetric bargaining power. However, simply allowing repeated interaction and endogenous group formation does not further increase average efficiency or payoffs, though it does create heterogeneity in group outcomes. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (even if only via refusal to cooperate) is likely to increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment. The effect of repeated interaction/endogenous matching is not to further increase average efficiency, but to increase variance across groups. The findings are directly relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as the ability to refuse cooperation, but less so for costly punishment mechanisms typical in standard PGGs.

- source: Bolton, G., Greiner, B., & Ockenfels, A. (2018). Dispute Resolution or Escalation? The Strategic Gaming of Feedback Withdrawal Options in Online Markets. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In laboratory markets with two-sided moral hazard (both buyer and seller can defect), the introduction of a mutual feedback withdrawal option leads to more strategic negative feedback, less honest feedback, and lower efficiency (measured as realized gains from trade divided by maximum possible gains). The feedback system becomes less informative, and trust/trustworthiness are reduced. In one-sided markets (only seller can defect), the feedback withdrawal option does not significantly affect efficiency or the informativeness of feedback. The negative effect of FBW on efficiency is thus conditional on the presence of two-sided moral hazard and mutual withdrawal. The paper also finds that some buyers engage in 'altruistic punishment' by refusing to withdraw negative feedback even at a cost to themselves, partially mitigating the detrimental effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to public goods games with two-sided moral hazard and mutual punishment/retaliation options, introducing a mutual feedback withdrawal (punishment-like) mechanism reduces efficiency relative to a control without such a mechanism. The effect is not observed in one-sided environments. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of mutual, bilateral punishment/retaliation options in two-sided games is likely to reduce efficiency, even if the control game is already inefficient. The effect is mediated by strategic retaliation and the breakdown of reputation as an incentive for cooperation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar two-sided, bilateral punishment/retaliation structures.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Diekmann, A. (2020). Binding Contracts, Non-Binding Promises and Social Feedback in the Intertemporal Common-Pool Resource Game. *Games*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated intertemporal common-pool resource game with five players and ten rounds, the study compares the effects of (1) binding contracts, (2) non-binding promises, (3) private social feedback, and (4) public social feedback, against a no-regulation control. Binding contracts, when unanimously signed, lead to the highest group efficiency (average profit per round close to the cooperative optimum). Non-binding promises increase sustainable investment and efficiency initially, but the effect decays over time. Private feedback (where only the recipient sees ratings) does not improve efficiency over the control. Public feedback (where feedback scores are visible to all) significantly increases sustainable investment and group efficiency, with the effect being more stable over time than promises. The study provides direct evidence that public, but not private, social feedback can substitute for formal sanctions in promoting efficiency in CPR dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated CPR games with continuous contribution choices, public social feedback (where feedback is visible to all) can substantially increase group efficiency, even in the absence of formal punishment or monetary sanctions. Private feedback is ineffective. Binding contracts are most effective but require unanimous agreement. For prediction tasks, the presence of public feedback (show_other_summaries = yes, show_punishment_id = yes) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, while private feedback (show_other_summaries = yes, show_punishment_id = no) should not. The absence of monetary punishment or reward mechanisms means that the findings are most relevant for settings where social (non-monetary) sanctions are possible. The game design dimensions (player_count = 5, num_rounds = 10, continuous contributions, no chat, known rounds, full feedback on others' choices and payoffs) are well-specified and should be used as reference points for prediction.

- source: Hoffmann, R., Kittel, B., & Larsen, M. (2021). Information exchange in laboratory markets: competition, transfer costs, and the emergence of reputation. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that voluntary, costless information sharing among trustors in a repeated investment game leads to higher trustworthiness (trustees return more), higher trust (trustors send more), and higher payoffs for both trustors and trustees. When direct costs are imposed on information sharing, or when competition among trustors is introduced (making information sharing privately costly), information sharing drops sharply, and the efficiency gains from reputation mechanisms disappear. The effect of direct costs is highly non-linear: even small costs cause a large reduction in information sharing and efficiency. Competition among trustors increases their risk-taking (sending more), but reduces information sharing and trustworthiness, so the net effect on efficiency is ambiguous or negative. The study provides direct evidence that barriers to information sharing (costs or competition) undermine the emergence of reputation and reduce market efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling costless, voluntary information sharing (a reputation mechanism) in repeated trust/investment games increases efficiency (group payoffs) relative to a control with no information sharing. However, even small costs to information sharing, or the introduction of competition among information holders, sharply reduce information sharing and eliminate efficiency gains. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of a costless, voluntary reputation/information sharing mechanism is a strong moderator of the efficiency effect. The findings are most directly relevant to trust/investment games, which are adjacent to PGGs, and to endogenous, voluntary, non-institutionalized punishment/sanctioning via information sharing. The results suggest that in environments where information sharing is costly or where competition discourages sharing, the efficiency benefits of reputation mechanisms will be minimal or absent.

- source: Ahn, T. K., Loukas, B., Batsaikhan, M., Campos-Ortiz, F., Putterman, L., & Sutter, M. (2018). Trust and communication in a property rights dilemma. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing opportunities for free-form chat communication in a repeated property rights dilemma (where players can allocate tokens to production, theft, private or collective protection) nearly doubles the share of maximum efficiency gain achieved, compared to a no-communication baseline. Communication reduces theft and protection expenditures, and increases production and group earnings. The effect is robust to whether or not players can easily identify thieves, suggesting that punishment threats are not the main driver. Instead, trust, agreement on cooperative plans, and references to efficient outcomes in chat are strongly associated with higher efficiency. The effect of communication is much larger than that of institutionalized collective protection or voting. There is substantial cross-country variation, with higher trust societies benefiting more from communication.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication in social dilemma environments with appropriation (theft) and protection options can substantially increase efficiency, even when punishment is not easily implemented or monitored. For prediction, the presence of chat/communication is a major moderator of the efficiency effect, with communication nearly doubling the realized efficiency relative to control. The effect is robust to whether punishment is easy or hard to coordinate, suggesting that communication's main value is in building trust and agreement, not in facilitating punishment. The game is not a standard PGG, but the efficiency results are directly relevant for settings where appropriation and protection are possible. Control efficiency (no chat) is about 36.6% of the maximum possible gain; with chat, it rises to 77.2% (full info) and 70.5% (low info). Thus, for similar games, enabling chat should be expected to produce large positive effects on efficiency, especially in higher-trust populations.

- source: Fehr, D., & Sutter, M. (2019). Gossip and the efficiency of interactions. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that enabling third-party gossip in a repeated trust game increases both trustor investments and trustee return ratios, resulting in higher efficiency (total group earnings) compared to a baseline with no third party and to a treatment with only observation (no communication). Efficiency in the Gossip treatment is 13% higher than baseline and 5% higher than Observation. The effect is robust to the cost of gossip and the amount of information available to gossipers, but is reduced when gossip is noisy (messages may be misdelivered). The presence of a third party who can observe and potentially gossip is more effective at increasing efficiency than simply providing first-hand information to trustors. The results suggest that the threat or possibility of gossip acts as a cost-effective social sanction, increasing cooperation and efficiency without direct material punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in trust-game-like environments, enabling a third-party gossip mechanism (even when costly and unverifiable) increases efficiency relative to both a baseline and an observation-only condition. The efficiency gain is substantial and robust to variations in the cost and informativeness of gossip, but is reduced if gossip is unreliable. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, the findings are likely relevant for adjacent environments where indirect social sanctions (gossip, reputation) are possible. For prediction, the presence of a gossip/observation mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency, with the largest gains when gossip is reliable and can be selectively transmitted. The effect is not due to direct punishment, but to reputational concerns and information transmission.

- source: Fonseca, M. A., & Peters, K. (2018). Will any gossip do? Gossip does not need to be perfectly accurate to promote trust. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that enabling gossip (reputational information transmission) in a repeated trust game increases both trust and trustworthiness, leading to higher efficiency (joint payoffs) compared to a no-message baseline. Even when gossip is substantially inaccurate (either endogenously or exogenously), efficiency remains higher than in the absence of gossip, though it is lower than with accurate information. The effect is robust across treatments, with the largest efficiency gains observed when information is accurate, but meaningful gains persist even with high inaccuracy. The mechanism is reputational concern and discrimination, not direct costly punishment. The study provides direct evidence that non-monetary, reputation-based social sanctions (gossip) can substitute for formal punishment in promoting efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a reputational mechanism (gossip) in repeated trust-based interactions increases efficiency, even when the information is not perfectly accurate. For prediction tasks, the presence of a gossip/reputation channel should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a no-communication baseline, with the effect size depending on the accuracy of the information. However, the mechanism is not direct costly punishment, but rather indirect social sanctioning via reputation. The findings are most directly applicable to trust games and adjacent to public goods games, especially in settings where partner choice or discrimination is possible. The results suggest that even imperfect reputation systems can yield substantial efficiency gains, and that the absence of any reputational channel is particularly detrimental to efficiency.

- source: Riedl, A., Rohde, I. M. T., & Strobel, M. (2021). Free neighborhood choice boosts socially optimal outcomes in stag-hunt coordination problem. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that free neighborhood choice in a repeated stag-hunt game (coordination problem) substantially increases the frequency of efficient coordination and group welfare compared to imposed (fixed) neighborhoods. In the fixed-neighborhood condition, groups mostly converge to the inefficient equilibrium, while in the free-choice condition, efficient outcomes become dominant over time. The ability to exclude inefficient players (a form of endogenous, costly social sanction) is the key mechanism: excluded players are much more likely to switch to efficient behavior, and overall exclusion is infrequent but sufficient to sustain high efficiency. Welfare gains in the free-choice condition are significantly higher, especially in later rounds, despite exclusion being costly. The results suggest that even limited, endogenous social sanctions (via exclusion) can robustly promote efficient group outcomes in coordination settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling endogenous, costly exclusion (a form of social sanction) in a repeated coordination game can dramatically increase group efficiency, even when exclusion is rare and costly. While the setting is a stag-hunt (coordination) game rather than a public goods game, the mechanism—costly exclusion of inefficient actors—parallels punishment in PGGs. The findings suggest that, in environments where players can choose their partners and exclusion is possible but costly, efficiency can be much higher than in fixed-group settings. For prediction tasks, this implies that the presence of endogenous exclusion or partner choice should be expected to have a large positive effect on efficiency, especially over repeated rounds, even if the cost of exclusion is nontrivial. However, the results may not transfer directly to standard PGGs with explicit punishment stages, as the underlying game is a coordination problem, not a social dilemma.

- source: Rietz, T. A., Schniter, E., Sheremeta, R. M., & Shields, T. W. (2018). TRUST, RECIPROCITY, AND RULES. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper experimentally studies the effect of exogenously imposed minimum return rules in a one-shot trust game. Four treatments are compared: no rule (0%), and minimum return rules of 10%, 20%, and 30% of the tripled investment. The main findings are: (1) Low minimum return rules (10%) reduce trust (investment), reciprocity (voluntary returns), and economic efficiency (ROI, group payoff) compared to no rule. (2) As the rule becomes more restrictive (20%, 30%), investment and efficiency recover, but trust and reciprocity do not. (3) Only the most restrictive rule (30%) restores or improves efficiency, but this is due to mechanical risk reduction, not increased trust. (4) The relationship between rule restrictiveness and efficiency is U-shaped: gentle rules are worse than no rule, but strong rules can restore efficiency. (5) The presence of rules crowds out voluntary cooperation and learning about partner trustworthiness.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that introducing weak or moderate minimum return rules in trust-based games can reduce efficiency compared to no rule, due to crowding out of trust and reciprocity. Only highly restrictive rules (that nearly eliminate risk) can restore or improve efficiency, but at the cost of voluntary cooperation. The effect is non-monotonic: efficiency is lowest at low rule levels, recovers at high rule levels. This suggests that, in adjacent trust-game-like environments, the effect of punishment-like minimum standards on efficiency depends critically on the restrictiveness of the rule. The findings are most relevant for settings where the control (no-punishment) game already achieves moderate efficiency; in such cases, adding weak punishment or minimum standards may reduce efficiency.

- source: Fonseca, M. A., & Peters, K. (2021). Is it costly to deceive? People are adept at detecting gossipers' lies but may not reward honesty. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Dishonest gossip reduces efficiency in trust-based interactions: participants who act on dishonest gossip (especially positive lies about untrustworthy agents or negative lies about trustworthy agents) earn lower payoffs than those who act on honest gossip. Participants can detect some dishonest gossip, especially after interacting with the target, and are more likely to trust gossipers they believe to be honest. However, the tendency to socially sanction dishonest gossipers (by reducing trust) is inconsistent and influenced by the valence of the gossip: gossipers who share negative information, even if honest, are trusted less. Thus, while social sanctions for dishonesty exist, they are imperfect and do not always align with actual honesty, limiting their effectiveness in promoting efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the presence of dishonest communication in trust-based games reduces efficiency, as measured by participant payoffs. While not a public goods game, the trust game with gossip is adjacent and the mechanism—misleading information undermining cooperation—transfers to PGG-like settings. The findings suggest that, in environments where social sanctions for dishonesty are weak or inconsistently applied, the efficiency gains from reputation mechanisms (like gossip) are limited by the prevalence and detectability of dishonesty. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effectiveness of punishment or sanction mechanisms in improving efficiency depends not only on their presence but also on their alignment with actual dishonest behavior and the social context (e.g., whether negative but honest information is socially penalized). The paper does not manipulate classic PGG punishment dimensions (cost, tech, etc.), but it does show that the ability to sanction dishonesty is necessary but not sufficient for efficiency gains.

- source: Kaplan, T. R., Ruffle, B. J., & Shtudiner, Z. (2018). Cooperation through coordination in two stages. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment compares three treatments of a two-player entry game: simultaneous moves (Now), endogenous timing (Wait), and exogenous sequential moves (Seq). In all treatments, players have private values and must decide whether to enter or exit, with payoffs depending on both players' choices. The main finding is that the Seq treatment yields the highest average pair profits (mean 170.2), followed by Wait (167.9), and Now (163.2). The difference between Seq and Now is statistically significant. The two-stage games allow cooperative pairs to coordinate more efficiently and achieve higher profits, but also allow uncooperative pairs to do worse, leading to a wider distribution of outcomes. The Wait and Seq treatments reduce double exit and increase efficient cooperation compared to Now, but also see a modest increase in double entry. The paper does not study punishment as a formal mechanism, but discusses how uncooperative behavior in the two-stage games can be met with retaliatory entry (a form of endogenous punishment).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that changing the timing structure of a two-player entry game (from simultaneous to sequential or endogenous timing) can increase average efficiency, but with substantial heterogeneity: cooperative pairs benefit, while uncooperative pairs may do worse. The results are highly relevant for predicting efficiency in games where the main design dimension is the timing of moves, but less so for games where formal punishment mechanisms (with explicit costs and impacts) are present. The findings suggest that enabling sequential or observable timing can improve efficiency, especially when players can coordinate, but may also increase the risk of low efficiency if players are uncooperative. The paper does not manipulate or analyze formal punishment costs or technologies, so its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled public goods games is limited, but it is informative about the role of timing and endogenous retaliation.

- source: Espín, A. M., Correa, M., & Ruiz-Villaverde, A. (2019). Patience predicts cooperative synergy: The roles of ingroup bias and reciprocity. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the mean patience of group members predicts higher group cooperative synergy in a problem-solving task with intergroup competition. Both short-term and long-term patience measures are positively associated with group efficiency. Patient individuals are more likely to make efficiency-maximizing and less likely to make competitive/spiteful choices in social preference tasks. The effect of patience on group synergy is not due to ingroup bias but is linked to reciprocal fairness. The design does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, but patient individuals are more likely to reward cooperative and punish uncooperative group members in allocation tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that individual differences in patience (delay discounting) are a strong moderator of group efficiency in intergroup competition settings structurally similar to repeated social dilemmas. While not a standard PGG or punishment experiment, the findings suggest that group composition (patience) can be as important as institutional design for predicting efficiency. The absence of explicit punishment/reward means the results are most relevant for baseline or control conditions, or for understanding individual-level moderators. The study does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment, but it does show that patient individuals are more likely to engage in reciprocal (reward/punish) behaviors in allocation tasks, which may inform expectations about punishment effectiveness in similar environments.

- source: Sabin, N., & Reed-Tsochas, F. (2020). Able but Unwilling to Enforce: Cooperative Dilemmas in Group Lending. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that group repayment (a direct measure of economic cooperation/efficiency) is highest in groups with moderate social cohesion and no disconnected subgroups. Both very high and very low cohesion are associated with worse repayment. Disconnected subgroups further reduce repayment, and the negative effect is mitigated by higher overall cohesion. The mechanisms are traced to a trade-off between ability and willingness to punish: high cohesion increases ability but reduces willingness to enforce, while disconnected subgroups increase willingness to punish out-group members but reduce ability to do so effectively. The net effect on efficiency is determined by the lesser of ability or willingness. These results are robust to controls for financial ability, group composition, and alternative outcome measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of informal punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like settings is highly sensitive to group social structure. In environments where punishment is informal and depends on social ties, both the ability and willingness to punish must be considered. Efficiency is maximized at moderate levels of cohesion; too much cohesion reduces willingness to punish, undermining enforcement. Disconnected subgroups are detrimental to efficiency, even if willingness to punish out-group members is high, because ability is low. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not monotonic in group connectedness and is context-dependent. The findings are most relevant for settings with informal, endogenous punishment and may not generalize to formal, externally imposed punishment mechanisms.

- source: Bernard, M., Fanning, J., & Yuksel, S. (2018). Finding cooperators: Sorting through repeated interaction. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that in the Baseline (replacement allowed), cooperation (measured by responder return) increases over time, surpassing the Fixed Partner treatment (no replacement) in later rounds, despite similar initial levels. The Contract Restrictions treatment (no replacement, no punishment) starts with lower cooperation but converges to the Fixed Partner level in later rounds. The ability to punish by reducing transfers does not significantly increase cooperation compared to the inability to punish. Instead, the ability to sort (replace uncooperative partners) is the key driver of higher long-run cooperation. Surplus sharing becomes more equal in long-term relationships, but overall efficiency is not maximized in any treatment. The findings suggest that sorting, not punishment, is the main mechanism for sustaining cooperation in this environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated gift-exchange games (adjacent to PGGs), the ability to punish (reduce transfers) does not significantly increase efficiency or cooperation compared to when punishment is not possible. Instead, the ability to sort (replace partners) is more important for sustaining cooperation and improving surplus. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment alone (without sorting/replacement) may not increase efficiency, and that the structure of partner matching is a critical moderator. The findings are most relevant for environments where partner selection or replacement is possible, and less so for standard PGGs with fixed groups and explicit costly punishment. The paper's results should be used to caution against overestimating the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games without sorting opportunities.

- source: Kas, J., Corten, R., & van de Rijt, A. (2020). Reputations in mixed-role markets: A theory and an experimental test. *Social Science Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that in a repeated dyadic trust/lending game, providing information about a participant's past behavior in both roles (mixed-role reputation) increases the rate at which trust is placed and honored in the Lending Game (free exchange, where lending is costly and not compensated), but not in the Trust Game (paid exchange, where lending is compensated). Specifically, in the Lending Game, the trust rate and the 'success rate' (trust placed and honored) are significantly higher under mixed-role reputation than under single-role reputation. In the Trust Game, there is no significant difference between reputation conditions. The paper interprets these results as evidence that mixed-role reputation systems facilitate reciprocal lending and increase overall cooperation and welfare in free exchange settings.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving public-goods-like environments where the main intervention is a change in the information structure (e.g., adding mixed-role reputation), this paper provides evidence that such interventions can increase efficiency-related outcomes (success rate) in free exchange settings, but not in paid exchange settings. The results are most relevant for environments where cooperation is costly and not directly compensated, and where reputation can be built across multiple roles. The findings suggest that enabling richer reputation signals (beyond single-role) can improve group outcomes in these contexts. However, the paper does not directly study classic public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, so the transfer to PGGs with punishment should be made with caution.

- source: Garrett, J. B., Livingston, J. A., Tayler, W. B., Cade, N. L., & McVay, S. E. (2019). Controls and Cooperation in Interactive and Non-Interactive Settings. *Contemporary Accounting Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a control (audit with bonus) in a repeated prisoner's dilemma increases subsequent cooperative behavior (measured as investment in a trust game) for both participants who directly experienced the control and those who only observed it, but the effect is significantly larger for those who directly benefited from cooperation. The effect is mediated by observed cooperation in the initial game. The results suggest that controls can foster trust and reciprocity, and that these effects are stronger in interactive settings. However, the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the main outcome is behavioral (investment/cooperation), not payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that control mechanisms (akin to monitoring or incentive-based controls) can increase cooperative behavior in subsequent social dilemmas, especially in interactive settings. However, because the main outcome is investment in a trust game (a behavioral measure) and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not involve explicit punishment or sanctions, but rather a positive incentive (bonus for high contribution). The results suggest that mechanisms increasing observed cooperation can have positive spillovers on future cooperation, but do not directly inform the efficiency impact of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Burum, B., Nowak, M. A., & Hoffman, M. (2020). An evolutionary explanation for ineffective altruism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people are generally insensitive to the efficacy (impact) of their charitable giving, in contrast to their sensitivity when making decisions for themselves or family. Social rewards (reputation, praise, monetary reward from third parties) are given for the act of giving, not for giving effectively or giving more. This insensitivity is not due to cognitive or emotional limitations, but rather to the evolutionary logic that social rewards are easier to coordinate around observable, well-defined actions (like giving) than around less observable or harder-to-define features (like efficacy or amount). The studies do not use public goods games or punishment, but the reward mechanism is conceptually adjacent to sanctioning. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers indirect evidence that social incentives (rewards) are most effective when they are based on observable, well-defined actions, and that people are not motivated by efficiency or impact when giving is socially rewarded. The findings suggest that, in environments where social rewards or punishments are based on easily observed behaviors (like contributing at all), these incentives may not improve efficiency if they do not target effective or high-impact contributions. The lack of sensitivity to efficacy in social rewards may limit the effectiveness of punishment or reward mechanisms in increasing group efficiency, unless the mechanisms are designed to be sensitive to impact. The paper is most relevant for understanding the limitations of social incentives in promoting efficient cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Wang, X. L., Han, J. H., Li, F. H., & Cao, B. H. (2018). Both Rewards and Moral Praise Can Increase the Prosocial Decisions: Revealed in a Modified Ultimatum Game Task. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that participants are less likely to reject unfair/self-benefiting offers (i.e., more likely to act selfishly) when doing so would result in a material loss (risk condition). When economic or moral rewards are provided for norm compliance, participants are more likely to reject unfair offers and act prosocially. The effect is stronger for economic rewards than for moral rewards, and is most pronounced for extremely unfair divisions. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, focusing instead on behavioral compliance with fairness norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. While it does not use a public goods game or analyze efficiency/payoff outcomes, it shows that the threat of material loss for norm compliance reduces prosocial behavior, and that rewards (especially economic) can restore norm compliance. The findings suggest that in environments where norm compliance is costly, punishment alone may not sustain cooperation, but rewards can help. However, because the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency under punishment is limited.

- source: Wu, J. H., Balliet, D., Kou, Y., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2019). Gossip in the Dictator and Ultimatum Games: Its Immediate and Downstream Consequences for Cooperation. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the possibility of gossip (reputation transmission) increases generosity in both dictator and ultimatum games. However, strategic motivation (fear of rejection in the ultimatum game) does not significantly increase generosity compared to the dictator game. In subsequent trust games, those who previously played the ultimatum game are less likely to reciprocate trust, especially when gossip is present. The effects are measured in terms of individual behavioral outcomes (generosity, trust, reciprocity), not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that reputation systems (gossip) can promote cooperation, but strategic motivations may undermine future cooperation, particularly when reputation is at stake.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers adjacent evidence that reputation mechanisms (gossip) can increase cooperative behavior in dyadic allocation games, and that strategic motivations (as in the ultimatum game) may not robustly increase cooperation and can even reduce reciprocity in subsequent interactions. Since the design lacks group-level interaction, explicit punishment, and efficiency outcomes, its direct predictive value for the target task is limited. It may inform the prediction task by analogy: reputation-based incentives can substitute for punishment in promoting cooperation, but strategic extrinsic incentives may have negative downstream effects on intrinsic cooperation.

- source: Samu, F., & Takács, K. (2021). Evaluating mechanisms that could support credible reputations and cooperation: cross-checking and social bonding. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment found that allowing for cross-checking of gossip or opportunities for social bonding did not significantly improve cooperation rates compared to a control. High competition for reputation increased cooperation, but overall cooperation declined over time in all conditions. Gossip was generally honest but became more negative about rivals under high competition. Reputation scores and gossip valence influenced conditional cooperation, but the mechanisms tested did not create a reliable path to sustained high cooperation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of communication, reputation, and competition on cooperation in repeated dyadic games, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not include explicit costly punishment or group-level payoff analysis. Findings suggest that mechanisms to improve the credibility of social information (cross-checking, social bonding) may not be sufficient to sustain cooperation, and that competition for reputation can increase cooperation rates. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper offers only contextual or indirect guidance.

- source: Vandermeer, J., Hosey, C., Epley, N., & Keysar, B. (2019). Escalation of negative social exchange: Reflexive punishment or deliberative deterrence?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that negative escalation in repeated taking/giving games is driven by reflexive punishment rather than strategic deterrence. Deliberation reduces escalation, while reflexive or high cognitive load conditions increase it. Escalation occurs only in response to perceived negative actions (taking), not positive ones (giving). Explicitly instructing participants to focus on future consequences or to deter does not increase escalation; only a goal to punish does. The findings are robust across one-shot and repeated interactions, and across different manipulations of deliberation and cognitive resources. However, the study does not use public goods games, does not implement a punishment stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is a repeated dictator/taking game, not a public goods game, and the main outcomes are escalation and behavioral responses to harm, not group efficiency or payoff. There is no punishment stage where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs, and no analysis of group welfare or efficiency. The findings may be relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punitive escalation, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Batsaikhan, M., & Putterman, L. (2019). An Honest Day's Pay: Cooperation among Entrepreneurs vs. Students, and Linkages to Real-World Business Success. *Southern Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Entrepreneurs in Mongolia display higher and increasing cooperation in a repeated property rights dilemma game compared to students, as measured by more tokens allocated to production and collective protection and fewer to theft. The opportunity to vote for mandatory collective protection (akin to a tax) is more frequently chosen by entrepreneurs, but this does not substantially change their already high cooperation levels. The chat treatment (pre-scripted messages) is associated with higher collective protection among entrepreneurs. There is no formal punishment mechanism (costly sanctions) as in standard PGGs; theft can be used as retaliation, but this is not a direct analog. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but discusses how behavioral allocations relate to social optimum. More successful entrepreneurs in real life are more cooperative in the lab. The main outcomes are behavioral, not payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games or close variants. The experimental design lacks a formal costly punishment mechanism; instead, it features theft (which is a form of negative externality, not a sanction) and collective protection (akin to public good provision). While the paper discusses how cooperation (less theft, more production, more collective protection) would increase efficiency, it does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, nor does it manipulate punishment in the relevant sense. Thus, the paper is not directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, though it provides contextual evidence about cooperation, institutional choice, and behavioral trends in repeated social dilemmas.

- source: Baik, K. H., Chowdhury, S. M., & Ramalingam, A. (2021). Group size and matching protocol in contests. *Canadian Journal of Economics-revue Canadienne D Economique*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in repeated Tullock contests, partner matching (fixed pairs) leads to lower bids and less overbidding compared to random stranger matching, but only in two-player groups. In three-player groups, matching protocol has no significant effect on bids or overbidding. Bid dispersion is also lower in smaller groups. The results suggest that identifiability and repeated interaction facilitate tacit collusion in small groups, reducing wasteful overbidding. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the findings are limited to behavioral measures (bids and overbidding) rather than direct efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency in contest-like environments, especially regarding the effects of group size and repeated interaction (partner matching). While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the finding that partner matching reduces overbidding in two-player groups suggests that efficiency losses from overbidding may be mitigated in such settings. For prediction tasks, the results imply that small group size and repeated interaction can reduce wasteful resource expenditure (overbidding), potentially increasing efficiency, but this is inferred from bid behavior rather than direct efficiency measurement. The absence of punishment or public goods framing limits direct transfer to PGG-like environments.

- source: Müller-Leinss, J. M., Enzi, B., Flasbeck, V., & Brüne, M. (2018). Retaliation or selfishness? An rTMS investigation of the role of the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex in prosocial motives. *Social Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: Inhibiting the right DLPFC via rTMS increases both retaliation against previously unfair opponents and selfish behavior (less fairness) towards previously fair opponents in a Dictator Game following an Ultimatum Game. The manipulation did not affect rejection rates in the UG. The findings suggest the right DLPFC is involved in controlling selfish motives, but the design does not distinguish between retaliation and pure selfishness. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and is not a public goods game or direct punishment paradigm.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent: it uses sequential UG and DG with role reversal and rTMS manipulation, focusing on individual-level retaliation/selfishness rather than group-level cooperation or efficiency. There is no manipulation of punishment institutions, costs, or group payoff structure as in PGGs. The main outcomes are behavioral (fair/unfair offers), not efficiency or payoff. Thus, the paper is not directly informative for the prediction task, except as context for the neural basis of retaliation/selfishness in economic games.

- source: Townsend, C., Aktipis, A., Balliet, D., & Cronk, L. (2020). Generosity among the Ik of Uganda. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that Ik participants in unframed Dictator Games give on average 23.3% of their endowment, which is within the expected range for similar societies. Framing the game to invoke both need and supernatural punishment significantly increases giving (to 43.2%), while either frame alone does not have a significant effect. The results challenge the claim that the Ik are uniquely selfish and show that cultural norms and beliefs can increase generosity. However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not include punishment as a costly sanctioning option, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is a Dictator Game, not a PGG or its variant, and the 'punishment' manipulation is a framing invoking supernatural punishment, not a real, costly, player-administered sanction. The outcome is individual giving, not group efficiency. The findings may be weakly informative for understanding how cultural beliefs about punishment can increase generosity, but they do not provide evidence about the efficiency effects of actual punishment mechanisms in PGG-like environments.

- source: Cagala, T., Glogowsky, U., Grimm, V., Rincke, J., & Tuset-Cueva, A. (2019). Rent extraction and prosocial behavior. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when an administrator actively chooses to extract a 10% rent from contributions, average contributions to non-profit associations drop significantly (by 41%) compared to a control where the same share is assigned passively. This effect is robust to framing and is interpreted as a motivational effect: rent extraction undermines contributors' general willingness to give, rather than triggering targeted negative reciprocity. The effect persists even when contributors interact with a new, uninvolved administrator in a subsequent round, indicating a spillover on generosity. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the design is a donation game with some similarities to public goods games but is not a standard PGG.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that visible anti-social actions by an authority figure (rent extraction) can sharply reduce prosocial contributions in a donation context, even when the price of giving is held constant. However, because the outcome is contribution rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a donation game rather than a standard public goods game, the findings should be transferred to PGG efficiency predictions with caution. The result suggests that introducing a salient, self-serving authority or punishment-like mechanism that is perceived as unfair or anti-social may reduce cooperation and thus efficiency, but the direct quantitative effect on efficiency in a PGG is not measured here.

- source: Bigoni, M., Potters, J., & Spagnolo, G. (2019). Frequency of interaction, communication and collusion: an experiment. *Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that, without communication, cooperation (collusion) rates are low and not significantly affected by the frequency of interaction (flexibility). When pre-play communication is allowed, cooperation rates increase dramatically across all treatments, and the non-monotonic effect of flexibility predicted by theory (higher cooperation at intermediate flexibility) becomes more apparent, but is still secondary to the effect of communication. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and there is no explicit punishment mechanism; instead, punishment is implemented via Nash reversion (endogenous). The findings suggest that communication is a much stronger facilitator of cooperation than flexibility in this setting with imperfect monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that communication (chat) has a very strong positive effect on cooperation rates in a repeated game with imperfect monitoring, even in the absence of explicit punishment. However, because the game is a Cournot duopoly (not a public goods game), there is no explicit punishment or reward mechanism, and efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The main implication is that enabling communication can greatly increase cooperation, but the effect of flexibility (frequency of interaction) is non-monotonic and only emerges when communication is present. The results do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but suggest that communication is a key moderator.

- source: Bengart, P., Gruendler, T., & Vogt, B. (2021). Acute tryptophan depletion in healthy subjects increases preferences for negative reciprocity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that lowering serotonin via ATD increases negative reciprocity in the Hawk-Dove game: participants in the ATD group are more likely to choose Hawk in response to an expected Hawk, compared to placebo. This effect is specific to negative reciprocity and does not extend to positive reciprocity or beliefs about the opponent's action. The incentive for mutual cooperation (payoff for mutual Dove) reduces Hawk choices. Risk attitude and mood are unaffected by ATD and do not predict strategy choice. The study does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that biological factors (serotonin availability) can modulate negative reciprocity in a competitive, dyadic game, but it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms or sanctions in public goods or similar games. The Hawk-Dove game is structurally adjacent to public goods and punishment games, but lacks explicit punishment or sanctioning stages, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, the findings are only weakly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The main relevance is in showing that negative reciprocity (costly, non-payoff-maximizing responses to perceived unkindness) can be increased by serotonin depletion, but the translation to efficiency or group payoff in multi-player, repeated, or sanction-enabled games is limited.

- source: Wang, X. J., Chen, Z. S., Krumhuber, E. G., & Chen, H. (2021). Money and flexible generosity. *British Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across three studies, the authors find that using money as the currency in DG/UG games reduces generosity compared to candy or food, but only when there are no sanctions (DG) or reputation concerns (private condition). When punishment is possible (UG) or when allocations are public (reputation at stake), the negative effect of money on generosity is reduced or eliminated. Imbuing money with prosocial meaning (charity) also attenuates the effect. The main outcome is generosity rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the presence of sanctions or reputation mechanisms can offset the self-serving effects of money on prosocial behavior, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of punishment or reputation mechanisms can mitigate the negative effect of monetary incentives on prosocial behavior, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game structure. The results are most relevant for predicting how punishment or reputation might moderate the effect of monetary incentives on generosity or cooperation, but less directly informative for predicting efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that punishment/reputation mechanisms may help maintain prosocial behavior even in monetary contexts, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits direct applicability to the prediction task.
