- source: Azevedo, R. T., Panasiti, M. S., Maglio, R., & Aglioti, S. M. (2018). Perceived warmth and competence of others shape voluntary deceptive behaviour in a morally relevant setting. *British Journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the perceived warmth and competence of the target modulate the likelihood of lying for self-gain: participants lie less to high-warmth individuals and more to high-competence individuals. The risk of disclosure (reputation risk) reduces lying, particularly toward low-warmth/low-competence targets. These effects are interpreted as reflecting malleable personal moral standards and social evaluation processes. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the game is not a public goods game or direct variant. The outcomes are behavioral (lying frequency) rather than payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how social context, reputation risk, and target characteristics can modulate dishonest behavior in a zero-sum, two-player setting. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The manipulation of reputation risk is conceptually adjacent to punishment/sanction mechanisms, but there is no explicit punishment, and the outcome is not efficiency. Thus, the paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it may inform understanding of how social evaluation and disclosure risk affect norm-violating behavior.

- source: Cruz, T. D. D., Beersma, B., Dijkstra, M. T. M., & Bechtoldt, M. N. (2019). The Bright and Dark Side of Gossip for Cooperation in Groups. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The studies find that (1) people are more likely to gossip to protect group members from norm violators, but also gossip to vent emotions; (2) being the target of true negative gossip increases short-term intended work effort, but being the target of gossip (true or false) reduces long-term cooperative intentions toward the gossipers; (3) false gossip does not increase short-term cooperation. The results highlight both a 'bright side' (short-term compliance) and a 'dark side' (long-term alienation) of gossip as a social sanction. However, no actual payoff, group earnings, or efficiency measures are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It does, however, suggest that informal social sanctions like gossip can increase short-term compliance (analogous to increased contribution) but may undermine long-term cooperation (analogous to group cohesion or repeated cooperation). The findings are relevant for understanding the psychological and motivational effects of informal sanctions, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, punishment cost) are not systematically manipulated or reported in a way that supports prediction for the target task.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2020). The role of third parties in norm enforcement in customary courts among the Enga of Papua New Guinea. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study documents that in Enga customary courts, norm violations are addressed primarily through restorative justice: compensation to victims, reintegration of wrongdoers, and broad third-party participation in settlements. Punitive sanctions (punishment) are rare and not institutionally emphasized. Third parties contribute to compensation both during and outside court hearings, motivated by kinship, reputation, and community harmony. Repeated offenders and free riders receive less support over time. The system is designed to maintain cooperation and social cohesion, not to maximize punishment or deterrence. The paper argues that such restorative systems are more effective for sustaining cooperation in small-scale societies than third-party punishment, but does not provide quantitative efficiency or payoff data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides contextual and ethnographic evidence that, in small-scale societies like the Enga, restorative justice and third-party compensation are preferred over punitive sanctions for norm enforcement. While it does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that punishment is not the primary or most effective means of sustaining cooperation in such environments. For prediction tasks involving public goods games or efficiency outcomes, this paper is mainly informative for understanding the limits of punishment's effectiveness and the importance of alternative, restorative mechanisms. It does not provide direct evidence for how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games or similar experimental settings.

- source: Oria, P. A., Wijnands, M., Alaii, J., & Leeuwis, C. (2018). Options for sustaining solar-powered mosquito trapping systems on Rusinga Island, Western Kenya: a social dilemma analysis. *Bmc Public Health*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that community members prefer individual responsibility for maintaining the mosquito trapping systems, with selective support for small, self-selected group savings (not large-scale collective action). Distrust in leaders, fear of misappropriation, and lack of confidence in group sanctions are major barriers to collective solutions. Some success is seen in small, trusted women's savings groups that can impose sanctions (e.g., expelling non-compliant members), but large governing bodies or compulsory group contributions are widely distrusted. The study highlights the importance of trust, group size, and leadership in sustaining public goods, but does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides qualitative, contextual evidence about the challenges of sustaining public goods in real-world settings, especially regarding trust, group size, and sanctioning. It suggests that effective punishment or sanctioning is difficult without strong trust and group identity, and that collective action may fail in large, heterogeneous groups. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental data on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions of treatment efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of social context and group structure as moderators of collective action and sanctioning effectiveness.

- source: Pisor, A. C., Gervais, M. M., Purzycki, B. G., & Ross, C. T. (2020). Preferences and constraints: the value of economic games for studying human behaviour. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that economic games can be strategically designed to reveal either private-world or real-world preferences, depending on which constraints are relaxed (e.g., resource constraints, anonymity, framing). In the Colombian case study, allocation and punishment behaviors in the games map onto real-world social variables (e.g., kinship, ethnicity, need), but also reveal preferences that are masked in observational data due to real-world constraints. Costly punishment (costly reduction) is more likely to be directed at wealthy or high-status individuals, and less likely at marginalized individuals, but the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff. The main contribution is methodological: showing how game design choices affect what is measured and how results relate to real-world behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it discusses costly punishment (costly reduction) games, the outcomes are about who is punished and why, not about group payoff or efficiency. The findings are useful for understanding how punishment behavior is shaped by social context and game design, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for methodological considerations and for understanding the importance of matching game design to research questions and local context.

- source: Eisenbarth, S., Graham, L., & Rigterink, A. S. (2021). Can Reminders of Rules Induce Compliance? Experimental Evidence from a Common Pool Resource Setting. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The SMS reminder intervention increased self-reported knowledge of forest use rules and the perceived probability of sanctions for rule-breaking, but did not increase actual scrutiny or sanctioning of others. There was no significant effect on compliance with forest use rules or on forest use (measured both by self-report and satellite data). The intervention did not systematically reduce resource extraction or improve compliance, and thus did not provide evidence that reminders of rules (and increased perceived sanctions) improve efficiency or group welfare in this common pool resource context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public goods or common pool resource games, as it does not manipulate or directly measure actual punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The intervention only increased perceived probability of sanctions, not actual sanctioning or compliance, and did not reduce resource use. Thus, the findings suggest that increasing perceived sanctions via reminders alone may not be sufficient to improve efficiency or reduce overuse in common pool resource settings. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that behavioral nudges increasing perceived sanctions (without changing actual punishment mechanisms or costs) are unlikely to have strong effects on efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Eriksson, K., Simpson, B., & Vartanova, I. (2021). The Power of Tolerance vs. Unselfishness as a Cultural Determinant of Cooperation. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Societies that place a higher value on tolerance (as measured by survey responses) exhibit higher average cooperation in a continuous PD, stronger norms against non-cooperation in a CPR scenario, and metanorms favoring gossip over physical confrontation. Societal valuation of unselfishness does not predict these outcomes. The effect of tolerance is robust to controlling for individual-level values. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that societal-level values (especially tolerance) predict cross-societal variation in cooperation and related norms/metanorms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not address how game design dimensions or punishment affect efficiency.

- source: Ge, E. H., Chen, Y., Wu, J. J., & Mace, R. (2019). Large-scale cooperation driven by reputation, not fear of divine punishment. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that cooperation (measured as donations) is higher in small, stable communities and among those with more public religious practice, but not among those with stronger belief in divine punishment. When reputational concerns are removed (private dice game), neither community size nor religiosity predicts honesty. The results suggest that reputation, not fear of supernatural punishment, drives large-scale cooperation in these settings. No evidence is provided on the effects of punishment or sanctions, nor on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or group payoff/efficiency measures. Its findings are only indirectly relevant: they suggest that reputational mechanisms (public observability, community stability) can increase prosocial behavior, but do not address the impact of punishment or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task, except as very weak contextual support for the importance of reputation versus punishment.

- source: Handley, C., & Mathew, S. (2020). Human large-scale cooperation as a product of competition between cultural groups. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds a strong negative association between cultural differentiation (measured by Fst across 49 norms) and the prevalence of cooperative norms toward outgroup members. Geographic distance does not significantly predict cooperation once cultural differentiation is controlled for. The effect is strongest for norms related to raiding/warfare. The findings support the idea that group-level selection on cultural variation shapes the scale of cooperative norms, but the study does not involve economic games, payoff measurement, or experimental manipulation of punishment or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a PGG or variant, does not manipulate or measure punishment as a treatment, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding the cultural evolution of cooperation and the role of norm enforcement in real-world societies, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in controlled game-theoretic settings.

- source: Kamei, K., & Putterman, L. (2018). REPUTATION TRANSMISSION WITHOUT BENEFIT TO THE REPORTER: A BEHAVIORAL UNDERPINNING OF MARKETS IN EXPERIMENTAL FOCUS. *Economic Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a substantial fraction of cooperators are willing to incur a cost to report defectors, even when there is no direct material benefit. Reporting is much more common when it is free, but remains significant at positive costs. Reporting is overwhelmingly targeted at defectors by cooperators, with little reporting in other pairings. The frequency of cooperation is relatively stable across treatments (about 50-60%), and beliefs about others' cooperation and reporting are correlated with own behavior. The results are consistent with models of social preferences (inequity aversion) and with emotional motivations (anger, obligation). However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the motivational underpinnings of costly punishment-like behaviors (here, reporting) in social dilemma settings, but does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The design is adjacent to PGGs with punishment, as it studies a two-player prisoners' dilemma with a costly reporting option that can affect a partner's reputation and future interactions. The findings suggest that costly punishment/reporting is more likely when costs are low and when social/emotional motivations are present, but do not allow direct prediction of efficiency changes from enabling punishment in a PGG. The results may inform priors about the likelihood of costly punishment being used, but not about its effect on group efficiency.

- source: McDougal, M. K. (2018). Unequal and Unfair: Free Riding in One-Shot Interactions. *Sociological Inquiry*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Participants in a hypothetical group task scenario evaluated free riders negatively, regardless of gender. Contrary to hypotheses, female free riders were sometimes evaluated less harshly than male free riders, especially by male participants. There were no significant differences in behavioral responses (e.g., willingness to work with, grading) toward free riders based on gender. Some spillover effects were observed: when the free rider was female, the cooperative partner was evaluated more negatively. No actual group task, contribution, or payoff occurred; all outcomes are self-reported attitudes and intentions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a hypothetical scenario to study perceptions and intended responses to free riding, not actual behavior or payoffs. Therefore, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games. It may be relevant for understanding social-psychological responses to free riding, but not for payoff-based modeling.

- source: de Melo, C. M., Terada, K., & Santos, F. C. (2021). Emotion expressions shape human social norms and reputations. *Iscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that participants cooperate more with counterparts who have positive or unknown reputations, use generous strategies, and display cooperative emotion expressions. Reputation, strategy, and emotion all independently and interactively affect cooperation rates and how reputations are updated. Emotion expressions can help forgive defectors with good reputations or rehabilitate those with bad reputations if they behave generously and express cooperative emotions. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses an iterated prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and focuses on cooperation rates and reputation rather than efficiency or group payoff. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the sense of costly punishment; instead, the study manipulates emotion expressions and reputation. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games is limited. It may be useful for understanding how nonverbal cues and reputation affect cooperation, but not for payoff-based prediction tasks.

- source: Berger, J. (2021). Social Tipping Interventions Can Promote the Diffusion or Decay of Sustainable Consumption Norms in the Field. Evidence from a Quasi-Experimental Intervention Study. *Sustainability*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that providing regular normative feedback about the prevalence of sustainable behavior (use of mugs) can trigger rapid norm change in the field, either increasing or decreasing sustainable behavior depending on the initial prevalence. In one cafeteria with a high initial rate of sustainable choices, the intervention led to a sharp increase in sustainable behavior, consistent with a social tipping dynamic. In another with a low initial rate, the same intervention led to a sharp decline (a 'boomerang effect'). Control conditions (appeal only or no intervention) showed little or no persistent change. The study does not report on efficiency, payoffs, or group welfare, and the outcome is strictly behavioral (proportion of sustainable choices).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The intervention is not a PGG or close variant, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The outcome is behavioral (norm adoption), not payoff-based. However, the study is relevant for understanding how feedback and norm interventions can shift group behavior in social dilemma contexts, and it highlights the importance of initial conditions for intervention success. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers only indirect, contextual evidence about norm change dynamics, not about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment.

- source: Vonasch, A. J., Reynolds, T., Winegard, B. M., & Baumeister, R. F. (2018). Death Before Dishonor: Incurring Costs to Protect Moral Reputation. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that people value their moral reputation extremely highly, often above wealth, success, or even life itself. In hypothetical scenarios, large proportions of participants preferred jail, amputation, or death to acquiring a bad reputation. In lab experiments, many participants chose to endure pain or disgust (cold pressor, touching worms) rather than have information suggesting they were racist shared with others. The motivation to avoid reputation damage is strong and can drive costly behavior. However, the paper does not use public goods games, does not manipulate or measure punishment or sanctions in a game-theoretic sense, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, support the broader claim that reputation concerns can motivate costly prosocial or norm-compliant behavior, which is a mechanism often invoked in explaining why punishment can sustain cooperation. The findings may be useful for contextualizing why individuals might comply with norms or avoid free-riding, but they do not inform quantitative predictions about efficiency or the effects of specific game design dimensions. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant and should not be weighted heavily for estimating efficiency outcomes.

- source: Hart, E., Mellers, B. A., & Bicchieri, C. (2019). Bad luck or bad intentions: When do third parties reveal offenders' intentions to victims?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Third party observers are more likely to inform victims when harm is accidental than when it is intentional. Moral anger increases the likelihood of informing about intentional harm, while empathy increases the likelihood of informing in both cases. Observers are more likely to inform when they believe the victim's beliefs about the offender's intent are inaccurate, and when the information has consequences for future interactions (e.g., potential for punishment). However, the paper does not study or report on group efficiency, total payoff, or the effects of punishment mechanisms as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods game environments. The design is adjacent in that it uses economic games and discusses punishment and fairness, but the dependent variables are about information sharing, not payoffs or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Albergaria, M., & Saes, M. S. (2018). Measuring externalities in an information commons: the case of libraries. *Journal of Cleaner Production*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The study finds that in a library (information commons) setting, users subject to non-monetary sanctions (professors, employees) generate negative externalities for users subject to monetary sanctions (students): an increase in non-student borrowing is associated with a nearly one-to-one decrease in student borrowing. Peer effects are positive but small. The analysis is based on observational data and does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding behavioral externalities and the impact of different sanction types in common-pool resource settings, but do not directly inform about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are limited to behavioral externalities (borrowing counts) in a field setting with different sanction types. While it highlights that sanction type can affect user behavior and externalities, it does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the setting is only adjacent to public goods games. Thus, its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is limited and indirect.

- source: Jin, S. X., Balliet, D., Romano, A., Spadaro, G., van Lissa, C. J., Agostini, M., Bélanger, J. J., Gützkow, B., Kreienkamp, J., & Leander, N. P. (2021). Intergenerational conflicts of interest and prosocial behavior during the COVID-19 pandemic. *Personality and Individual Differences*.

  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that age is associated with perceived costs of the pandemic (older people perceive higher health risks, younger people perceive more daily life disruption and job insecurity), but age is not robustly associated with prosocial COVID-19 behaviors (such as social distancing or staying at home) or support for behavioral regulations (such as mandatory quarantine or vaccination). The study conceptualizes pandemic-related prosocial behaviors as a social dilemma but does not use a public goods game or direct analog, nor does it measure or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. No experimental manipulation of punishment or sanctions is present; support for behavioral regulations is measured as an attitude, not as an implemented or experienced sanctioning institution.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. While it discusses social dilemmas and support for behavioral regulations (as a proxy for second-order cooperation), it does not manipulate or implement punishment, nor does it measure efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. The findings are based on self-reported attitudes and behaviors in a real-world pandemic context, not on controlled game-theoretic experiments. Therefore, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs is limited and indirect at best.
