  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Incomplete political contracts (vote buying, promises) are common even with secret ballots and no monitoring, especially when interactions are repeated. Candidates spend significant resources to influence voters, and voters reciprocate, leading to frequent reversals of the election outcome compared to the rational baseline. These reversals almost always reduce group total payoff, i.e., efficiency. Redistribution from candidates to voters reduces within-group inequality but does not increase total welfare. Repetition amplifies these effects: candidates spend more, voters receive more, and outcome reversals are more frequent. The design does not include explicit punishment, but the costly transfers function as a form of strategic expenditure to influence behavior, with negative efficiency consequences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in election-like games with costly, non-enforceable transfers (vote buying, promises), the introduction of such mechanisms reduces group efficiency when they distort the outcome. The effect is more pronounced with repeated interactions. For prediction tasks, the presence of costly, voluntary transfer mechanisms (even without explicit punishment) is likely to reduce efficiency relative to a baseline with no such mechanisms, especially when repetition is present. The design is not a standard PGG, but the evidence is informative for adjacent settings where costly, strategic transfers are possible. The paper does not study punishment per se, but the efficiency logic is similar: costly, strategic expenditures to influence others can reduce group welfare if they distort optimal outcomes.

- source: Ibbotson, P., Hauert, C., & Walker, R. (2019). Effort Perception is Made More Accurate with More Effort and When Cooperating with Slackers. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that participants are generally accurate in perceiving their partner's effort in a cooperative task, with higher accuracy when the partner is a slacker and when the participant invests more effort. The experimental design involves a two-player online game where both invest effort to move balls into a bucket, with rewards split 50/50. The main outcome is the accuracy of effort perception, not efficiency or payoff. Theoretical modeling suggests that strategies based on effort perception can help avoid inefficient cooperation, but this is not empirically tested in terms of group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. The findings are relevant to understanding how effort perception may support cooperation, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or game design dimensions. The theoretical discussion of effort-prober strategies is adjacent to efficiency but not empirically linked to payoff outcomes.

- source: Kagel, J. H. (2018). Cooperation through communication: Teams and individuals in finitely repeated Prisoners' dilemma games. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: Allowing unrestricted communication between opponents in finitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games leads to a large increase in early-stage cooperation rates for both teams and individuals. With communication, stage-one cooperation rates rise from 62.2% to 92.9% for teams and from 57.4% to 97.9% for individuals. Communication content focuses on mutual benefit and fairness, with little explicit discussion of punishment. Teams are more likely than individuals to plan and execute defections near the end of the game, consistent with greater strategic reasoning. Retaliation for defection is observed but is not a formal punishment mechanism. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling communication (chat) between players in finitely repeated prisoner's dilemma games greatly increases cooperation rates, especially in early rounds, for both teams and individuals. However, it does not directly report or analyze efficiency or group payoff, and does not implement or manipulate punishment mechanisms. Thus, while the findings are highly informative about the effect of chat/communication on cooperation, they are not directly transferable to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The evidence is most useful for understanding the impact of chat and the behavioral context in which punishment might operate, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes with or without punishment.

- source: Nockur, L., & Pfattheicher, S. (2020). Intuitive decision-making promotes rewarding prosocial others independent of the personality trait Honesty-Humility. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that instructing participants to make intuitive decisions (as opposed to reflective or control) increases the amount of reward given to prosocial allocators in a third-party reward task. Honesty-Humility, a personality trait, is not significantly associated with reward behavior. The effect of intuition is robust to controls for other personality dimensions and social value orientation. The study does not examine group efficiency, total payoff, or public goods game settings, and does not include punishment or sanctioning options.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot third-party reward task based on a dictator game, not a repeated or group-based public goods game. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported. The findings may be weakly relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of rewarding prosocial behavior, but do not inform the effect of punishment or the efficiency consequences of institutional design in public goods games.

- source: Bradley, A., Lawrence, C., & Ferguson, E. (2018). Does observability affect prosociality?. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The meta-analysis finds that making prosocial actions observable increases prosocial behavior (r = 0.141), with stronger effects in repeated interactions, social dilemmas (including public goods games), and when decisions have consequences for the actor. The effect is also stronger in laboratory settings and with passive observers. However, the outcome is always a behavioral measure (e.g., contribution, donation, volunteering) or intention, not efficiency or group payoff. The analysis does not directly address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that observability (e.g., making contributions public) can increase prosocial behavior in public-goods-game-like environments, especially in repeated games and social dilemmas. However, it does not report on efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or the effect of punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, suggesting that increasing observability may increase contributions, which could in turn affect efficiency, but without direct evidence. It does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment, nor does it report the relevant game design dimensions in a way that would allow direct mapping to efficiency outcomes.

- source: Blair, G., Littman, R., & Paluck, E. L. (2019). Motivating the adoption of new community-minded behaviors: An empirical test in Nigeria. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study found that both a social norms intervention (film with role models) and a nudge intervention (mass text message) increased the number of people who reported corruption by text message in Nigerian communities. The mass text message had a much larger and more immediate effect than the film. The film shifted some perceptions of social norms (e.g., increased perceived prevalence of corruption and anger about corruption), but did not increase the perceived prevalence of reporting. There was no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and no punishment or sanction mechanism was present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental context is adjacent to public goods (encouraging costly community-minded behavior), but there is no group payoff, no efficiency measure, and no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The findings may be of limited conceptual interest for understanding the adoption of rare cooperative behaviors, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: van Leeuwen, B., Schram, A., & Offerman, T. (2020). COMPETITION FOR STATUS CREATES SUPERSTARS: AN EXPERIMENT ON PUBLIC GOOD PROVISION AND NETWORK FORMATION. *Journal of the European Economic Association*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that competition for status (via status rents for incoming links) is a powerful mechanism for achieving efficient public good provision in repeated network games. When status rents are high, groups tend to form stable star or superstar networks, with a single core player providing the public good at or above the efficient level, and periphery players free-riding. Efficiency (measured as group earnings or welfare relative to the efficient benchmark) is much higher with status rents, especially in larger groups. In contrast, without status rents, groups fail to coordinate on efficient outcomes, and efficiency is low. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or related environments. It is not suitable for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as punishment is not present in any treatment. However, it does provide high-quality evidence on how other mechanisms (status rents, endogenous network formation, group size) affect efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment, but may be useful for understanding alternative mechanisms that can increase efficiency in repeated public goods environments.

- source: Serdarevic, N., Stromland, E., & Tjotta, S. (2021). It pays to be nice: The benefits of cooperating in markets. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that enabling mutual partner choice in repeated prisoner's dilemma games leads to higher overall earnings (efficiency) compared to random matching. This effect is robust across three lab experiments with different group sizes (5 or 9) and information conditions (reminder of full vs. current history). Cooperators benefit most from partner choice, earning significantly more than free riders, who actually earn less under partner choice than under random matching. The effect of partner choice on efficiency is larger in groups of 9 than in groups of 5, but the difference is not statistically significant. Reminding subjects only of their current period's information (rather than full history) increases efficiency more. There is no punishment or explicit sanctioning; exclusion from play is endogenous to the partner choice process.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling endogenous partner choice (mutual consent matching) in repeated social dilemma games increases efficiency relative to random matching, especially for cooperative types. While not a public goods game per se, the repeated prisoner's dilemma with continuous contributions and endogenous exclusion is closely related. The findings suggest that, in the absence of explicit punishment, mechanisms that allow for partner selection and exclusion can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency. The results also indicate that group size and information about partner history can moderate the efficiency gains from partner choice, though the group size effect is not statistically significant. For prediction tasks, the presence of partner choice (vs. random matching) should be expected to increase efficiency, particularly for cooperators, even without punishment.

- source: Andreoni, J., Kuhn, M. A., & Samuelson, L. (2019). Building rational cooperation on their own: Learning to start small. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a twice-played prisoners' dilemma, allowing players to endogenously choose the allocation of stakes between rounds leads to higher joint payoffs, especially as players learn to 'start small' (allocate more to the second round). The payoff-maximizing allocation is similar whether chosen endogenously or exogenously, but endogenous choice yields even higher payoffs, likely due to signaling effects. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the design, and the results are not directly about public goods games but are adjacent in their relevance to cooperation and efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning is present. However, it is highly informative about how endogenous structuring of game parameters (here, stake allocation) can enhance efficiency and cooperation, especially via signaling. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly applicable, but it does suggest that endogenous institution formation can yield higher efficiency than exogenous imposition, a principle that may generalize to other game design features.

- source: Huber, J., Shubik, M., & Sunder, S. (2018). Financing of public goods through taxation in a general equilibrium economy: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that enforceable taxation—especially when set by democratic vote—sustains high efficiency and public good provision, while voluntary anonymous contributions (VACs) lead to rapid decline in both. Endogenous (voted) tax rates result in higher efficiency and private good production than fixed exogenous tax rates. When subjects can choose, they almost always vote for taxation over VACs. No punishment or sanctioning is present in any treatment. The results suggest that, in this dynamic general equilibrium setting, enforceable taxes are a robust and efficient solution to the public goods provision problem, while VACs are unreliable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in dynamic public good economies with and without enforceable taxation, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. The findings show that, in the absence of punishment, enforceable taxes (especially when set endogenously) yield much higher efficiency than voluntary contributions. The design is adjacent to standard PGGs: it is a dynamic, general equilibrium model with investment, depreciation, and a government actor, rather than a simple linear PGG. The results are directly relevant for predicting the effect of introducing enforceable taxation (not punishment) on efficiency, but do not inform about punishment effects. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper provides a strong control (no-punishment) baseline, but no evidence on the effect of adding punishment.

- source: Wang, Z., Jusup, M., Shi, L., Lee, J. H., Iwasa, Y., & Boccaletti, S. (2018). Exploiting a cognitive bias promotes cooperation in social dilemma experiments. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Introducing a decoy 'reward' option in a repeated prisoner's dilemma increases cooperation rates and improves the likelihood that cooperators achieve higher payoffs. The decoy is rarely chosen, but its presence shifts initial choices toward cooperation, and this effect is stable over time. In the control, higher cooperation is associated with lower payoffs, but in the decoy condition, this negative correlation disappears, suggesting that cooperation is no longer disadvantageous in terms of payoff. The effect is robust to gender and academic background. The study does not involve punishment or sanctions, but rather a prosocial decoy option.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not study punishment or public goods games directly, but it provides evidence that introducing a prosocial decoy option can increase cooperation and improve the payoff prospects for cooperators in repeated dyadic social dilemmas. For prediction tasks involving efficiency, the findings suggest that adding a third, prosocial but inferior option can shift behavior and payoffs, even if the option is rarely chosen. However, the results do not directly inform the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor do they address group public goods settings. The payoff analysis is relevant for understanding how behavioral interventions (like decoys) can affect efficiency, but the lack of punishment and the dyadic structure limit direct transfer to PGG-punishment environments.

- source: Sonntag, A., & Zizzo, D. J. (2019). Personal accountability and cooperation in teams. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing group members the option to acquire information about the true source of others' output (endogenous accountability) leads to the highest average effort and group output, even though almost no one actually chooses to acquire the information. High accountability (where the source is always revealed) leads to the lowest effort and the fastest decline in cooperation over time. Low accountability (no source information) results in intermediate effort. The results suggest that the mere possibility of being observed (the 'Damocles effect') can sustain higher effort, even if observation is rare. There is no explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the study does not report efficiency as a ratio, but group output is a direct function of effort.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or team production. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the effects of information/accountability structures on group output in a repeated team production context. Since there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism, the findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with punishment enabled. However, the results suggest that making accountability optional (even if rarely used) can sustain higher group output than either always-on or always-off accountability. For prediction tasks, this paper is most relevant as a control or comparison for environments without punishment, but with varying information about individual contributions.

- source: Huang, L. B., DeScioli, P., & Murad, Z. (2021). Pulling for the Team: Competition Between Political Partisans. *Evolutionary Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In deterministic contest games where participants can spend money to increase their team's chance of winning a prize, people exert significantly less effort when in teams (3-on-3 or best-of-3) than when competing individually (1-on-1), with about a 20% reduction in effort. This pattern holds both for arbitrarily assigned teams and for teams based on political partisanship, even under strong partisan cues and during a highly salient election period. The results support theories of shirking and free-riding in group settings and challenge the idea that group identity or partisanship alone is sufficient to mobilize greater costly effort. No evidence is provided about the effects of punishment, communication, or other mechanisms that might increase group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, group/team settings in contest-like environments lead to lower total effort (and thus lower group payoff/efficiency) compared to individual settings. The findings are adjacent to public goods games: the contest game is not a standard PGG, but the group incentive structure and the opportunity for free-riding are similar. The results suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is lower in group settings. However, the paper does not test punishment, so it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that group identity or partisanship alone is not sufficient to overcome shirking. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in the absence of punishment, group efficiency will be lower than in individual settings, and that adding group identity cues is unlikely to increase efficiency without additional mechanisms.

- source: Chang, C. C., Kristensen, N. P., Nghiem, T. P. L., Tan, C. L. Y., & Carrasco, L. R. (2021). Cooperating with the future through natural resources restoration. *Sustainability Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that all groups overexploited the resource during extraction, but some pools were rescued through restoration. The likelihood of sustaining the resource pool was lower when previous generations were unsustainable. Individuals extracted more and had higher final payoffs when inheriting from unsustainable previous generations. Restoration behavior was positively associated with feelings of guilt and anger, but not with cognitive style. Deliberate (vs. intuitive) thinkers extracted more and had higher payoffs. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public-goods-game-like environments. It is not informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or analyzed. The findings are only indirectly relevant, as they pertain to restoration and intergenerational reciprocity, not to punishment or its efficiency effects.

- source: Nockur, L., & Pfattheicher, S. (2021). The Beautiful Complexity of Human Prosociality: On the Interplay of Honesty-Humility, Intuition, and a Reward System. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reward system (the possibility of being rewarded by a third party) increases the likelihood and amount of giving in a dictator game. Honesty-Humility is also positively associated with giving. Intuitive decision making does not have a main effect, but among individuals low in Honesty-Humility, intuition reduces giving when no reward system is present. The reward system effect is robust across personality and decision-making style. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. It does provide evidence that a reward system (not punishment) increases prosocial behavior in a dictator game, but the outcome is individual giving, not group efficiency. The design is not a PGG or close variant, and there is no analysis of efficiency or related payoff outcomes. Thus, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is very limited.

- source: Whitham, M. M. (2018). Paying It Forward and Getting It Back: The Benefits of Shared Social Identity in Generalized Exchange. *Sociological Perspectives*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that group-based social identity (even at minimal levels) significantly increases the rate of giving in a pure-generalized exchange setting, compared to both category-based social identity and no social identity. Category-based social identity does not significantly increase giving compared to control. The experiment controls for sex, norms, and gratitude. The design does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. The findings suggest that fostering group-based social identity can increase prosocial behavior in generalized exchange, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. However, it does provide evidence that group-based social identity can increase cooperation (giving) in a generalized exchange context, which is adjacent to PGGs. For prediction tasks focused on punishment or efficiency, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding the role of social identity as a moderator of cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Yang, C. L., & Yue, C. S. J. (2019). Cooperation in an Assortative Matching Prisoners Dilemma Experiment with Pro-Social Dummies. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that assortative matching based on recent cooperation history can increase cooperation rates compared to random matching, but the effect is inconsistent across groups. Introducing pro-social dummies (always-cooperating computer players) into the assortative matching environment (WHc) leads to a substantial and robust increase in cooperation rates for all types of players, including those initially inclined to defect. The presence of dummies shifts the distribution of cooperation rates to higher values and reduces the prevalence of all-defection. Payoff is positively correlated with cooperation in the assortative matching treatments, but efficiency or group welfare is not directly measured or reported. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or similar games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the effects of assortative matching and exogenous pro-social agents on cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The study does show that structural features (like matching rules and initial population composition) can have large effects on cooperation rates, which may be conceptually relevant for understanding moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Ma, S. M., Zhang, B. Y., Cao, S. N., Liu, J. S., & Wang, W. X. (2021). Limited memory optimizes cooperation in social dilemma experiments. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that providing players with memory of their own and their partner's actions from previous rounds (memory length L) in a repeated, randomized-pair Prisoner's Dilemma leads to a non-monotonic effect on cooperation: cooperation rates are lowest with no memory (L=0), increase sharply with minimal memory (L=1), and peak at intermediate memory (L=2), then decline with longer memory (L=3,4). The behavioral mechanism is memory-based conditional cooperation, where most subjects cooperate if their partner's recent cooperation rate is high. Theoretical models (replicator dynamics and Markov processes) fit the observed cooperation rates well. No punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant for understanding how memory and information about past actions affect cooperation rates in repeated dyadic social dilemmas, but it does not address group payoff, efficiency, or the impact of punishment or reward mechanisms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that memory length (information about past actions) can have a non-monotonic effect on cooperation rates, but provides no evidence about efficiency or about punishment effects. It is not directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public-goods-like environments.

- source: Schitter, C., Fleiss, J., & Palan, S. (2019). To claim or not to claim: Anonymity, symmetric externalities and honesty. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that removing anonymity (making claims public to other group members) significantly reduces dishonest overclaiming, while adding symmetric externalities (where claims reduce the pool for others) does not significantly affect honesty. There is no evidence that the combination of both mechanisms has a stronger effect than removing anonymity alone. The presence of symmetric externalities does not increase honesty, possibly because fear of being exploited by others crowds out pro-social motives. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are robust to controls and alternative model specifications.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide evidence that increasing transparency (removing anonymity) can reduce dishonest behavior in environments where individuals can make unverifiable claims that affect others. The presence of symmetric externalities alone does not increase honesty. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency, this paper is only weakly relevant, as it does not manipulate or measure those variables.

- source: Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2021). Rule Following Mitigates Collaborative Cheating and Facilitates the Spreading of Honesty Within Groups. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that individuals who are more likely to follow arbitrary, costly rules are also more likely to behave honestly in situations where cheating is possible and profitable. In dyadic and four-person group die-rolling tasks, groups with more high rule-followers exhibit less collaborative cheating (i.e., lower rates of reporting doubles and lower average reported die-rolls). The presence of even a single high rule-follower in a group can reduce collaborative cheating among low rule-followers. However, the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or any formal enforcement mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes beyond the behavioral proxies for dishonesty.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related enforcement mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. Its findings are relevant to understanding the role of individual differences in rule-following and the dynamics of collaborative cheating in the absence of punishment, but it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Powers, S. T., Ekárt, A., & Lewis, P. R. (2018). Modelling enduring institutions: The complementarity of evolutionary and agent-based approaches. *Cognitive Systems Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants' choices in one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma games are influenced by information about the other player's action and by individual personality traits. The disjunction effect (greater cooperation under uncertainty) is present but weaker than in previous studies and is not explained by a general bias to cooperate. Higher potential payoffs (high variability matrices) reduce the disjunction effect, suggesting more optimal responding when incentives are larger. Reward Responsiveness predicts more optimal (defecting) choices when the optimal strategy is to defect and the other player's action is known. Agreeableness predicts more cooperative choices when the optimal strategy is to cooperate. No evidence is found for a general bias toward cooperation, and the results are similar whether the task is framed as a social dilemma or a neutral card game. The study does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not include punishment or sanctions, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on the influence of payoff variability and personality on cooperation/defection may be contextually informative for understanding individual decision-making in social dilemmas, but it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects or efficiency.

- source: Du, J., Thomas, M. G., Bårdsen, B. J., Mace, R., & Næss, M. W. (2019). Comparison of social complexity in two independent pastoralist societies. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both kinship and herding group membership predict gift-giving among pastoralists, with some cross-cultural differences: Saami herders in Norway emphasize reciprocity and group membership, while Tibetan herders in China emphasize kinship and giving to poorer herders. Gifts are more likely to be given within herding groups and to close kin, but the relative importance of these factors varies by site. There is no evidence of punishment, sanctions, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes. The association between gifts received and herd size (wealth) is negative in Tibetan sites (gifts go to poorer herders) and absent in Norwegian sites.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report group efficiency or payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs in that it involves allocation of resources in a social context, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task. It may provide some contextual insight into how cooperation is structured in real-world groups, but not in a way that informs the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Bluffstone, R., Dannenberg, A., Martinsson, P., Jha, P., & Bista, R. (2020). Cooperative behavior and common pool resources: Experimental evidence from community forest user groups in Nepal. *World Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that Martu hunters who share a higher proportion of their foraging income are more central in cooperative hunting networks, suggesting that prosocial generosity (pecuniary distancing) is rewarded with more cooperative opportunities. High producers do not keep or receive more for themselves, and the best hunters often keep less than others. The benefits of generosity are indirect, via increased access to cooperation, not direct material gain. No evidence is provided on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency outcomes. Its findings on prosocial signaling and cooperation may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation in the absence of formal enforcement, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency. The game structure is not a PGG or close variant, and there is no manipulation of punishment or efficiency.

- source: Ember, C. R., Skoggard, I., Ringen, E. J., & Farrer, M. (2018). Our better nature: Does resource stress predict beyond-household sharing?. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that beyond-household food and labor sharing is nearly universal in nonindustrial societies. Societies experiencing higher resource stress (e.g., famine, natural hazards, chronic scarcity) are more likely to have frequent and extensive sharing customs, especially seasonal sharing. However, increased resource stress does not predict broader social or geographic scope of sharing (i.e., with non-relatives or beyond the community); in some cases, sharing with non-relatives is less common under high resource stress. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not involve experimental manipulation or public goods game paradigms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not involve experimental games, punishment, or payoff-based outcomes. However, it provides contextual evidence that cooperation (in the form of sharing) is more frequent in environments with higher resource stress, suggesting that environmental risk can increase cooperative customs. This may be indirectly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation in environments with or without punishment, but does not inform the effect of punishment or game design dimensions on efficiency.

- source: Columbus, S., & Böhm, R. (2021). Norm shifts under the strategy method. *Judgment and Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in a continuous two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, participants are more cooperative under the simultaneous decision method than under the strategy method, especially among conditional cooperators with low expectations of others' cooperation. This is explained by a shift in the salient social norm: the simultaneous method foregrounds an egalitarian fairness norm (give half), while the strategy method foregrounds a reciprocity norm (match the other's behavior). Norm elicitation confirms that fairness is more normative under the simultaneous method, and reciprocity is more normative under the strategy method. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about how elicitation methods and norm salience can affect cooperation rates in two-player continuous social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, except as a caution that ancillary design features (like elicitation method) can shift cooperation via norm salience, which could interact with punishment in other designs.

- source: Romano, A., Sutter, M., Liu, J. H., Yamagishi, T., & Balliet, D. (2021). National parochialism is ubiquitous across 42 nations around the world. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that people across 42 nations cooperate more with ingroup (same-nation) partners than with outgroup or unidentified partners in a one-shot, continuous prisoner's dilemma. This national parochialism is highly consistent across nations and is not explained by cultural distance, institutional quality, or ecological factors. Observability (public vs private) increases overall cooperation but does not moderate parochialism. There is substantial cross-national variation in baseline cooperation rates (impersonal cooperation), which is associated with cultural and ecological factors. No punishment, sanctioning, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its design (continuous PD, no punishment, no repeated interaction) is adjacent to but not directly informative for the downstream prediction task. The only potentially relevant dimension is the effect of observability (public vs private) on cooperation, but this is not tied to efficiency or payoff, and there is no punishment treatment.

- source: Zhang, M., Wang, S. Y., Hu, X. T., & Alfaro-Bittner, K. (2021). Local and global information affect cooperation in networked Prisoner's dilemma games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that providing players with global information about their opponents' strategies, or allowing them to choose between local and global information, increases the frequency of cooperation compared to providing only local information. In the local information condition, cooperation declines steadily over time. In the global and discretionary conditions, some sessions show a reversal with increasing cooperation in later rounds. Players strongly prefer global information when given a choice. The experiment does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a networked Prisoner's Dilemma without punishment or reward. Its main relevance is indirect: it suggests that the type and scope of information available to players can influence cooperation rates, which may in turn affect efficiency in related games. However, since efficiency or payoff outcomes are not analyzed, and punishment is not present, this paper offers only weak, contextual support for the prediction task.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2019). Collective Action for War and for Peace: A Case Study among the Enga of Papua New Guinea. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that collective action for war is often easier to mobilize than for peace, due to emotional triggers, private benefits for some individuals (especially young men), and the contagiousness of anger and revenge. Peacemaking, by contrast, requires overcoming internal rifts, mistrust, and the lack of unifying emotions, but is facilitated by strong leadership, compensation payments, and the involvement of broader social networks (including women and affinal kin). The development of rules and institutions for peacemaking (e.g., compensation, mediation) reduced the costs and destructiveness of war, promoted social complexity, and shifted incentives toward peace. However, the breakdown of traditional norms (e.g., with the introduction of guns) led to more destructive wars and greater reluctance to participate. Sanctions against free riders in war are rare due to the importance of brotherhood, but in peacemaking, non-contributors can be gradually reintegrated. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff in a formal sense, but describes the social and material costs and benefits of war and peace.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It offers contextual and theoretical insights into how collective action, norm enforcement, and compensation (a form of sanction) operate in a real-world society, and how these mechanisms affect the costs and benefits of group action. However, it does not manipulate or measure game design dimensions such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff in a way that can be mapped to PGG outcomes. Its relevance is mainly in illustrating the complexity of sanctioning, the importance of leadership and social structure, and the potential for compensation to restore balance after conflict. It should be used as contextual background rather than as direct input for quantitative prediction.

- source: Alvard, M., & Daiy, K. (2021). A Naturalistic Study of Norm Conformity, Punishment, and the Veneration of the Dead at Texas A&M University, USA. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=close | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that the majority of individuals comply with the hat-removal norm at the Memorial Student Center, with compliance rates much higher than at control locations. Punishment (i.e., direct enforcement or reproach of violators) is relatively rare, occurring in a small minority of observed norm violations. Survey data indicate that most students internalize the norm as a matter of respect for the dead and tradition, and report strong negative emotions when imagining norm violations. While many claim willingness to enforce the norm, actual enforcement is infrequent. The study provides evidence that internalized norms can be maintained with little overt punishment, and that a motivated minority may suffice to sustain compliance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or similar economic games with or without punishment. It does, however, offer indirect support for the idea that in some real-world settings, norm compliance can be high even when punishment is rare, due to internalization and cultural transmission. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, as it does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or related economic outcomes, nor does it use a PGG or variant. Its findings may be contextually relevant for understanding the role of punishment in norm maintenance, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Aksoy, O. (2021). Does Herding Undermine the Trust Enhancing Effect of Reputation? An Empirical Investigation with Online-Auction Data. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: Using a large dataset of eBay auctions, the study shows that sellers with more positive reputations are more likely to sell their items and at higher prices, while negative reputations reduce these outcomes. Buyers exhibit herding behavior, being more likely to bid on items that already have bids, but this effect peaks and then declines as the number of bidders increases. Importantly, even when herding, buyers still consider seller reputation, and the effect of reputation on sales and price remains significant. There is no evidence that herding undermines the efficiency or trust-enhancing effect of reputation systems in this market context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that reputation mechanisms (a form of informal sanctioning) increase efficiency in online market environments by raising both the probability of sale and the price sellers can obtain. While not a public goods game or direct punishment setting, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of reputation-based sanctions on efficiency in environments where trust and cooperation are important. The results suggest that, even in the presence of herding (social influence), reputation systems continue to enhance efficiency. However, the market context differs from standard PGGs, so direct quantitative transfer should be cautious.

- source: Falk, A., Becker, A., Dohmen, T., Enke, B., Huffman, D., & Sunde, U. (2018). GLOBAL EVIDENCE ON ECONOMIC PREFERENCES. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds substantial heterogeneity in economic preferences across and within countries. Preferences such as patience, risk tolerance, altruism, reciprocity, and trust are systematically related to individual characteristics (age, gender, cognitive ability) and to country-level geographic and cultural variables. Patience is strongly correlated with economic development (GDP per capita), while negative reciprocity is correlated with the frequency of armed conflicts. Social preferences (altruism, positive reciprocity, trust) are associated with prosocial behaviors and charitable activities. However, the study does not involve public goods games, group efficiency, or experimental manipulation of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it measures negative reciprocity (willingness to punish unfairness) as a preference and relates it to macro-level outcomes (e.g., conflict), it does not analyze the effect of punishment opportunities on group payoff, efficiency, or cooperation in controlled game settings. Therefore, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except perhaps as very indirect context on the prevalence of punishment motives across societies.

- source: Peperkoorn, L. S., Becker, D. V., Balliet, D., Columbus, S., Molho, C., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2020). The prevalence of dyads in social life. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Across five studies (four surveys and one experience-sampling), dyads are consistently the most common group size reported for a variety of social activities in both the US and the Netherlands. The paper finds some small sex differences (women are sometimes more dyadic), and minor associations with age and relationship status, but the main result is the predominance of dyads. The discussion connects this to theories of reciprocity, coordination, and social exclusion, but does not empirically address cooperation, punishment, or efficiency in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not report on any economic game, nor does it analyze efficiency, payoff, or punishment. Its only indirect relevance is in highlighting that most real-world social interactions occur in dyads, which may inform the ecological validity of two-player game studies versus larger group games. However, it does not provide any data or analysis that would inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions or control efficiency.

- source: Jordan, J. J., & Kouchaki, M. (2021). Virtuous victims. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that people perceive victims of immorality as more moral and trustworthy than nonvictims, even when their behavior is identical. This effect is specific to moral traits and does not extend to nonmoral traits or to victims of accidents. The effect is explained by the Justice Restoration Hypothesis: seeing victims as virtuous motivates people to help them and punish perpetrators. The effect disappears when there are disincentives for justice-restorative action. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in economic games, but does show that perceived victim virtue increases willingness to punish and help in hypothetical contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. It does not manipulate or measure group payoff, efficiency, or actual economic outcomes. However, it provides adjacent evidence that willingness to punish is motivated by perceptions of victim virtue, and that social incentives can modulate this willingness. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be contextually relevant for understanding psychological motivations behind punishment and justice-restorative actions.

- source: Galeotti, F., Maggian, V., & Villeval, M. C. (2021). FRAUD DETERRENCE INSTITUTIONS REDUCE INTRINSIC HONESTY. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Exposure to centralized deterrence institutions (ticket inspections and sanctions) increases subsequent unethical behavior (dishonesty) in a different context, for both previous rule-breakers and compliers. The effect is robust to controls and is stronger with larger inspection teams, suggesting a possible normative or signaling mechanism. The study does not use a public goods game or report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are about behavioral spillovers of punishment institutions, not about their effect on efficiency or group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not use a PGG or variant, does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, and does not manipulate or report on any of the key PGG design dimensions. Its findings are about behavioral spillovers of deterrence institutions on individual honesty in unrelated contexts. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, though it may be of conceptual interest for understanding broader effects of punishment institutions.

- source: Neilands, P., Hassall, R., Derks, F., Bastos, A. P. M., & Taylor, A. H. (2020). Watching eyes do not stop dogs stealing food: evidence against a general risk-aversion hypothesis for the watching-eye effect. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Dogs are sensitive to owner commands and are slower to take forbidden food when told to 'leave' compared to 'go.' However, the presence of images of eyes does not further increase their latency to steal food compared to control images. This suggests that the watching-eye effect, robustly observed in humans, does not generalize to dogs, even though dogs are highly sensitive to human eye cues in other contexts. The findings argue against a general risk-aversion explanation for the watching-eye effect and support the idea that it is linked to human-specific reputation management. No efficiency, payoff, or group-level outcomes are measured or discussed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar economic games. The study is about animal behavior (dogs) in a food-stealing paradigm, not about human cooperation, punishment, or efficiency in economic games. While the discussion references punishment and reputation management in humans, these are not empirically tested in this study. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Mujcic, R., & Leibbrandt, A. (2018). Indirect Reciprocity and Prosocial Behaviour: Evidence from a Natural Field Experiment. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The study provides strong field evidence that upstream indirect reciprocity substantially increases prosocial behavior in one-shot interactions among strangers. Drivers who were helped by another driver were 32.1% likely to help a third party, compared to 14.6% in the baseline (a 119% increase). This effect is robust to gender, age, social class, presence of co-passengers, traffic congestion, and the physical distance (time elapsed) since being helped. The authors discuss that such increased prosociality could reduce waiting times and improve social welfare, especially during busy periods, but do not directly measure efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not study public goods games or punishment, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings on indirect reciprocity and prosocial behavior are adjacent to the prediction task, as they show that certain social mechanisms (here, being helped) can increase prosocial actions in one-shot field settings. However, the design, context, and outcome measures are not directly transferable to public goods games with or without punishment. The results may be useful for understanding the behavioral drivers of cooperation, but do not provide quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Kardas, M., Shaw, A., & Caruso, E. M. (2018). How to Give Away Your Cake and Eat It Too: Relinquishing Control Prompts Reciprocal Generosity. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that when one person abdicates a resource allocation decision to another, the recipient is more likely to respond generously (give the better resource to the abdicator) than in a baseline where no abdication occurs. This effect is robust across hypothetical and real-stakes settings, and is mediated in part by perceptions of the abdicator's generosity. The studies are all dyadic, with two individuals and a binary allocation (better/worse resource), and do not involve repeated interaction, group cooperation, or any form of punishment or sanction. No efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes are measured or discussed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design, outcomes, and mechanisms studied (abdication, reciprocity, perceived generosity) are not related to punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes in group cooperation games. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods game-like settings.

- source: Sutter, M., Angerer, S., Glätzle-Rützler, D., & Lergetporer, P. (2018). Language group differences in time preferences: Evidence from primary school children in a bilingual city. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that German-speaking children are significantly more patient (more likely to delay gratification) than Italian-speaking children, even after controlling for age, IQ, risk attitudes, and family background. This difference is robust across different elicitation methods and is also reflected in self-reported saving behavior. Children from bilingual households show intermediate levels of patience. The study does not involve public goods games, punishment, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not study group cooperation, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on time preferences and cultural/language group differences in patience are not transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in public goods games.
